Linearized Analysis of Adiabatic Oscillations of Rotating Gaseous Stars
Tetu Makino

Abstract
We study adiabatic oscillations of rotating self-gravitating gaseous stars in mathematically rigorous manner. The internal motion of the star is supposed to be governed by the Euler-Poisson equations with rotation of constant angular velocity under the equation of state of the ideal gas. The motion is supposed to be adiabatic, but not to be barotropic in general. This causes a free boundary problem to gas-vacuum interface. Existence of solutions to the linearized equation in the Lagrangian coordinates of the perturbations around a fixed stationary solution, the eigenvalue problem with concept of quadratic pencil of operators, and the stability problem with a new concept of stability introduced in this article are discussed. Key Words and Phrases. Gaseous star, adiabatic oscillation, Euler-Poisson equations, Hille-Yoshida theory, quadratic pencil, stability 2020 Mathematical Subject Classification Numbers. 3610, 35Q65, 35P05, 35R35, 35B35, 47N20, 76N15, 76U05, 85A30.



1
Introduction

We consider the motion of a rotating gaseous star governed by the Euler-Poisson equation:

\displaystyle\frac{D\rho}{Dt}+\rho\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}=0,

(1.1a)

\displaystyle\rho\Big[\frac{D\bm{v}}{Dt}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})\Big]+{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P+\rho{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}\Phi=0,

(1.1b)

\displaystyle\rho\frac{DS}{Dt}=0,

(1.1c)

\displaystyle\triangle\Phi=4\pi\mathsf{G}\rho

(1.1d)

on
(t,\bm{x})=(t,x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})\in[0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
We are denoting

\nabla_{\bm{x}}=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}\\
\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}}\\
\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\end{bmatrix},\quad\frac{D}{Dt}=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+\sum_{k=1}^{3}v^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}},\quad\triangle=\sum_{k=1}^{3}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}\Big)^{2}.

(1.2)

The unknown variables
\rho,P,S,\bm{v}=(v^{1},v^{2},v^{3})^{\top}
are density, the pressure, the specific entropy, and
the velocity field, while

\bm{\Omega}=\Omega\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}},

(1.3)

\Omega
being a constant, the angular velocity of the rotation around the
x^{3}
-axis.
Hereafer we denote

B=2\Omega\begin{bmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}

(1.4)

so that

B\bm{v}=2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}=2\Omega\begin{bmatrix}-v^{2}\\
v^{1}\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

(1.5)

On the other hand
\Phi
is the
gravitational potential and
\mathsf{G}
is a positive constant, while we suppose that the support of
\rho(t,\cdot)
is compact for
\forall t
and replace the Poisson equation (
1.1d
) by the Newton potential

\Phi(t,\mbox{\boldmath$x$})=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\rho(t,\cdot)](\bm{x}),

(1.6)

where

\mathcal{K}[f](\bm{x}):=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int\frac{f(\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\prime})}{\|\mbox{\boldmath$x$}-\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\prime}\|}d\mbox{\boldmath$x$}^{\prime}.

(1.7)

The pressure
P
is supposed to be a prescribed function
\mathscr{P}(\rho,S)
of
\rho,S
, where
\mathscr{P}\in C^{1}([0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R})
and

\displaystyle\mathscr{P}>0,\frac{\partial\mathscr{P}}{\partial\rho}>0
for
\rho>0
and
\mathscr{P}(0,S)=0
. So we read

P=\mathscr{P}(\rho,S)

(1.8)

in (
1.1b
).

We consider the initial data
\rho^{0},S^{0},\bm{v}^{0}
for which the motion enjoys

\rho(0,\bm{x})=\rho^{0}(\bm{x}),\quad S(0,\bm{x})=S^{0}(\bm{x}),\quad\bm{v}(0,\bm{x})=\bm{v}^{0}(\bm{x}).

(1.9)

We denote

P^{0}(\bm{x})=\mathscr{P}(\rho^{0}(\bm{x}),S^{0}(\bm{x})),\quad\Phi^{0}(\bm{x})=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\rho^{0}](\bm{x}).

(1.10)

We suppose that there is fixed an axially symmetric stationary solution

(\rho,S,\bm{v})=(\rho_{b}(\bm{x}),S_{b}(\bm{x}),\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x}))
, which satisfy (
1.1a
), (
1.1b
), (
1.1c
), (
1.6
),
such that
\mathfrak{R}_{b}=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}|\rho_{b}(\bm{x})>0\}
is a bounded domain.
The existence of stationary solutions is discussed in Section 2.

We consider small perturbations at this fixed stationary solution.
The linearized equation which governs the perturbations, which will be called “

ELASO
( the equation of linear adiabatic stellar oscillations )“,
turns out to be

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{u}}{\partial t^{2}}+B\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=0\quad\mbox{on}\quad[0,+\infty[\times\mathfrak{R}_{b},

(1.11)

where

\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}\mathfrak{d}P-\frac{\mathfrak{d}\rho}{\rho_{b}^{2}}{\nabla}P_{b}+{\nabla}\mathfrak{d}\Phi,

(1.12)

with

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\rho=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u}),

(1.13a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}S=-(\bm{u}|{\nabla}S_{b}),

(1.13b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}P=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}\mathfrak{d}\rho+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\mathfrak{d}S,

(1.13c)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\Phi=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\mathfrak{d}\rho].

(1.13d)

Here we are denoting

\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}=\partial_{\rho}\mathscr{P}(\rho_{b},S_{b}),\quad\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}=\partial_{S}\mathscr{P}(\rho_{b},S_{b}).

The unknown variable
\bm{u}
means

\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{\varphi}(t,\bm{x})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bm{x})+\bm{u}^{0}

(1.14)

where
\bm{u}^{0}
is a given vector field on
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
and
\bm{\varphi}(t,\bm{x})
is the solution of

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bm{\varphi}(t,{\bm{x}})=\bm{v}(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bm{x})),\quad\bm{\varphi}(0,\bm{x})=\bm{x},

(1.15)

while
\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bm{x})
is the solution of

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bm{x})),\quad\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0,\bm{x})=\bm{x}.

(1.16)

Here we suppose

Assumption 1

It holds that

\{\bm{x}\ |\ \rho^{0}(\bm{x})>0\}=\mathfrak{R}_{b}

(1.17)

and
\bm{u}^{0}
enjoys

\displaystyle\rho^{0}(\bm{x})-\rho_{b}(\bm{x})=-\mathrm{div}\Big(\rho_{b}(\bm{x})\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})\Big),

(1.18a)

\displaystyle S^{0}(\bm{x})-S_{b}(\bm{x})=-\Big(\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})\Big|{\nabla}S_{b}(\bm{x})\Big).

(1.18b)

The equation (
1.11
) is considered with the initial condition

\bm{u}|_{t=0}=\bm{u}^{0},\quad\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}=\bm{v}^{0}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{R}_{b}.

(1.19)

In Section 3 the derivation of this
ELASO
(
1.11
) is discribed
under the situation which enjoys the assumption

Assumption 2

\rho-\rho_{b},S-S_{b},\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{b}
are of
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
and moreover

\bm{v}_{b}
itself is of
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.

Here we use

Notation 1

Considering small
\varepsilon
, a quantity
Q
will denoted by
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
if
Q
and its derivatives are of order
O(\varepsilon)
uniformly on
t\in[0,T]
for
\forall
fixed
T\in[0,+\infty[
.

In Section 4 we give the basic existence result to the initial value problem
(
1.11
), (
1.19
), by realizing the integrodifferential operator
\mathcal{L}
as a
self-adjoint operator
\bm{L}
in the Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}_{b},\rho_{b}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}^{3})
. This is done by considring the first order system, which will be called ‘
ELASO ‡
’,

\frac{dU}{dt}+\bm{A}U=\bm{0},

(1.20)

where

U=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}\\
\\
\frac{d\bm{u}}{dt}\end{bmatrix},\quad\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}O&-I\\
\\
\bm{L}&\bm{B}\end{bmatrix},

(1.21)

which is equivalent to
ELASO
, and
applying the Hille-Yosida theory. The applied theorem is formulated and proved in
Appendix A
for the sake of selfcontainedness.

In Section 5 we discuss about the eigenvalue problem to the equation

ELASO
(
1.11
). The issue is addressed to the problem of possibility of eigenmode expansions associated with
ELASO
. The ‘eigenvalue’ of
ELASO
(
1.11
) is

\pm\sqrt{-\lambda}
for the
usual eigenvalue
\lambda
of
\bm{L}
only when
\Omega=0,B=O
, But, when
\Omega\not=0
, we need the concept ‘quadratic pencil’ (after Bognar
[
3
]
) and its ‘spectrum’. The result of the structure of the ‘spectrum’ of the quadratic pencil by J. Dyson and B. F. Schultz
[
7
]
is justified.

In Section 6 we discuss about the stability of solutions of
ELASO
(
1.11
)(
1.19
). We propose a new concept of stability based on seminorms on the space of the values
\bm{u}(t,\cdot)
of the solution at instant
t
, taking into account that the magnitude of
\bm{u}(t,\cdot)
itself is not essential but the magnitude of the perturbation
\mathfrak{d}\rho=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u}),\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}=\partial\bm{u}/\partial t-\bm{v}_{b}^{L}
is essential in the discussion of stability. Examples of the seminorms are presented
with an open question.

Traditionally astrophysicists used to
discuss a linearized equation of adiabatic stellar oscillations which is slightly different from
ELASO
(
1.11
). In Section 7 we introduce the equation, which will be called ‘
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
’, of astrophysists:

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}}{\partial t^{2}}+\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}=\bm{0}.

(1.22)

Here
\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge}
and
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}
are slightly different from
\bm{B}
and
\bm{L}
,
but
\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge}=\bm{B}
and
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}=\bm{L}
when
\bm{v}_{b}=\bm{0}
. The
unknown variable
\bm{\xi}
means

\bm{\xi}(t.\bm{x})=\bm{\varphi}(t\Big|0,\bm{\varphi}(t|0\|\bm{v}_{b})\Big\|\bm{v})-\bm{x}.

(1.23)

Here
\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y})\|\bm{V})
stands for the solution of

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}\|\bm{V})=\bm{V}(t,\bm{\varphi}(t|s,y\|\bm{V})),\quad\bm{\varphi}(s|s,\bm{y}\|\bm{V}))=\bm{y},

(1.24)

while
\bm{V}=\bm{v}
or
\bm{v}_{b}
. The derivation of
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)

is performed inder the situation which enjoys the assumption

Assumption 3

The initial configurations of
\rho,S
are the same to those of the background, that is,

\displaystyle\rho^{0}

\displaystyle=\rho(0,\bm{x})=\rho_{b}(\bm{x}),

(1.25a)

\displaystyle S^{0}

\displaystyle=S(0,\bm{x})=S_{b}(\bm{x}).

(1.25b)

This is stronger than Assumption
1
, which reduces this when
\bm{u}^{0}=\bm{0}
.
But we suppose, instead of Assumption
2
, the weaker

Assumption 4

\rho-\rho_{b},S-S_{b},\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{b}
are of
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
,

without supposing that
\bm{v}_{b}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.

Let us note the following relation between
ELASO
and

ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
:
The solution
\bm{\xi}
of
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
should satisfy

\bm{\xi}(0,\cdot)=\bm{0}
; Consider the solution
\bm{u}
of
ELASO

such that
\bm{u}^{0}=\bm{u}(0,\cdot)=\bm{0}
; Then we have the relation

\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{u}(t,\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b})),

(1.26)

since
the Lagrange displacement

\bm{u}
in
ELASO
(
1.11
) was defined by
(
1.14
), which reads

\bm{u}(t,{\bm{x}})=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,{\bm{x}}||\bm{v})-\bm{\varphi}(t|0,{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}_{b})+\bm{u}^{0}({\bm{x}});

Therefore
\bm{\xi}=\bm{u}
when
\bm{v}_{b}=\bm{0}
so that

\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b})=\bm{x}
, but
\bm{\xi}\not=\bm{u}
in general.

In Section 8 some properties of
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
are discussed in view of functional analysis, keeping in mind the analysis of
ELASO
in Section 4.

2
Existence of stationary solutions –Distorted Lane-Emden functions

In this section we establish the existence of stationary solutions which enjoy good properties used in the following consideration, supposing

Assumption 5

There are a function
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}(\upsilon,S)\in C^{\infty}([0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R})
and a number
\gamma
such that
1<\gamma<2
,

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}(\upsilon,S)>0
for
\upsilon\geq 0,|S|<\infty
,
\mathscr{F}+\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}\upsilon\frac{\partial\mathscr{F}}{\partial\upsilon}>0
for
\upsilon>0,|S|<\infty
,
\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\partial}{\partial\upsilon}\log\mathscr{F}\Big|\leq C
, for which

\mathscr{P}(\rho,S)=\rho^{\gamma}\mathscr{F}(\rho^{\gamma-1},S)\quad\mbox{for}\quad\rho\geq 0,|S|<\infty.

(2.1)

We keep in mind the case of
\mathscr{F}(\upsilon,S)=\exp(S/\mathsf{C}_{V})
,
\mathsf{C}_{V}
being a positive constant, namely, the case when the equation of state (
1.8
) turns out to be

P=\rho^{\gamma}\exp\Big(\frac{S}{\mathsf{C}_{V}}\Big).

(2.2)

Let us put the following

Definition 1

A triple of
t
-independent axially symmetric functions
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})\in C_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};[0,+\infty[)\times C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R})\times C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3})

which satisfies (
1.1a
)(
1.1b
)(
1.1c
) with
\Phi=\Phi_{b}
,
P=P_{b}
determined by (
1.6
) (
1.8
) with
\rho=\rho_{b},S=S_{b}

is called an
admissible stationary solution
, if

0) there is a function
\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)
of
(\varpi,z)\in[0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}
such that

\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})=\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\times\bm{x}=\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)\begin{bmatrix}-x^{2}\\
x^{1}\\
0\end{bmatrix},

(2.3)

where

\varpi=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}},\quad z=x^{3}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{x}=(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3}),

(2.4)

and the function
\bm{x}\mapsto\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)
belongs to

C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
;

1)
\rho_{b}(\bm{x}),S_{b}(\bm{x})
are functions of
(r,\zeta)
, and

\mathfrak{R}_{b}=\{\bm{x}|\rho_{b}(\bm{x})>0\}
is of the form
\{\bm{x}|r<R_{b}(\zeta)\}
, where

r=\|\bm{x}\|=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}+(x^{3})^{2}},\quad\zeta=\frac{x^{3}}{r}\qquad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{x}=(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3}),

(2.5)

and
R_{b}(\zeta)
is a continuous function of
\zeta\in[-1,1]
such that

R_{b}(\zeta)>0\ \forall\zeta\in[-1,1]
;

2) The boundary
\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b}
is of
C^{3,\alpha}
-class and the functions

\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1},S_{b},\Phi_{b}=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\rho_{b}]\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})\cap C^{3,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})
,
\alpha
being a positive number such that

\displaystyle 0<\alpha<\Big(\frac{1}{\gamma-1}-1\Big)\wedge 1\Big(:=\min\Big\{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}-1,1\Big\}\Big)
;

3)
\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho_{b}}{\partial r},\frac{\partial P_{b}}{\partial r}<0
in
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
and

\frac{\partial\rho_{b}}{\partial r}\leq-\frac{r}{C},\quad\frac{\partial P}{\partial r}\leq-\frac{r}{C}\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0<r\ll 1;

(2.6)

4) The boundary
\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b}
, on which
\rho_{b}=0
, is a physical vacuum boundary, that is,

0<\frac{\partial}{\partial\bm{n}}\sqrt{\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}}<+\infty\quad\mbox{on}\quad\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b},

(2.7)

where
\bm{n}
stands for the unit normal vector at the boundary point directed inward to
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
.
Here we note that
\partial P/\partial\rho
is the square of the sound speed.

Note that the condition 2)
requires
\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|,\|\nabla P_{b}\|\not=0
for
r\not=0
.

Definition 2

The admissible stationary solution
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
is said to be‘
barotropic
’, if

\omega_{b}
is independent of
z
, that is,

\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})=\omega_{b}(\varpi)\begin{bmatrix}-x^{2}\\
x^{1}\\
0\end{bmatrix},

and
there is a function
\Pi\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
such that

d\Pi(\upsilon)/\upsilon>0
and it holds

P_{b}(\bm{x})=\Pi(\rho_{b}(\bm{x})^{\gamma-1})\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}.

(2.8)

Note that, then,

(\nabla P_{b}|\nabla\rho_{b})=(\gamma-1)\upsilon\frac{d\Pi}{d\upsilon}\Big|_{\upsilon=\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1}}\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|^{2}>0\quad\mbox{for}\quad r\not=0.

(2.9)

Remark 1

It is well-known as the Poincaré-Wavre theorem (
[
22
, CHAPITRE II, 20, p.33]
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Omega_{b}}{\partial z}=0
if and only if

P_{b}=f(\rho_{b})
, where
\Omega_{b}(\varpi,z):=\Omega+\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)
.

For admissible stationary solutions, let us put

Definition 3

We denote

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle:=\frac{\rho}{P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}={\rho}\frac{\partial_{\rho}\mathscr{P}(\rho,S)}{\mathscr{P}(\rho,S)},

(2.10a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}

\displaystyle:=-\Big(\rho\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)^{-1}\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)\nabla S=-\Big(\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)\nabla S

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\nabla P+\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla\rho,

(2.10b)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{(\nabla P|\nabla\rho)}{\|\nabla\rho\|}-\frac{\|\nabla\rho\|}{\rho}

\displaystyle=-(\mathfrak{a}|\bm{n}),\quad\mbox{with}\quad\bm{n}:=\frac{\nabla\rho}{\|\nabla\rho\|}\quad\mbox{wherever}\quad\|\nabla\rho\|\not=0.

(2.10c)

and

\displaystyle\Gamma_{b}

\displaystyle:=\Big(\frac{\rho}{P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}={\rho_{b}}\frac{\partial_{\rho}\mathscr{P}(\rho_{b},S_{b})}{\mathscr{P}(\rho_{b},S_{b})},

(2.11a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{b}

\displaystyle:=-\Big(\rho\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}^{-1}\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\nabla S_{b}=-\Big(\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\nabla S_{b}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\Gamma_{b}P_{b}}\nabla P_{b}+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\rho_{b},

(2.11b)

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{b}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\Gamma_{b}P_{b}}\frac{(\nabla P_{b}|\nabla\rho_{b})}{\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|}-\frac{\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|}{\rho_{b}}

\displaystyle=-(\mathfrak{a}_{b}|\bm{n}),\quad\mbox{with}\quad\bm{n}:=\frac{\nabla\rho_{b}}{\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{R}_{b}\setminus\{O\}.

(2.11c)

Since

\Gamma=\gamma+(\gamma-1)\upsilon\frac{\partial}{\partial\upsilon}\log\mathscr{F},

(2.12)

we have
\Gamma_{b}\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})\cap C^{3,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})
. Since

\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}=\frac{\partial_{S}\log\mathscr{F}}{\gamma+(\gamma-1)\upsilon\frac{\partial}{\partial\upsilon}\log\mathscr{F}},

(2.13)

we see

\mathfrak{a}_{b}\in C^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})\cap C^{2,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})
. And
we see, at least,

\mathscr{A}_{b}\in\bigcap_{0<\delta\ll 1}C^{1,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b}\setminus\{r<\delta\})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}).

When
(\rho_{b},S_{b})
is equatorially symmetric moreover, we can claim
\mathscr{A}_{b}\in C(\mathfrak{R}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})

by evaluating
\mathscr{A}_{b}(O)=0
.

If the admissible stationary soltion is barotropic, then
we have

\mathfrak{a}_{b}=-\mathscr{A}_{b}\bm{n}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\mathfrak{R}_{b},

(2.14)

and
\mathscr{A}_{b}\in C^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R})
, since

\mathscr{A}_{b}=\frac{\gamma-1}{\Gamma P}\upsilon\frac{d\Pi}{d\upsilon}\Big|_{\upsilon=\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1}}\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|-\frac{\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|}{\rho_{b}}.

Remark 2

When the stationary solution is barotropic, this does not mean that the perturbed motion to be considered should be barotropic. The perturbed state
\rho=\rho_{b}+\mathfrak{d}\rho,P=P_{b}+\mathfrak{d}P
is approximately

\rho=\rho_{b}-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u}),\quad P=P_{b}-\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})+\Gamma_{b}P_{b}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a}_{b}),

and
{\nabla}\rho,{\nabla}P

are not necessarily parallel.

Remark 3

After
[
7
]
we can call
\mathfrak{a}_{b}
the “
vector Schwarzschild discriminens
’, and
\mathscr{A}_{b}
the “
generalized Schwarzschild discriminant
”. But, in spite of the definition
[
7
, (3.7)]
, we would like to define
the square of the “
generalized Brunt-Väisälä frequency (local buoyancy frequency)
”

\mathscr{N}^{2}
by

\mathscr{N}^{2}:=-\mathscr{A}_{b}\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}P_{b}\Big|\bm{n}\Big)=-\mathscr{A}_{b}\frac{({\nabla}P_{b}|{\nabla}\rho_{b})}{\rho_{b}\|{\nabla}\rho_{b}\|}.

(2.15)

Then, at
\|\bm{x}\|\not=0
,

\mathscr{N}_{b}(\bm{x})\in\mathbb{R}
if and only if

\mathscr{A}_{b}(\bm{x})\leq 0

, since
(\nabla P_{b}|\nabla\rho_{b})>0
. It seems curious that the Lagrangian displacemnt of perturbation
\bm{\xi}
is involved in the definition
[
7
, (3.7)]
.
Anyway, if
\Omega=\omega_{b}=0
and the background is spherically symmetric, then it turns out to be
\displaystyle\bm{n}=-\bm{e}_{r}=-\frac{\bm{x}}{\|\bm{x}\|}
and the definitions (
2.14
), (
2.15
) of
\mathscr{A}
,
\mathscr{N}^{2}
coincide with those
[
21
, (1.11), (1.12)]
. In
Appendix B
we derive the definition (
2.15
) of the Brunt-Väisälä frequency
\mathscr{N}
here by generalizing that found in meteorological texts, e.g.
[
5
, Chapter II, Section 21]
,
[
12
, Chapter 2, Section 2.7.3]
.

We are going to construct a barotropic admissible stationary solution.

Suppose

\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})=\omega_{b}(\varpi)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\times\bm{x}=\omega_{b}(\varpi)\begin{bmatrix}-x^{2}\\
x^{1}\\
0\end{bmatrix},

(2.16)

where
\omega_{b}\in C^{1}([0,+\infty[)
.

The equation (
1.1a
) is reduced to

(\bm{v}_{b}|{\nabla}\rho_{b})+\rho_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{v}_{b}=0.

(2.17)

But

(\bm{v}_{b}|{\nabla}\rho_{b})=\omega(\varpi)\Big(-x^{2}\frac{\partial\rho_{b}}{\partial x^{1}}+x^{1}\frac{\partial\rho_{b}}{\partial x^{2}}\Big)=\omega(\varpi)\frac{\partial\rho_{b}}{\partial\phi}=0,

if
\rho_{b}
is axially symmetric. Moreover we have
\mathrm{div}\bm{v}_{b}=0

for (
2.16
). Thus (
2.17
) holds for axially symmetric
\rho_{b}
.

The equation (
1.1c
) is reduced to

(\bm{v}_{b}|{\nabla}S_{b})=0.

(2.18)

This holds provided (
2.16
) and axial symmetricity of
\rho_{b}
.

Thus the equations are reduced to

(\bm{v}_{b}|{\nabla})\bm{v}_{b}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}P_{b}+{\nabla}\Phi_{b}=0

(2.19)

with

\Phi_{b}=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\rho_{b}],\quad P_{b}=\mathscr{P}(\rho_{b},S_{b}).

We claim

Theorem 1

Let a smooth function
\omega_{b}
on
[0,+\infty[
,
a smooth function
\Sigma
on
\mathbb{R}
and a positive number
\rho_{O}
be given.
Supoose that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\rho}\mathscr{P}(\rho,\Sigma(\rho^{\gamma-1}))=

\displaystyle=\upsilon\Big[\gamma+(\gamma-1)\Big(\upsilon\frac{\partial}{\partial\upsilon}+\upsilon\frac{d\Sigma}{d\upsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial S}\Big)\Big]\mathscr{F}>0\quad\mbox{for}\quad\upsilon=\rho^{\gamma-1}>0.

(2.20)

If
\displaystyle\frac{6}{5}<\gamma<2
, and

\frac{1}{\rho_{O}}\|\Omega+\omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}

is sufficiently small,
then there uniquely exists a
barotropic admissible stationary solution
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b}))
such that
S_{b}=\Sigma(\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1})

\rho_{b}(O)=\rho_{O}
, and
\bm{v}_{b}=\omega_{b}(\varpi)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\times\bm{x}
.

Proof . Consider the functions
f^{P},f^{\varUpsilon}
defined by

\displaystyle f^{P}(\rho)=f^{P}(\rho;\Sigma)

\displaystyle:=\mathscr{P}(\rho,\Sigma(\rho^{\gamma-1}))

\displaystyle=\rho^{\gamma}\mathscr{F}(\rho^{\gamma-1},\Sigma(\rho^{\gamma-1})),

(2.21)

\displaystyle f^{\varUpsilon}(\rho)=f^{\varUpsilon}(\rho;\Sigma)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{\rho}\frac{Df^{P}(\rho^{\prime})}{\rho^{\prime}}d\rho^{\prime}

(2.22)

for
\rho>0
. Thanks to the assumption (
2.20
) we have

Df^{P}(\rho)>0

for
\rho>0
, and there exists a smooth function
\Lambda
on
\mathbb{R}
such that

\Lambda(0)=0
and

f^{P}(\rho)=\mathsf{A}\rho^{\gamma}(1+\Lambda(\rho^{\gamma-1}))

(2.23)

for
\rho>0
. Here

\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}(\Sigma):=\mathscr{F}(0,\Sigma(0))

(2.24)

is a positive constant.
Then we have

\varUpsilon=f^{\varUpsilon}(\rho)=\frac{\gamma\mathsf{A}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma-1}(1+\Lambda_{1}(\rho^{\gamma-1}))

(2.25)

for
\rho>0
, where
\Lambda_{1}
is a smooth function on
\mathbb{R}
such that

\Lambda_{1}(0)=0
, and the inverse function
f^{\rho}
of
f^{\varUpsilon}

f^{\rho}(\varUpsilon)=\Big(\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma\mathsf{A}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(\varUpsilon\vee 0)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(1+\Lambda_{2}(\varUpsilon))

(2.26)

is given so that

\rho=f^{\rho}(\varUpsilon)\Leftrightarrow\varUpsilon=f^{\varUpsilon}(\rho)
for
\varUpsilon>0(\rho>0)
. Here
\varUpsilon\vee 0
stands for
\max(\varUpsilon,0)
and
\Lambda_{2}
is a smooth functions on

\mathbb{R}
such that
\Lambda_{2}(0)=0
.

As shown in
[
14
]
with the inertial coordinate system,
the problem is reduced to :

\varUpsilon_{b}(\bm{x})+\Phi_{b}(\bm{x})-\mathfrak{B}(\varpi)=\varUpsilon_{O}+\Phi_{b}(O)\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{D}_{0}

(2.27)

with

\displaystyle\Phi_{b}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\rho_{b}],

\displaystyle\rho_{b}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=f^{\rho}(\varUpsilon_{b}(\bm{x}))=\Big(\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma\mathsf{A}}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(\varUpsilon_{b}(\bm{x})\vee 0)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(1+\Lambda_{2}(\varUpsilon_{b}(\bm{x})).

(2.28)

and

\mathfrak{B}(\varpi)=\mathfrak{B}(\varpi;\Omega+\omega_{b}):=\int_{0}^{\varpi}(\Omega+\omega_{b}(\varpi^{\prime}))^{2}\varpi^{\prime}d\varpi^{\prime}.

(2.29)

Here
\mathfrak{D}_{0}
is a domain such
that

\varUpsilon_{b}\in C^{2,\alpha}(\mathfrak{D}_{0}\cup\partial\mathfrak{D}_{0})
and

\{\varUpsilon_{b}\geq 0\}\subset\mathfrak{D}_{0}
.

Put

\varUpsilon_{O}=\varUpsilon_{O}(\rho_{O},\Sigma):=f^{\varUpsilon}(\rho_{O})=\frac{\gamma\mathsf{A}}{\gamma-1}\rho_{O}^{\gamma-1}(1+\Lambda_{1}(\rho_{O}^{\gamma-1}))

(2.30)

by the given
\rho_{O}
, and put

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}=\mathsf{a}(\rho_{O},\Sigma):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi\mathsf{G}}}\Big(\frac{\mathsf{A}\gamma}{\gamma-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2(\gamma-1)}}\varUpsilon_{O}^{-\frac{2-\gamma}{2(\gamma-1)}},

(2.31)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{b}(\eta)=\mathfrak{b}(\eta;\rho_{O},\Sigma,\Omega+\omega_{b}):=\varUpsilon_{O}^{-1}\mathfrak{B}(\mathsf{a}\eta).

(2.32)

We put

\|\mathfrak{b}\|_{1}=\sup_{\eta\leq r_{\infty}/\mathsf{a}}|\mathfrak{b}|+\sup_{\eta\leq r_{\infty}/\mathsf{a}}\Big|\frac{d\mathfrak{b}}{d\eta}\Big|,

where
\mathfrak{D}_{0}\subset\{\|\bm{x}\|\leq r_{\infty}\}
,
and
note

\|\mathfrak{b}\|_{1}\leq\frac{1}{4\pi\mathsf{G}}\Big(\frac{\mathsf{A}\gamma}{\gamma-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\varUpsilon_{O}^{-\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\|\Omega+\omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,r_{\infty})}\Big(\frac{r_{\infty}^{2}}{2}+r_{\infty}\Big).

We suppose

Assumption
:
\frac{6}{5}<\gamma<2
, and

\mathfrak{b}
is sufficiently small, say,
\|\mathfrak{b}\|_{1}\leq\beta^{0}
,
\beta^{0}
being a positive number depending on
\gamma,\Lambda
.

Then we put

\displaystyle\rho_{b}(\bm{x})=f^{\rho}(\varUpsilon_{O}\Theta)=\Big(\frac{\gamma-1}{\mathsf{A}\gamma}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(\varUpsilon_{O}\Theta\vee 0)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}(1+\Lambda_{2}(\varUpsilon_{O}\Theta))

with

\displaystyle\Theta=\Theta\Big(\frac{r}{\mathsf{a}},\zeta\Big),

and

\displaystyle S_{b}(\bm{x})=\Sigma(\rho_{b}(\bm{x})^{\gamma-1}).

(2.33)

Here

\Theta(\xi,\zeta)

is the ”
distorted Lane-Emden function
”,
which is the solution of the integral equation

\Theta(\xi,\zeta)=1+\mathfrak{b}(\xi\sqrt{1-\zeta^{2}})+\mathscr{K}g(\xi,\zeta)-\mathscr{K}g(0,0)

with

\displaystyle\mathscr{K}g(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{-1}^{1}\int_{0}^{+\infty}K(\xi,\zeta,\xi^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime})g(\xi^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime})\xi^{\prime 2}d\xi^{\prime}d\zeta^{\prime},

\displaystyle K(\xi,\zeta,\xi^{\prime},\zeta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{d\phi}{\sqrt{\xi^{2}+\xi^{\prime 2}-2\xi\xi^{\prime}(\sqrt{1-\zeta^{2}}\sqrt{1-\zeta^{\prime 2}}\cos\phi+\zeta\zeta^{\prime})}},

\displaystyle g(\xi,\zeta)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\gamma\mathsf{A}}{\gamma-1}\Big)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}f^{\rho}\Big(\Upsilon_{O}\Theta(\xi,\zeta)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\Theta(\xi,\zeta)\vee 0)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}}\Big(1+\Lambda_{2}(\varUpsilon_{O}\Theta(\xi,\zeta))\Big),

and enjoys the following properties:

1) The function
\bm{\xi}\mapsto\Theta(\|\bm{\xi}\|,\frac{\xi^{3}}{\|\bm{\xi}\|})
belongs to
C^{2,\alpha}(\{\|\bm{\xi}\|\leq{\Xi}_{0}\})

with
0<\alpha<(\frac{1}{\gamma-1}-1)\wedge 1
,

{\Xi}_{0}=4\xi_{1}(\frac{1}{\gamma-1})
,
\xi_{1}(\frac{1}{\gamma-1})
being the zero of the Lane-Emden function

\theta(\xi;\frac{1}{\gamma-1})
of the index
\frac{1}{\gamma-1}
, that is, the solution
of

-\frac{1}{\xi^{2}}\frac{d}{d\xi}\xi^{2}\frac{d\theta}{d\xi}=(\theta\vee 0)^{\frac{1}{\gamma-1}},\quad\theta|_{\xi=0}=1.

2)
\Theta(0,\zeta)=1
and there is a curve

\zeta\in[-1,1]\mapsto{\Xi}_{1}(\zeta)
such that

\xi_{1}(\frac{1}{\gamma-1})\leq{\Xi}_{1}(\zeta)<\frac{1}{2}{\Xi}_{0}=2\xi_{1}(\frac{1}{\gamma-1})

and, for
0\leq\xi<{\Xi}_{0}
,

0<\Theta(\xi,\zeta)\Leftrightarrow 0\leq\xi<{\Xi}_{1}(\zeta).

(2.34)

3)
\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Theta}{\partial\xi}\leq-\frac{\xi}{C}
with a positive number
C
.

For proof of the existence of the distorded Lane-Emden function
\Theta
, see
[
14
]
.

For the sake of simplicity we consider
\Theta(\xi,\zeta)<0
for
\xi\geq{\Xi}_{0}

by modyfing the values of
\Theta
on
\xi>{\Xi}_{0}
,
so that (
2.34
) is valid for
0\leq\xi<\infty,|\zeta|\leq 1
.
Let us denote

\mathfrak{D}_{0}=\{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\Big|\|\bm{x}\|<R_{b0}\},\quad R_{b0}=\mathsf{a}{\Xi}_{0}=4\mathsf{a}\xi_{1}(\frac{1}{\gamma-1}).

(2.35)

Then

\varUpsilon_{b}(\bm{x})=\varUpsilon_{O}\Theta\Big(\frac{\|\bm{x}\|}{\mathsf{a}},\frac{x^{3}}{\|\bm{x}\|}\Big),

(2.36)

which belongs to
C^{2,\alpha}(\mathfrak{D}_{0}\cup\partial\mathfrak{D}_{0})
, satisfies
(
2.27
).

We denote

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{b}

\displaystyle=\{\rho_{b}>0\}=\{\Theta>0\}=\{r<R_{1b}(\zeta)\},

(2.37a)

\displaystyle\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b}

\displaystyle=\{\Theta=0\}=\{r=R_{1b}(\zeta)\},

(2.37b)

where we put

R_{1b}(\zeta)=\mathsf{a}{\Xi}_{1}(\zeta)
.

Since

P_{b}=\Pi(\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1}),

(2.38)

where

\Pi(\upsilon)=(\upsilon\vee 0)^{\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\mathscr{F}(\upsilon,\Sigma(\upsilon)),

(2.39)

the stationary solution is barotropic. Here
\Pi\in C^{1,\alpha}
for

\displaystyle\frac{\gamma}{\gamma-1}>1+\alpha
.

As for

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{b}=-\Big(\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\nabla S_{b}
,
since
S_{b}=\Sigma(\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1})
with
\Sigma\in C^{\infty}
, we see

\mathfrak{a}_{b}=-\Big(\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}{\nabla}S_{b}=-\Big(\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\frac{d\Sigma}{d\upsilon}{\nabla}\upsilon\Big|_{\upsilon=\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1}},

(2.40)

and

\mathscr{A}_{b}=\Big(\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\upsilon\frac{d\Sigma}{d\upsilon}\|{\nabla}\upsilon\|\Big|_{\upsilon=\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1}}.

(2.41)

Since
\rho_{b}^{\gamma-1}\in C^{2,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})
, we have

\mathscr{A}_{b}\in C^{1,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})
.
Recall (
2.13
).

Summing up, we get an admissible barotropic stationary solution
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
.
\square

Remark 4

Let us note that, by (
2.41
), we see

\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{b}=Df^{\varUpsilon}(\rho_{b})==\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}\Big(1+\mathscr{A}_{b}\frac{\rho_{b}}{\|\rho_{b}\|}\Big).

Therefore, if
\displaystyle\frac{d\Sigma}{d\upsilon}=0\quad\forall\upsilon
, that is,
\Sigma
is constant,
then
\mathscr{A}_{b}=0
and it holds

\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{b}=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}.

(2.42)

We shall say that the background is ‘
isentropic
’ when
\mathfrak{a}_{b}=0
as in this case. Then, if

\mathscr{F}(\upsilon,S)=\exp(S/\mathsf{C}_{V})
, we have
\Gamma=\gamma
and

P=\mathsf{A}\rho^{\gamma},\quad\varUpsilon=\frac{\gamma\mathsf{A}}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma-1};\quad S=\mathsf{C}_{V}\log\mathsf{A}.

3
Derivatin of
ELASO

Let us fix a compactly supported axially symmetric stationary solution
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
with

\displaystyle\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})=\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{3}}\times\bm{x}
.
In order to investigate solutions
(\rho,S,\bm{v})
near the fixed stationary solution, for which the domain

\mathfrak{R}(t)=\{\bm{x}\Big|\rho(t,\bm{x})>0\}
should move with the free boundary

\Sigma(t)=\partial\mathfrak{R}(t)
, we introduce the Lagrangian co-ordinate to describe the equations.

Let us look at a solution
(\rho,S,\bm{v})
which belongs to

C^{1}([0,T[\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
with

\mathfrak{R}(t)=\{\bm{x}\Big|\rho(t,\bm{x})>0\}
,
provided that it exists. Suppose that
(\mathfrak{R}(t),\partial\mathfrak{R}(t))
is differmorphic onto

(\mathfrak{R}_{b},\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})
. We are supposing that

\mathfrak{R}(0)=\{\bm{x}|\rho^{0}(\bm{x})>0\}=\mathfrak{R}_{b}.

(3.1)

However we do not suppose that
\rho^{0}=\rho_{b}
.

We can consider the flow mapping

\bm{\varphi}\in C^{1}([0,T[\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
defined by

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{v}(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),\quad\bm{\varphi}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bar{\bm{x}},

(3.2)

and
\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\in C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathbb{R}^{3})
defined by

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),\quad\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bar{\bm{x}},

(3.3)

which can be written explicitely as

\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\omega_{b}t)&-\sin(\omega_{b}t)&0\\
\sin(\omega_{b}t)&\cos(\omega_{b}t)&0\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}\bar{\bm{x}},

(3.4)

where
\omega_{b}=\omega_{b}(\bar{\varpi},\bar{z}),\bar{\varpi}=\sqrt{(\bar{x}^{1})^{2}+(\bar{x}^{2})^{2}},\bar{z}=\bar{x}^{3}
.

Through the mapping
\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})
the equations on
(t,\bm{x})
can be written as equations on
(t,\bar{\bm{x}})
. This is the Lagrangian description.

Hereafter we denote

(D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}{\bm{x}})(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=D\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\bm{x}}^{\alpha}}\Big(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\Big)^{j}\Big)_{j,\alpha}.

(3.5)

Then it hold that
D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})=I
and

D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=D\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\exp\Big[\int_{0}^{t}(D_{\bm{x}}\bm{v})(t^{\prime},\bm{\varphi}(t^{\prime},\bar{\bm{x}}))dt^{\prime}\Big]

(3.6)

Here

D_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}
stands for the matrix field

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\partial v^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}\Big)_{k,j}
.

Let us denote

\displaystyle\rho^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\rho(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),\quad P^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=P(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),

\displaystyle S^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=S(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),\quad\bm{v}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{v}(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))

(3.7)

for
(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\in[0,+\infty[\times\mathfrak{R}_{b}
.

The equations (
1.1a
), (
1.1b
), (
1.1c
)
read

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\rho^{L}}{\partial t}+(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v})(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=0,

(3.8a)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(\bm{v}^{L}-\bm{v}_{b}^{L})+B(\bm{v}^{L}-\bm{v}_{b}^{L})+\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}({\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P)-\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}({\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P_{b})+

\displaystyle+({\nabla}_{x}(\Phi-\Phi_{b}))\Big)^{L}=0,

(3.8b)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial S^{L}}{\partial t}=0.

(3.8c)

Here, for function
Q(t,\bm{x})
, we denote

Q^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=Q(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))

(3.9)

in general.

Integrating (
3.8c
), we have

S^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}}).

(3.10)

Integrating (
3.8a
), we have

\rho^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\exp\Big[-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds\Big]

(3.11)

for
(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\in[0,+\infty[\times\mathfrak{R}_{b}
.

Let us denote

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\rho^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\rho(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),

(3.12a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}P(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=P^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-P_{b}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=P(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-P_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),

(3.12b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}S(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=S^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-S_{b}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=S(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-S_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=

\displaystyle=S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-S_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),

(3.12c)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\Phi(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\Phi^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\Phi_{b}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\Phi(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\Phi_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),

(3.12d)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\bm{v}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{v}_{b}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}),

(3.12e)

and

\displaystyle{\delta}\rho(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\rho(t,\bm{x})-\rho_{b}(t,\bm{x}),

(3.13a)

\displaystyle{\delta}P(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=P(t,{\bm{x}})-P_{b}(t,{\bm{x}}),

(3.13b)

\displaystyle{\delta}S(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=S(t,{\bm{x}})-S_{b}(t,{\bm{x}}),

(3.13c)

\displaystyle{\delta}\Phi(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\Phi(t,{\bm{x}})-\Phi_{b}(t,{\bm{x}}),

(3.13d)

\displaystyle{\delta}\bm{v}(t,{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\bm{v}(t,{\bm{x}})-\bm{v}_{b}(t,\bm{x}).

(3.13e)

Then the equations
(
3.8b
) and (
2.19
) read

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}+

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P-\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P_{b}+{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}(\Phi-\Phi_{b})\Big)^{L}=-\Big((D_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}_{b})\delta\bm{v}\Big)^{L}.

(3.14)

• Now let us derive the linearized approximation of (
3.11
),
(
3.14
),
supposing Assumption
2
:
\rho-\rho_{b},S-S_{b},\bm{v}_{b},\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{b}
are of
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.

First of all we note that (
3.6
) shows

\displaystyle D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=I+\int_{0}^{t}(D_{\bm{x}}\bm{v})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(3.15)

\displaystyle=I+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon),

(3.16)

since we are assuming
\bm{v}=\bm{v}_{b}+(\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{b})=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.
Of course

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\bar{\bm{x}}+\int_{0}^{t}\bm{v}(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds

\displaystyle=\bar{\bm{x}}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon).

(3.17)

Therefore, if
\bm{Q}=\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x})=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, then

\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x})=\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{Q}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.18)

and, if
Q=Q(t,\bm{x})=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, then

{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x})={\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}Q^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.19)

while
\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})
. In other words

\displaystyle\Big(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{Q}\Big)^{L}=\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{Q}^{L}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.20)

\displaystyle\Big({\nabla}_{\bm{x}}Q\Big)^{L}={\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}Q^{L}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(3.21)

Remark 5

If we assume only
\bm{v}-\bm{v}_{b}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, but do not assume
\bm{v}_{b}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, then (
3.16
) can be not the case, but we have

\displaystyle D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon),

\displaystyle D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\cos(\omega_{b}t)&-\sin(\omega_{b}t)&0\\
\sin(\omega_{b}t)&\cos(\omega_{b}t)&0\\
0&0&1\end{bmatrix}+\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial\varpi}t\begin{bmatrix}-x_{b}^{2}\bar{x}^{1}&-x_{b}^{1}\bar{x}^{2}&0\\
x_{b}^{1}\bar{x}^{1}&x_{b}^{1}\bar{x}^{2}&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle+\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial z}t\begin{bmatrix}0&0&-x_{b}^{2}\\
0&0&x_{b}^{1}\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix},

where
\bm{x}_{b}=\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})
,

\omega_{b}=\omega_{b}(\bar{\varpi},\bar{z})=\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)
,

\varpi=\sqrt{(x_{b}^{1})^{2}+(x_{b}^{2})^{2})},\bar{\varpi}=\sqrt{(\bar{x}^{1})^{2}+(\bar{x}^{2})^{2})}
,

z=x_{b}^{3},\bar{z}=\bar{x}^{3}
.
Thus, when
\omega_{b}\not=0
, we have

D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}\not=I+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, and the formulae (
3.20
),(
3.21
) do not work.

Let us look at (
3.11
), which can be written as

\rho(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\exp\Big[-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}{\delta}\bm{v})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds\Big].

(3.22)

Let us verify it. Look at

\displaystyle\exp\Big[-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds\Big]=

\displaystyle=\exp\Big[-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}_{b})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds\Big]\cdot\exp\Big[-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}{\delta}\bm{v})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds\Big].

Since

\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})=\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)\begin{bmatrix}-x^{2}\\
x^{1}\\
0\end{bmatrix},

we see
\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}_{b}=0
. Therefore

\exp\Big[-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}_{b})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds\Big]=I

and (
3.22
) follows.

Recalling

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{v}_{b}(t,\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),\quad\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bar{\bm{x}},

we have

\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\int_{0}^{t}({\delta}\bm{v})(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.23)

since

\displaystyle\bm{v}(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=

\displaystyle=({\delta}\bm{v})(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))+\Big(\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))\Big)

\displaystyle=({\delta}\bm{v})(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Then we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\Big({\delta}\bm{v}(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))\Big)ds+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}({\delta}\bm{v})\Big)(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))ds+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Thus (
3.22
)
reads

\rho(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\exp\Big[-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))\Big]+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

or
we can claim

\rho(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(3.24)

Further we have, in modulo
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\rho(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))

\displaystyle\equiv\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))

\displaystyle\equiv\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\Big({\nabla}\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))\Big|\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\Big)

\displaystyle-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))+\Big({\nabla}\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\Big|\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\Big)

\displaystyle=\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\Big(\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))\Big).

Since it holds that

\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}}),

(3.25)

we can claim

\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\Big(\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\Big)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.26)

since we are assuming that

\rho_{b}-\rho^{0}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.

Now let us introduce
\bm{u}
by

\bm{u}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\bm{u}^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}}),

(3.27)

where
\bm{u}^{0}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
. Then we can claim

\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\bm{u}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.28)

provided that

\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})=-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\bm{u}^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})).

(3.29)

We note that

\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.30)

where we recall

\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=({\delta}\bm{v})(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))

and
\bm{v}(t,\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
.

Let us look at
\mathfrak{d}S
. We can claim

\mathfrak{d}S(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=-\Big(\bm{u}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\Big|{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\Big)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(3.31)

provided that

S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})=-(\bm{u}^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\Big|{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})).

(3.32)

In fact, we see

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}S(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-S_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))

\displaystyle=S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\Big[S_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))+\Big(\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))\Big|{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}S_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))\Big)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\Big]

\displaystyle=S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\Big(\bm{u}-\bm{u}^{0}\Big|{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\Big)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=-(\bm{u}\Big|{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}}))+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

provided (
3.32
). Here we have used the indentity

S_{b}(\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}))=S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}}),

(3.33)

which holds thanks to the axial symmetricity of
S_{b}
.

Moreover we can replace (
3.32
) by

\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})=-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\Big(\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\bm{u}^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\Big)

(3.34)

in modulo
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
, since

\displaystyle\rho^{0}S^{0}

\displaystyle-\rho_{b}S_{b}+\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}S_{b}\bm{u}^{0})=\rho^{0}\Big(S^{0}-S_{b}+(\bm{u}^{0}|{\nabla}S_{b})\Big)+

\displaystyle+S_{b}\mathrm{div}\Big((\rho^{0}-\rho_{b})\bm{u}^{0}\Big)+(\rho^{0}-\rho_{b})(S^{0}-S_{b}),

provided (
3.29
).
Note that (
3.29
) and (
3.34
) imply

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\rho^{0}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}=\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\rho_{b}(\bm{x})d\bm{x},

(3.35)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\rho^{0}(\bm{x})S^{0}(\bm{x})d\bm{x}=\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\rho_{b}(\bm{x})S_{b}(\bm{x})d\bm{x},

(3.36)

which mean that the total mass and the total entropy are the same for the perturbed configulation and the unperturbed background.

Let us look at
\mathfrak{d}P
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}P(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b}^{L},S_{b}^{L})(t,\bar{\bm{x}})}\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b}^{L},S_{b}^{L}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})}\mathfrak{d}S(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Since

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b}^{L},S_{b}^{L})(t,\bar{\bm{x}})}=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b},S_{b})(\bar{\bm{x}})}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b},S_{b})(\bar{\bm{x}})}=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b},S_{b})(\bar{\bm{x}})}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon),

we see

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}P(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b},S_{b})(\bar{\bm{x}})}\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b},S_{b})(\bar{\bm{x}})}\mathfrak{d}S(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Let us look at

\displaystyle\Phi(t,\bm{x})-\Phi_{b}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{G}\int\frac{\rho(t,\bm{x}^{\prime})-\rho_{b}(\bm{x}^{\prime})}{\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|}d\bm{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\frac{\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{\|\bar{\bm{x}}-\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\|}d\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(3.37)

In fact, applying the Gronwall argument to the identity

\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})=\bar{\bm{x}}-\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}+\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\bm{v}(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\bm{v}(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}))\Big]ds,

we can derive the estimate

\frac{1}{1+C_{1}|\varepsilon|T}\|\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})\|\leq\|\bar{\bm{x}}-\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\|\leq\frac{1}{1-C_{1}|\varepsilon|T}\|\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})\|

for
t\in[0,T],\bar{\bm{x}},\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}
, provided that
C_{1}|\varepsilon|T<1
,
where

C_{1}=\frac{1}{|\varepsilon|}\sup\Big\{\|D_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}(t,\bm{x})\|\Big|0\leq t\leq T,\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{D}_{0}\Big\}

so that

\Big|\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\bm{v}(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}))-\bm{v}(s,\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}))\Big]ds\Big|\leq C_{1}|\varepsilon|\int_{0}^{t}\|\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}})-\bm{\varphi}(s,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})\|ds,

and we have

d\bm{x}^{\prime}=\Big|\mathrm{det}D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})\Big|d\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}=(1+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon))d\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}.

Hence

\frac{\rho(t,\bm{x}^{\prime})-\rho_{b}(\bm{x}^{\prime})}{\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|}d\bm{x}^{\prime}=\frac{\mathfrak{d}\rho(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})}{\|\bar{\bm{x}}-\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\|}d\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

For
\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(1,\bar{\bm{x}}),\bm{x}^{\prime}=\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime})
, therefore
(
3.37
) holds.

Combining (
3.28
) and (
3.37
), we see

\Phi(t,\bm{x})-\Phi_{b}(\bm{x})=\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\frac{\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\rho_{b}\bm{u}(t,\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}))}{\|\bar{\bm{x}}-\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}\|}d\bar{\bm{x}}^{\prime}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(3.38)

We note

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho}{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P-\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}(P-P_{b})-\frac{\rho-\rho_{b}}{\rho_{b}^{2}}{\nabla}_{\bm{x}}P_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}^{L}}{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\mathfrak{d}P)-\frac{\mathfrak{d}\rho}{(\rho_{b}^{L})^{2}}{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}P_{b}^{L}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(3.39)

with
\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})
.

Summing up, we get the linearized approximation of (
3.14
):

\frac{\partial\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}}{\partial t}+B{\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}}+\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=0,

(3.40)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\mathfrak{d}P)-\frac{{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}P_{b}}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\mathfrak{d}\rho+{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\Big(4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[-\mathfrak{d}\rho]\Big),

(3.41)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\rho=-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\rho_{b}\bm{u}),

(3.42)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}S=-(\bm{u}|\nabla_{\bar{\bm{x}}}S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})),

(3.43)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}P(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b},S_{b})(\bar{\bm{x}})}\mathfrak{d}\rho+

(3.44)

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)\Big|_{(\rho,S)=(\rho_{b},S_{b})(\bar{\bm{x}})}\mathfrak{d}S,

(3.45)

where we read
\rho_{b}=\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}}),P_{b}=P_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}}),S_{b}=S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})
.

Since
\mathfrak{d}\bm{v}=\partial\bm{u}/\partial t+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
( (
3.30
) ), (
3.40
)
reads

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{u}}{\partial t^{2}}+B\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=0.

(3.46)

This equation (
3.46
) is the linearized equation for perturbations described by the Lagrangian coordinate to be analyzed.

Let us recall
that we are assuming (
3.1
), (
3.29
) and (
3.32
).

Remark 6

Strictly speaking, we should introduce
\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho\rfloor,\lceil\mathfrak{d}S\rfloor,\lceil\mathfrak{d}P\rfloor,\lceil\mathfrak{d}\Phi\rfloor
by defining

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho\rfloor(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{div}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}(\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\bm{u}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})),

(3.47a)

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}S\rfloor(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=-(\bm{u}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})|{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})),

(3.47b)

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}P\rfloor(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho\rfloor(t,\bar{\bm{x}})+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})\lceil\mathfrak{d}S\rfloor(t,\bar{\bm{x}}),

(3.47c)

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}\Phi\rfloor(t,\bar{\bm{x}})

\displaystyle=4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[-\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho\rfloor](t,\bar{\bm{x}}).

(3.47d)

Then (
3.41
) should read

\bm{L}\bm{u}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\lceil\mathfrak{d}P\rfloor-\frac{{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}P_{b}}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho\rfloor+{\nabla}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\lceil\mathfrak{d}\Phi\rfloor,

(3.48)

while

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\rho

\displaystyle=\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho\rfloor+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}S

\displaystyle=\lceil\mathfrak{d}S\rfloor+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}P

\displaystyle=\lceil\mathfrak{d}P\rfloor+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\Phi

\displaystyle=\lceil\mathfrak{d}\Phi\rfloor+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Remark 7

The derivation of the linearized approximation of the equations in Lagrangian co-ordinate system can be found

[
19
, Sect. 56]
,
[
1
, pp. 139-140.]
,
[
2
, p.11, (A)]
,
[
20
]
,
[
17
, p.500, (1)]
and so on. But there was considered only the case of
\rho^{0}=\rho_{b},S^{0}=S_{b}
. Moreover, the definition of Lagrange displacement and ELASO, the linearized equation of adiabatic perturbations, derived by them and traditionally used by astrophysicists are slightly different from those formulated here, when
\bm{v}_{b}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
is not supposed. For the details see Section 7.

4
Basic existence theorem for
ELASO
–Application of the Hille-Yosida theory

We are going to discuss on the existence of solutions to the
equation of
ELASO
(
1.11
).
We formulate the initial value problem to be considered as:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{u}}{\partial t^{2}}+B\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=\bm{f}(t,\bm{x})\quad\mbox{on}\quad[0,+\infty[\times\mathfrak{R}_{b},

(4.1a)

\displaystyle\bm{u}|_{t=0}=\bm{u}^{0},\quad\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}=\bm{v}^{0}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{R}_{b},

(4.1b)

where
\bm{u}^{0},\bm{v}^{0},\bm{f}
are given data.

Let us rewrite

\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}:=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\mathfrak{d}P-\frac{\nabla P_{b}}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\mathfrak{d}\rho,

using
\Gamma_{b},\mathfrak{a}_{b}
defined in Definition
3
. Since

\mathfrak{d}P=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\mathfrak{d}\rho+(\Gamma P)_{b}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a}_{b}),

we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}

\displaystyle=\nabla\Big[-\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})+\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a}_{b})\Big]+

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}\Big[-\mathfrak{a}_{b}\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})+(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a}_{b})\nabla\rho_{b}\Big].

Consequently we can write

\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{L}_{1}

(4.2)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}

\displaystyle=\nabla\Big[-\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})+\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a}_{b})\Big]+

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}\Big[-\mathfrak{a}_{b}\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})+(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a}_{b})\nabla\rho_{b}\Big]

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}\bm{u}

\displaystyle={\nabla}\mathcal{K}(\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})).

(4.4)

4.1
Case of barotropic backgrounds

Now we suppose

Assumption
:
The equation of state enjoys
Assumption

5
and the background
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
is a barotropic admissible stationary solution.

Hereafter we denote

\mathfrak{R}_{b}
,

\rho_{b}(\bm{x}),P_{b}(\bm{x}),S_{b}(\bm{x})
,
\mathfrak{a}_{b},\mathscr{A}_{b}

by

\mathfrak{R}
,

\rho(\bm{x}),P(\bm{x}),S(\bm{x})
,
\mathfrak{a},\mathscr{A}

for the sake of simplicity of symbols.

Let us consider the integro-differential operator
\mathcal{L}
in the Hilbert space

\mathfrak{H}
:

Definition 4

We denote

\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathfrak{R},\rho d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}^{3})
endowed with the inner product

(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}(\bm{u}_{1}(\bm{x})|\bm{u}_{2}(\bm{x}))\rho(\bm{x})d\bm{x}.

(4.5)

Of course

(\bm{u}_{1}(\bm{x})|\bm{u}_{2}(\bm{x})):=\sum_{k}u_{1}^{k}(\bm{x})u_{2}^{k}(\bm{x})^{*}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{u}_{\mu}(\bm{x})=\begin{bmatrix}u_{\mu}^{1}(\bm{x})\\
\\
u_{\mu}^{2}(\bm{x})\\
\\
u_{\mu}^{3}(\bm{x})\end{bmatrix},\mu=1,2.

Here and hereafter
Z^{*}
denotes the complex conjugate
X-\mathrm{i}Y
of
Z=X+\mathrm{i}Y
, while
\mathrm{i}
stands for the imaginary unit,
\sqrt{-1}
.

First we observe
\mathcal{L}
restricted on
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{C}^{3})
.

We have the following formula by integration by parts:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}+

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big(-(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathfrak{a})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}-\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})(\bm{u}_{2}|\mathfrak{a})^{*}\Big)+

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathfrak{a})({\nabla}\rho|\bm{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}+

\displaystyle+\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}\Big((\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{n})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}+(\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})(\bm{u}_{2}|\bm{n})^{*}\Big)

\displaystyle-\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{n})(\bm{u}_{2}|\bm{n})^{*},

where
we recall

\mathfrak{a}=-\mathscr{A}\bm{n},\quad\bm{n}=\frac{{\nabla}\rho}{\|{\nabla}\rho\|},

(4.6)

and

(\mathcal{L}_{1}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})=-\int\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})]\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}.

(4.7)

Hence

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}+2\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{n})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}\Big]

\displaystyle-\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{n})(\bm{u}_{2}|\bm{n})^{*}+

\displaystyle-4\pi\mathsf{G}\int\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})]\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}.

(4.8)

Then it is clear that

(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})=(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{2})\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}),

(4.9)

that is,
\mathcal{L}
restricted on
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
is a symmetric operator.

Moreover we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}|\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+2\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(u|\bm{n})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}\Big]+

\displaystyle-\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}|(\bm{u}|\bm{n})|^{2}.

(4.10)

Since
\mathscr{A}\in C^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}))
, we have

\kappa_{1}:=\sup_{\mathfrak{R}}\Big\{\ |\mathscr{A}|^{2}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\ \Big\}<\infty.

(4.11)

Therefore

\Big|2\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\bm{n})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}\Big|\leq\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+2\kappa_{1}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

On the other hand, we have

\kappa_{2}:=\sup_{\mathfrak{R}}\Big\{\frac{\Gamma P|\mathscr{A}|}{\rho}\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}\Big\}<\infty.

(4.12)

Then

\Big|\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}|(\bm{u}|\bm{n})|^{2}\Big|\leq\kappa_{2}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

Thus we have

Proposition 1

It holds that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}-(2\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2})\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq(\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}|\bm{u})\leq

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+(2\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2})\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

(4.13)

As for
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, on the other hand, we have

Proposition 2

It holds that

-\rho_{O}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq(\mathcal{L}_{1}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq 0.

(4.14)

Proof. Look at

\Psi:=-\mathcal{K}[g],\quad\bm{C}:=\rho\bm{u}-{\nabla}\Psi.

(4.15)

Since
\triangle\Psi=g
, we have

\mathrm{div}\bm{C}=0.

Keeping in mind that
\Psi=O(\frac{1}{r}),{\nabla}\Psi,\bm{C}=O(\frac{1}{r^{2}})

as
r\rightarrow+\infty
, we derive from this that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}({\nabla}\Psi|\bm{C})d\bm{x}=0

(4.16)

Then we see

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}({\nabla}\Psi|\rho\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}({\nabla}\Psi|\bm{C}+{\nabla}\Psi)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\|{\nabla}\Psi\|^{2}d\bm{x}\quad\mbox{(by \eqref{4.12})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\|\rho\bm{u}\|^{2}d\bm{x}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\|\bm{C}\|^{2}d\bm{x}\quad\mbox{(again by \eqref{4.12})}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\|\rho\bm{u}\|^{2}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{O}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\|u\|^{2}\rho d\bm{x}=\rho_{O}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

and

\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}d\bm{x}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\|{\nabla}\Psi\|^{2}d\bm{x}\geq 0.

\square

This proof is due to Juhi Jang. See
[
15
, Proposition 2]
.

Summing up, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}-(2\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\rho_{O})\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})\leq

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+(2\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2})\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

(4.17)

Therefore the operator
\mathcal{L}
restricted on
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
is
symmetric, and is bounded from below as an operator in
\mathfrak{H}
. Applying the Stone-Friedrichs theorem, (e.g.,
[
16
, Chapter VI, Section 2.3]
),
we can claim:

Theorem 2

\mathcal{L}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
admits the Friedrichs extension

\bm{L}
, which is a self-adjoint operator
in
\mathfrak{H}
, whose domain is

\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})=\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}\}.

(4.18)

Here we use

Definition 5

\mathfrak{G}
stands for the Hilbert space
of all
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}<\infty
endowed with the inner product

(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{G}}=(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}+\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}(\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})(\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}d\bm{x},

(4.19)

and
\mathfrak{G}_{0}
is the closure of
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
in
\mathfrak{G}
.

In fact, we define the quadratic form
Q_{0}
by

\displaystyle Q_{0}[\bm{u}]=Q_{0}(\bm{u},\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+2\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\bm{n})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}d\bm{x}\Big]+

\displaystyle-\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}|(\bm{u}|\bm{n})|^{2}\|{\nabla}\rho\|d\bm{x}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\int\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})]\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}d\bm{x}.

(4.20)

Then
it holds that

(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}=Q_{0}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})

(4.21)

for
\forall\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
.
We define

Q_{a}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})=Q_{0}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})+a(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

(4.22)

with

a:=2\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\rho_{O}.

(4.23)

Since

\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}\leq Q_{a}[\bm{u}]\leq\frac{3}{2}\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+(2\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+a)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

(4.24)

we have

0\leq Q_{a}[\bm{u}]\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}.

(4.25)

Summing up, we define the alternative inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}
of
\mathfrak{G}
by

\displaystyle(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}

\displaystyle=Q_{a}(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})+(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=Q_{0}(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})+(a+1)(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

(4.26)

with the norm

\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}=\sqrt{(\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}}.

(4.27)

Then
\|\cdot\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}
is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{G}}
, that is, there is a constant
C

such that

\frac{1}{C}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{G}}\leq\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}\leq C\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{G}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}.

(4.28)

It hold that

\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}\geq\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G},

(4.29)

and

(\bm{u}|\bm{v})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}=((\bm{L}+a+1)\bm{u}|\bm{v})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{v}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}.

(4.30)

In order to avoid confusions, hereafter we use

Notation 2

We denote the Hilbert space of all funstions in
\mathfrak{G}

[(\mathfrak{G}_{0})]
endowed with the inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}
and norm
\|\cdot\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}
by
\breve{\mathfrak{G}}

[(\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}})]
respectively.

In this situation we can apply the Hille-Yosida theory to the first order evoltion equation
ELASO‡
in
[0,+\infty[\times\mathfrak{E}
:

\displaystyle\frac{dU}{dt}+\bm{A}U=\bm{0},

(4.31)

\displaystyle\bm{A}=\begin{bmatrix}O&-I\\
\\
\bm{L}&\bm{B}\end{bmatrix},\quad\mathsf{D}(\bm{A})=\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\times\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}},

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}=\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{H}

(4.33)

which is equivalent to
ELASO
through the interpretation

U=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}\\
\\
\frac{d\bm{u}}{dt}\end{bmatrix}.

(4.34)

This application iss described in
Appendix A
. The conclusion is:

Theorem 3

Suppose
\bm{u}^{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
,

\bm{v}^{0}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}

and
\bm{f}\in C([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H})
. Then the initial value problem
(
4.1a
) (
4.1b
) admits a unique solution

\bm{u}=\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})
in

C^{2}([0,+\infty[,\mathfrak{H})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[,\mathfrak{G}_{0})\cap C([0,+\infty[,\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}))

and the energy

\displaystyle E(t,\bm{u})

\displaystyle:=\||\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{u}||_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+Q_{a}[\bm{u}]+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}=(1+a)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+Q_{0}[\bm{u}]+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

(4.35)

enjoys the estimate

\sqrt{E(t,\bm{u})}\leq e^{\kappa t}\sqrt{E(0,\bm{u})}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\kappa(t-s)}\|\bm{f}(s)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}ds

(4.36)

for

\kappa=1+a
.

Let us claim the following

Theorem 4

0
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{L}
and
\mathrm{dim}\mathsf{N}(\bm{L})=\infty
.

Proof. 1) Suppose
\mathscr{A}\not=0
, that is,
{\nabla}S_{b}(\bm{x})\not=0
for
\exists\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}
. Then

\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}(\bm{x})}{\nabla}S_{b}(\bm{x})\times{\nabla}f(\bm{x}),

(4.37)

where
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{R})
is arbitrary, enjoys

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho;\bm{u}^{0}\rfloor=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u}^{0})=0,

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}S;\bm{u}^{0}\rfloor=-(\bm{u}^{0}|{\nabla}S_{b})=0,

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}P;\bm{u}^{0}\rfloor=-\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho;\bm{u}^{0}\rfloor+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\lceil\mathfrak{d}S;\bm{u}^{0})\rfloor=0,

\displaystyle\lceil\mathfrak{d}\Phi;\bm{u}^{0}\rfloor=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\lceil\mathfrak{d}\rho;\bm{u}^{0}\rfloor]=0,

therefore
\bm{L}\bm{u}^{0}=\bf{0}
. (Recall the notations of Remark
6
.) If
{\nabla}f(\bm{x}_{1})\not=0

and
{\nabla}S_{b}(\bm{x}_{1})\not=\bf{0}
at
\exists\bm{x}_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}
, then
\bm{u}^{0}\not=0
and
\bm{u}^{0}
is an eigenvector of the eigenvalue
0
.

2) Suppose
\mathscr{A}=0
, that is,
{\nabla}S_{b}(\bm{x})=0
for
\forall\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}
,
or, the background is isentropic. Then,
for any vector field
\bm{f}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the vector field

\bm{u}^{0}
defined by

\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}(\bm{x})}\mathrm{rot}\bm{f}(\bm{x})

(4.38)

enjoys

\mathfrak{d}\rho=0,\mathfrak{d}S=0,\mathfrak{d}P=0,\mathfrak{d}\Phi=0
, so that
\bm{L}\bm{u}^{0}=\bf{0}
.
If
\mathrm{rot}\bm{f}\not=\bf{0}
somewhere, then
\bm{u}^{0}\not=\bf{0}
and it is an eigenvector.
\square

Roughly or formally speaking, the boundary condition for
\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
is:

(\rho_{b}\bm{u}|\bm{N})=0\quad\mbox{on}\quad\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b},

where
\bm{N}
is the normal vector at the boundary point, namely,

\Big(\rho_{b}(\bm{x})\bm{u}(\bm{x})\Big|\bm{n}(\bm{x})\Big)\rightarrow 0\quad\mbox{as}\quad\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}\rightarrow\bm{z}\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b},

where
\bm{n}(\bm{x})=\nabla\rho_{b}(\bm{x})/\|\nabla\rho_{b}(\bm{x})\|

which is in
C^{1,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})
and enjoys

\bm{n}(\bm{z})=\lim_{\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}\rightarrow\bm{z}}\bm{n}(\bm{x})=\bm{N}(\bm{z})

for
\bm{z}\in\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b}
. Note that, if
\bm{u}
is bounded near the boundary point, this condition is satisfied, since
\rho_{b}(\bm{z})=0
,
and that the boundary condition does not require
(\bm{N}|\bm{u})=0

on the boundary.

More precisely speaking, the boundary condition means as follows. Let
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}
. Put

\hat{\bm{u}}:=\rho_{b}\bm{u}
. Then
\hat{\bm{u}}\in H_{0}(\mathrm{div})
, where

\displaystyle H(\mathrm{div}):=\{\bm{w}\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{C}^{3})\ |\ \mathrm{div}\bm{w}\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{C}\},

\displaystyle H_{0}(\mathrm{div}):=\mbox{the closure of}\quad C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{C}^{3})\quad\mbox{in}\quad H(\mathrm{div}),

since

\inf_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}>0,\quad\inf_{\mathfrak{R}_{b}}\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}=\frac{1}{\rho_{O}}>0.

It is known that the boundary value operator

\gamma_{\bm{N}}:\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})\rightarrow C(\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{C}):\bm{w}\mapsto(\bm{w}|\bm{N})
, where

\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}):=C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{C}^{3})\restriction R_{b},

can be extended to a continous linear mapping

\tilde{\gamma}_{\bm{N}}
from
H(\mathrm{div})
into

H^{-\frac{1}{2}}(\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{C})
(
[
9
, Theorem I.2.5]
),
and
H_{0}(\mathrm{div})=\mathsf{N}(\tilde{\gamma}_{\bm{N}})
(
[
9
, Theorem I.2.6]
).
The boundary condition means

(\hat{\bm{u}}|\bm{N})=(\rho_{b}\bm{u}|\bm{N})=0\quad\mbox{on}\quad\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b}

in this distribution sense.

4.2
Case of baloclinic backgrounds

Let us consider the case when the background is not barotropic, but ‘
baloclinic
’, that is,
\nabla P
and
\nabla\rho
are not parallel somewhere in
\mathfrak{R}
.

In this case
\mathcal{L}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{C}^{3})
is not symmetric.
In fact, we consider

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}-(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big((\bm{u}_{1}|\mathfrak{a})(\nabla\rho|\bm{u}_{2})-(\bm{u}_{1}|\nabla\rho)(\mathfrak{a}|\bm{u}_{2})\Big)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\int\frac{1}{\rho}\Big(-(\bm{u}_{1}|\nabla P)(\nabla\rho|\bm{u}_{2})+(\bm{u}_{1}|\nabla\rho)(\nabla P|\bm{u}_{2})\Big)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=:\clubsuit

for
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
. Put

c(\bm{x}):=\frac{|(\nabla\rho(\bm{x})|\nabla P(\bm{x}))|}{\|\nabla\rho(\bm{x})\|\|\nabla P(\bm{x})\|}.

If
\nabla P
is not parallel to
\nabla\rho
at a point
\bm{x}_{1}\in\mathfrak{R}
, then there is a neighborhood
\mathscr{V}
of
\bm{x}_{1}
, say a small ball with center
\bm{x}_{1}
and radius
\delta
, such that

\mathscr{V}\Subset\mathfrak{R},\qquad c(\bm{x})^{2}\leq 1-\kappa\quad\forall\bm{x}\in\mathscr{V}

with
0<\kappa<1
. Consider
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
such that

\bm{u}_{1}=\nabla\rho,\qquad\bm{u}_{2}=\nabla P\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathscr{V}.

(4.39)

Then

\displaystyle\clubsuit

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathscr{V}}\frac{1}{\rho}\|\nabla\rho\|^{2}\|\nabla P\|^{2}(1-c(\bm{x})^{2})d\bm{x}+I

\displaystyle\geq\varepsilon+I,

where

\displaystyle\varepsilon=\kappa\inf_{\mathscr{V}}\frac{1}{\rho}\|\nabla\rho\|^{2}\|\nabla P\|^{2}\cdot\frac{4\pi}{3}\delta^{3}>0,

\displaystyle I=\int_{\mathfrak{R}\setminus\mathscr{V}}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big(-(\bm{u}_{1}|\nabla P)(\nabla\rho|\bm{u}_{2})+(\bm{u}_{1}|\nabla\rho)(\nabla P|\bm{u}_{2})\Big)d\bm{x}.

Clearly we can find
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})
which enjoy (
4.39
) and

|I|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
. Then
\clubsuit\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}>0
, that is,

(\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}\not=(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}
.

Therefor we cannot suppose that
(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}
is real for any
\bm{u}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{C}^{3})
.
In fact we have a following counterexample. Look at

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Im}\Big[(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\mathfrak{Im}[(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})(\nabla\rho|\bm{u})]d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{1}{\rho}\mathfrak{Im}[(\bm{u}|\nabla P)(\nabla\rho|\bm{u})]d\bm{x}.

Noting that

\nabla P\parallel\nabla\rho\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad|(\nabla P|\nabla\rho)|=\|\nabla P\|\|\nabla\rho\|,

we suppose that there is a small subdomain
\mathcal{V}
of
\mathfrak{R}
and a small positive number
\delta
such that

|(\nabla P|\nabla\rho)|\leq(1-\delta)\|\nabla P\|\|\nabla\rho\|\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{V}.

Let us consider
\bm{u}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{C}^{3})
such that

\bm{u}=\nabla P+\mathrm{i}\nabla\rho\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathcal{V}.

Look at

\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{1}{\rho}\mathfrak{Im}[(\bm{u}|\nabla P)(\nabla\rho|\bm{u})]d\bm{x}=J_{1}+J_{2},

where

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{V}}\frac{1}{\rho}\mathfrak{Im}[(\bm{u}|\nabla P)(\nabla\rho|\bm{u})]d\bm{x}

\displaystyle J_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{R}\setminus\mathcal{V}}\frac{1}{\rho}\mathfrak{Im}[(\bm{u}|\nabla P)(\nabla\rho|\bm{u})]d\bm{x}.

Then we have

\displaystyle J_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{V}}\Big(\|\nabla P\|^{2}\|\nabla\rho\|^{2}-|(\nabla P|\nabla\rho)|^{2}\Big)d\bm{x}

\displaystyle\geq\delta(2-\delta)\int_{\mathcal{V}}\frac{1}{\rho}\|\nabla P\|^{2}\|\nabla\rho\|^{2}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=:\delta^{\prime}>0.

We can suppose
\displaystyle|J_{2}|\leq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}
by evaluating
\bm{u}
outside
\mathcal{V}
appropriately, we have

\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{1}{\rho}\mathfrak{Im}[(\bm{u}|\nabla P)(\nabla\rho|\bm{u})]d\bm{x}\geq\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}.

Then
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Im}[(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}]\leq-\frac{\delta^{\prime}}{2}<0
and

(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\not\in\mathbb{R}
.
This is a counterexample against
(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}
to be real.
Due to this trouble we discuss the problem as follows.

Now, supposing only

Assumption
:
The equation of state enjoys
Assumption

5

,

we consider the
operator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bm{u}

\displaystyle=\nabla\Big[-\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})+\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})\Big]+\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big[-\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\mathfrak{a}+(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})\nabla\rho\Big]+

\displaystyle+4\pi\mathsf{G}\nabla\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})].

(4.40)

Let us consider the formal adjoint operator
\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}
of
\mathcal{L}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\bm{u}

\displaystyle:=\nabla\Big[-\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})+\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})\Big]+\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big[-\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\mathfrak{a}+(\bm{u}|\nabla\rho)\mathfrak{a}\Big]+

\displaystyle+4\pi\mathsf{G}\nabla\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})].

(4.41)

Then, for
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}
, we have

(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(4.42)

Thus “
\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=\bm{f}
in the distribution sense” means

(\bm{u}|\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\bm{\psi})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{f}|\bm{\psi})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{\psi}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}).

Put

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{S}\bm{u}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}+\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\bm{u})=

\displaystyle=\nabla\Big[-\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})+\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})\Big]+

\displaystyle+\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big[-\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\mathfrak{a}+\frac{1}{2}\Big((\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})\nabla\rho+(\bm{u}|\nabla\rho)\mathfrak{a}\Big)\Big]+

\displaystyle+4\pi\mathsf{G}\nabla\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})],

(4.43)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\bm{u}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}-\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}\bm{u})=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big[(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})\nabla\rho-(\bm{u}|\nabla\rho)\mathfrak{a}\Big].

(4.44)

Then

\mathcal{L}^{S}
is symmetric,
\mathcal{N}
is skew-symmetric,
that is,
(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{N}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}=-(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{N}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

for
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}
, and

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}^{S}+\mathcal{N},\qquad\mathcal{L}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{L}^{S}-\mathcal{N}.

(4.45)

We consider the sesquilinear form
Q_{0}^{S}
defined by

\displaystyle Q_{0}^{S}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}(\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})(\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}+

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big((\mathfrak{a}|\bm{u}_{1})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})+(\bm{u}_{2}|\mathfrak{a})^{*}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})\Big)d\bm{x}+

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathfrak{a})(\nabla\rho|\bm{u}_{2})d\bm{x}+

\displaystyle-4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{1})]\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u}_{2})^{*}d\bm{x}

(4.46)

on
\mathfrak{G}
.
Then we have

Q_{0}^{S}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})=(\mathcal{L}^{S}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}.

(4.47)

Moreover we have
Q_{0}^{S}[\bm{u}]=Q_{0}^{S}(\bm{u},\bm{u})\in\mathbb{R}
for
\forall\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}
.

Now we see that
\mathcal{L}^{S}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}
is bounded from below.
Actually we look at

\displaystyle Q_{0}^{S}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle:=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|(\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+

\displaystyle-2\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathfrak{Re}[(\mathfrak{a}|\bm{u})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}]d\bm{x}+

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\mathfrak{Re}[(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})(\nabla\rho|\bm{u})]d\bm{x}+

\displaystyle-4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})]\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}d\bm{x}.

(4.48)

Since

\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\in\rho^{\gamma-1}C^{3,\alpha},\mathfrak{a}\in C^{3,\alpha},\frac{\nabla\rho}{\rho}\in\rho^{\gamma-1}C^{3,\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}\cup\partial\mathfrak{R}),

the numbers

\displaystyle\kappa_{1}:=\sup_{\mathfrak{R}}\Big\{\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\|\mathfrak{a}\|^{2}\Big\},

(4.49)

\displaystyle\kappa_{2}:=\sup_{\mathfrak{R}}\Big\{\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\|\mathfrak{a}\|\|\nabla\rho\|\Big\},

(4.50)

are finite, and, putting

\displaystyle a:=2\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\rho_{O},

(4.51)

\displaystyle Q_{a}^{S}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}):=Q_{0}^{S}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})+a(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(4.52)

we see

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}\leq Q_{a}^{S}[\bm{u}]\leq
