\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+2a\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

(4.53)

Therefore
\mathcal{L}^{S}
is symmetric and bounded from below, namely,
for
\bm{u}\in C_{0}^{\infty}
,

(\mathcal{L}^{S}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}=Q_{a}^{S}[\bm{u}]-a\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\geq-a\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

The Stone-Friedrichs therem says that
\mathcal{L}^{S}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}
admits the self-adjoint extension
\bm{L}^{S}
defined on

\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}^{S})=\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}|\mathcal{L}^{S}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}\quad\mbox{in distribution sense}\}
. Let us put

\bm{L}:=\bm{L}^{S}+\bm{N},

(4.54)

where
\bm{N}
is the bounded operator on
\mathfrak{H}
,
\bm{N}\bm{u}=\mathcal{N}\bm{u}
, namely,

\|\bm{N}\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq\kappa_{2}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}},

(4.55)

so that

\mathcal{L}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}

\Leftrightarrow

\mathcal{L}^{S}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
. Hence
\bm{L}
is a closed extension of
\mathcal{L}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}

with domain

\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})=\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}^{S})=\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}|\mathcal{L}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}\},

and its adjoint operator
\bm{L}^{*}
turns out to be

\bm{L}^{*}=(\bm{L}^{S})^{*}+\bm{N}^{*}=\bm{L}^{S}-\bm{N}.

In fact we can claim that
(\bm{T}+\bm{A})^{*}=\bm{T}^{*}+\bm{A}^{*}
when
\bm{A}

is a bounded linear operator defined on the whole space.

Note that

(\bm{L}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}-(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{L}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}=-2(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{N}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(4.56)

therefore

|(\bm{L}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}-(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{L}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}|\leq 2\kappa_{2}\|\bm{u}_{1}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\bm{u}_{2}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(4.57)

Then we define

(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}=Q_{a}^{S}(\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2})+(\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

(4.58)

and

\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}=\sqrt{(\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}}

(4.59)

Then
\|\cdot\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}
is a equivalent norm to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{G}}
and
it hold that

\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}\geq\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G},

(4.60)

and

\mathfrak{Re}\Big[(\bm{u}|\bm{v})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}\Big]=\mathfrak{Re}\Big[((\bm{L}+a+1)\bm{u}|\bm{v})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big]\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{v}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}.

(4.61)

Anyway we can claim

Proposition 3

Let
\lambda>a
. Then the equation

(\bm{L}+\lambda)\bm{u}=\bm{f},\qquad\bm{f}\in\mathfrak{H}

(4.62)

admits a unique solution
\bm{u}
in
\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
and the solution enjoys

\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda-a}\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(4.63)

Proof. We consider the operator
\bm{L}+\lambda
defined on
\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
. If
\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
satisfies (
4.62
), then

\mathfrak{Re}[((\bm{L}+\lambda)\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}]=Q_{a}^{S}[\bm{u}]+(\lambda-a)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\geq(\lambda-a)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

since

\mathfrak{Re}[(\bm{N}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}]=0
, and

\mathfrak{Re}[((\bm{L}+\lambda)\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}]\leq\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}},

therefore

\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda-a}\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}.

Hence
\bm{L}+\lambda
is one-to-one. The range

\mathsf{R}(\bm{L}+\lambda)=\mathsf{D}((\bm{L}+\lambda)^{-1})
is
dense in
\mathfrak{H}
. In fact, if

\bm{h}\in\mathfrak{H}
satisfies

((\bm{L}+\lambda)\bm{u}|\bm{h})_{\mathfrak{H}}=0\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),

then
\bm{h}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}^{*})
and

(\bm{u}|(\bm{L}^{*}+\lambda)\bm{h})_{\mathfrak{H}}=0\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}).

Since
\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\supset C_{0}^{\infty}
is dense in
\mathfrak{H}
,
we have
(\bm{L}^{*}+\lambda)\bm{h}=0
. Then

0=\mathfrak{Re}[(\bm{L}^{*}+\lambda)\bm{h}|\bm{h})_{\mathfrak{H}}]\geq(\lambda-a)\|\bm{h}|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

therefore
\bm{h}=\bm{0}
.
Since
\bm{L}
is closed, we can claim that
(\bm{L}+\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
and the estimate
(
4.63
) holds.
\square

Then we can apply the Hille-Yosida theory in the version described in
Appendix C
.
The conclusion is

Theorem 5

Suppose
\bm{u}^{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{v}^{0}\in\mathfrak{G}_{0}
and

\bm{f}\in C([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H})
. Then the initial value problem
(
4.1a
)(
4.1b
) admits a unique solution
\bm{u}=\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})

in

C^{2}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{G}_{0})\cap C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})),

and the energy

\displaystyle E(t,\bm{u})

\displaystyle:=\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{u}||_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+Q_{a}^{S}[\bm{u}]+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}=(1+a)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+Q_{0}^{S}[\bm{u}]+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

(4.64)

enjoys the estimate

\sqrt{E(t,\bm{u})}\leq e^{\kappa t}\sqrt{E(0,\bm{u})}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\kappa(t-s)}\|\bm{f}(s)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}ds

(4.65)

for

\kappa=\frac{5}{4}+\frac{a+\kappa_{2}}{2}=\frac{5}{4}+\kappa_{1}+\kappa_{2}+2\pi\mathsf{G}\rho_{O}.

5
Eigenvalue problem –Possibility of eigenmode expansions

We are considering the equation
ELASO
:

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{u}}{\partial t^{2}}+\bm{B}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}+\bm{L}\bm{u}=0.

(5.1)

Since

\displaystyle\bm{B}\bm{v}=2\Omega\begin{bmatrix}0&-1&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}\bm{v}
,

\bm{B}
is a skew-symmetric bounded linear operator in
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
,

\bm{B}^{*}=-\bm{B}
, and

\mathfrak{Re}[(\bm{B}\bm{v}|\bm{v})]=0\quad\forall\bm{v}
. Put

\beta^{\star}:=|\|\bm{B}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}=\sup\frac{\|\bm{B}\bm{v}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\|\bm{v}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}=2|\Omega|.

(5.2)

\bm{L}
is a self-adjoint operator in
\mathfrak{H}

and

((\bm{L}+a)\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\geq 0\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}).

Put

\mu_{*}:=\Big(-\inf_{\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})}\frac{(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}\Big)\vee 0.

(5.3)

Then

(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\geq-\mu_{*}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),

(5.4)

and

\mu_{*}\leq a
.

Now we look for nontrivial solution of (
5.1
) of the form

\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})=e^{\lambda t}\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})
, where
\lambda\in\mathbb{C},\bm{u}^{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
. Namely

Definition 6

If there is a
\bm{u}^{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
such that
\bm{u}^{0}\not=\bm{0}
, and

\Big[\lambda^{2}+\lambda\bm{B}+\bm{L}\Big]\bm{u}^{0}=\bm{0}

(5.5)

holds, then
\lambda
is called an eigenvalue of
ELASO
(
5.1
) and
\bm{u}^{0}
is called an eigenvector associated with the eigenvalue
\lambda
.

If
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
ELASO
(
5.1
) and
\bm{u}^{0}
is an associated eigenvector such that
\|\bm{u}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
, then

1)

\bm{u}:(t,\bm{x})\mapsto e^{\lambda t}\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})
is a solution of (
5.1
)
with the initial condition

\bm{u}|_{t=0}=\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x}),\quad\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}=\lambda\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x}),

(5.6)

2)
\bm{u}^{\mathfrak{Re}}:(t,\bm{x})\mapsto\mathfrak{Re}[e^{\lambda t}\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})]

is a solution of (
5.1
) with the initial conditon

\bm{u}^{\mathfrak{Re}}|_{t=0}=\mathfrak{Re}[\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})],\quad\frac{\partial\bm{u}^{\mathfrak{Re}}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}=\mathfrak{Re}[\lambda]\mathfrak{Re}[\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})]-\mathfrak{Im}[\lambda]\mathfrak{Im}[\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})],

(5.7)

3)
\lambda
satisfies the quadratic equation

\lambda^{2}+\mathrm{i}b\lambda+c=0

(5.8)

where the coefficents
b,c
are given by

b=-\mathrm{i}(B\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{u}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}},\quad c=(\bm{L}\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{u}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(5.9)

and
b,c
are real numbers.

By Theorem
4
we see that

0
is an eigenvalue of
ELASO
(
5.1
).
The solution
\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})
with
\bm{u}^{0}\in\mathsf{N}(\bm{L})
may be called a ‘trivial perturbation’ after
[
8
]
.

Recall that by the operator
\bm{A}
in
\mathfrak{E}=\mathsf{G}_{0}\times\mathfrak{H}
defined by

\bm{A}U=\begin{bmatrix}O&-I\\
\\
\bm{L}&\bm{B}\end{bmatrix}U,\quad\mathsf{D}(\bm{A})=\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\times\mathsf{G}_{0},

(5.10)

the equation (
5.1
) reads
ELASO‡
:

\frac{dU}{dt}+\bm{A}U=\bm{0}

(5.11)

with

U=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}\\
\\
\displaystyle\frac{d\bm{u}}{dt}\end{bmatrix}.

Proposition 4

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of
ELASO
(
5.1
) if and only if

\lambda
is an eigenvalue of the operator
-\bm{A}
.

In fact, if
\bm{u}^{0}
is an eigenvector of
ELASO
(
5.1
) associated with eigenvector
\lambda
, then

U^{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}^{0}\\
\\
\lambda\bm{u}^{0}\end{bmatrix}

is an eigenvector of
\bm{A}
with eigenvalue
-\lambda
; if

U^{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}^{0}\\
\\
\bm{v}^{0}\end{bmatrix}(\not=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}\\
\\
\bm{0}\end{bmatrix})

is an eigenvector of
\bm{A}
with eigenvalue
-\lambda
, then

(\lambda^{2}+\lambda\bm{B}+\bm{L})\bm{u}^{0}=\bm{0},\bm{u}^{0}\not=\bm{0}
,
since
\bm{v}^{0}=\lambda\bm{u}^{0}
, that is,
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
ELASO
(
5.1
).

Following
[
7
]
, we use the terminology defined by

Definition 7

The family of operators
\mathfrak{L}=(\mathfrak{L}(\lambda))_{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}}
,
where

\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)=\lambda^{2}+\lambda\bm{B}+\bm{L},

(5.12)

is called the ‘quadratic pencil’ of the equation (
5.1
).

The resolvent set
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is the set of

\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)
has the bounded incverse in
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
.

\mathbb{C}\setminus\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is the spectrum of
\mathfrak{L}
, and is denoted by
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
.

The set of all eigenvalues of
ELASO
(
5.1
) is denoted by
\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})
.

If
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of (
5.1
), then it belongs to the spectrum of
\mathfrak{L}
, that is,

\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})\subset\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
. However we cannot say that any
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is an eigenvalue of (
5.1
),
that is,
\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
, a priori.
We are going to study the structure of
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})

as much as possible at the moment.

Proposition 5

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is an open subset of
\mathbb{C}
and
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is closed .

Proof. Let us consider
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
. Then

\mathfrak{L}(\lambda+\Delta\lambda)=\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)\Big[I+\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}(\Delta\lambda(2\lambda+\bm{B}+\Delta\lambda)\Big]

admits the bounded inverse and
\lambda+\Delta\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
, if

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\Delta\lambda(2\lambda+\bm{B}+\Delta\lambda)\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}<1.

For this inequality, it is sufficient that

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\cdot|\Delta\lambda|\cdot|(2|\lambda|+\beta^{\star}+|\Delta\lambda|)<1,

or

|\Delta\lambda|<-\Big(|\lambda|+\frac{\beta^{\star}}{2}\Big)+\sqrt{\Big(|\lambda|+\frac{\beta^{\star}}{2}\Big)^{2}+\frac{1}{|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}}}.

This means
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is open.

\square

Proposition 6

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
and
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
are symmetric about the imaginary axis
\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
.

In fact we see
\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{*}=\mathfrak{L}(-\lambda^{*})
.

Proposition 7

It hold

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}(-\bm{A}),\quad\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}(-\bm{A}),\quad\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})\subset\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(-\bm{A}).

Here
\mbox{}(-\bm{A}),\mbox{}(-\bm{A}),\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(-\bm{A})

stand for the usual resolvent set, spectrum, point spectrum (the set of all eigenvalues) of the operator

-\bm{A}
in
\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{H}
.

Proof. Let
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
. Consider the equation

\mathbf{A}U+\lambda U=F=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{g}\\
\\
\bm{f}\end{bmatrix}\in\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{H},

or,

\begin{cases}&-\bm{v}+\lambda\bm{u}=\bm{g}\\
&\\
&\bm{L}\bm{u}+\bm{B}\bm{u}+\lambda\bm{v}=\bm{f}\end{cases}

This system of equations can be solved
as

\begin{cases}\bm{u}&=\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}(\bm{f}+\bm{B}\bm{g}-\lambda\bm{g})\\
&\\
\bm{v}&=\lambda\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}(\bm{f}+\bm{B}\bm{g}-\lambda\bm{g})-\bm{f}\end{cases}

since
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})

, while

F\mapsto U
is continuous. Here we note that
\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\mathfrak{H})
belongs to
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}})
thanks to the following Proposition
8
.
Therefore
\lambda\in\mbox{}(-\mathbf{A})
.

Inversely let
\lambda\in\mbox{}(-\mathbf{A})
. Consider the equation

(\lambda^{2}+\lambda\bm{B}+\bm{L})\bm{u}=\bm{f}\in\mathfrak{H},

which is equivalent to the system of equations

\begin{cases}&\lambda\bm{v}+\bm{B}\bm{v}+\bm{L}\bm{u}=\bm{f},\\
&\\
&\bm{v}=\lambda\bm{u}\end{cases}

But this is nothing but

\mathbf{A}U+\Lambda U=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\\
\bm{f}\end{bmatrix}

for
U=(\bm{u},\bm{v})^{\top}
.
Since
\lambda\in\mbox{}(-\mathbf{A})
is supposed, this admits the solution

U=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}\\
\\
\bm{v}\end{bmatrix}=(\mathbf{A}+\lambda)^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{0}\\
\\
\bm{f}\end{bmatrix},

and
\bm{f}\mapsto\bm{u}
is continuous, that is,

\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
.
\square

Proposition 8

\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
, that is,
\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
if and only if

\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}})
.

Proof. Suppose
\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
. We consider

\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)\bm{u}=(\lambda^{2}+\lambda\bm{B}+\bm{L})\bm{u}=\bm{f}\in\mathfrak{H},\quad\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}).

Then

\lambda^{2}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\lambda(\bm{B}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{f}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}},

while

\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}=(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(1+a)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

Therefore
we have

\displaystyle\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}+(|1+a-\lambda^{2}|+|\lambda|\beta^{\star})\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big(|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}+|1+a-\lambda^{2}|+|\lambda|\beta^{\star}\Big)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

so that

\displaystyle\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}

\displaystyle\leq\Big[|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}+|1+a-\lambda^{2}|+|\lambda|\beta^{\star}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle\leq|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\Big[|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}+|1+a-\lambda^{2}|+|\lambda|\beta^{\star}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathfrak{H}},

Consequently we see
\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}})
and

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}|\|_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}})}\leq|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\Big[|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}+|1+a-\lambda^{2}|+|\lambda|\beta^{\star}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Suppose
\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}})
.
We consider

\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)\bm{u}=(\lambda^{2}+\lambda\bm{B}+\bm{L})\bm{u}=\bm{f}\in\mathfrak{H},\quad\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}).

We have

\displaystyle\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\bm{u}|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}-(\bm{L}+a)\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Big[{C}|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}})}\|\bm{f}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\Big]^{2}.

Consequently
\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
and

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\leq|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H},\breve{\mathfrak{G}})}.

\square
.

We consider the question:

Question 1

Is
\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
?

in the following view point.

Let us consider the case of
\bm{B}=O
, or
\Omega=0
. Then, we see that, if

\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\bm{L})=\mbox{}(\bm{L})
,
there is an orthonormal system
(\bm{\phi}_{n})_{n}
which consists of eigenvectors of

\bm{L}
, say,
\bm{L}\bm{\phi}_{n}=\lambda_{n}\bm{\phi}_{n},\lambda_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
, and is complete in
\mathfrak{H}
, according to

Theorem
(
[
6
, Theorem X.3.4]
):
If the spectrum of the normal operator
T
in the Hilbert space
\mathsf{X}
is countable, then there is an orthonormal basis
\mathscr{B}
for
\mathsf{X}
consisting of eigenvectors of
T
;

x=\sum_{y\in\mathscr{B}}(x|y)_{\mathsf{X}}y\quad\forall x\in\mathsf{X},

and, for each
x
, all but a countable number of the coefficients
(x|y)_{\mathsf{X}}
are zero.

Namely, if
\Omega=0
and if

\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\bm{L})=\mbox{}(\bm{L})
is the case, then
all solutions
\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})
of (
5.1
)
in
C([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{G}_{0})\cap C^{2}([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}))

can be expanded by
the eigenmodes

\bm{u}_{n}^{\pm}(t,\bm{x})=\begin{cases}e^{\pm\sqrt{\lambda_{n}}\mathrm{i}t}\bm{\phi}_{n}(\bm{x})\quad(\lambda_{n}\geq 0)\\
e^{\pm\sqrt{|\lambda_{n}|}t}\bm{\phi}_{n}(\bm{x})\quad(\lambda_{n}<0)\end{cases}

as

\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})=\sum_{n}(c_{n}^{+}\bm{u}_{n}^{+}(t,\bm{x})+c_{n}^{-}\bm{u}_{n}^{-}(t,\bm{x})),

where

c_{n}^{\pm}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\Big((\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{\phi}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{n}}\mathrm{i}}(\bm{v}^{0}|\bm{\phi}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big)\quad(\lambda_{n}>0)\\
\frac{1}{2}\Big((\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{\phi}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\lambda_{n}|}}(\bm{v}^{0}|\bm{\phi}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big)\quad(\lambda_{n}<0)\\
\frac{1}{2}(\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{\phi}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad(\lambda_{n}=0),\end{cases}

with
\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x})=\bm{u}(0,\bm{x}),\bm{v}^{0}(\bm{x})=\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}(0,\bm{x})
.

But, if
\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\bm{L})=\mbox{}(\bm{L})
is not the case, we cannot claim the completeness of eigenmodes, the possibility of
the eigenmode expansion. In this sense we we are concerned with whether
\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
or not.

Ler us note the following observations:

1)

Suppose that
\Omega\not=0,\bm{B}\not=O
. Then, even if
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L})
, we know neither whether
\mbox{}(\bm{L})=\mbox{}_{p}(\bm{L})

nor whether eigenmode expansion is available or not. Actually, for an eigenvalue
\mu\not=0
of
\bm{L}
with eigenvector
\bm{\phi}
such that

\overset{=}{\bm{\phi}}=\begin{bmatrix}\phi^{1}\\
\phi^{2}\\
0\end{bmatrix}\not=\bm{0}
, we have
\mathfrak{L}(\pm\sqrt{-\mu})\bm{\phi}=\pm\sqrt{-\mu}\bm{B}\bm{\phi}\not=\bm{0}
. The relation between

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
and
\mbox{}(\bm{L})
, and between

\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L})
and
\mbox{}_{p}(\bm{L})
are not clear.

2)

Supoose

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L})
. Then, although
\mbox{}(\bm{A})=\mbox{}_{p}(\bm{A})
, we cannot claim that there is a complete orthonormal basis in
\mathfrak{E}=\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}\times\mathfrak{H}
which consistes of eigenvectors of
\bm{A}
, since
\bm{A}
is not normal. In fact we have

Proposition 9

The adjoint of
\bm{A}
turns out to be

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(\bm{A}^{*})=\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\times\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}},

(5.13)

\displaystyle\bm{A}^{*}=\begin{bmatrix}O&I-(a+1)\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\\
\\
-\bm{L}_{a}&-B\end{bmatrix},

(5.14)

where
\bm{L}_{a}=\bm{L}+a+1
.

Proof. Recall that

\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}=((\bm{L}+a+1)\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}
for
\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
,
a\geq 0

and that

\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}
belongs to
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
and is self-adjoint.

For
\bm{F}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{g}\\
\bm{f}\end{bmatrix},\bm{F}^{{\dagger}}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{g}^{{\dagger}}\\
\bm{f}^{{\dagger}}\end{bmatrix}
,
“
\bm{F}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{A}^{*}),\bm{A}^{*}\bm{F}=\bm{F}^{{\dagger}}

“
means

(\bm{A}U|\bm{F})_{\mathfrak{E}}=(U|\bm{F}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{E}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall U\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{A})

(5.15)

or

\displaystyle-(\bm{v}|\bm{g})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}+(\bm{L}\bm{u}+\bm{B}\bm{v}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{u}|\bm{g}^{{\dagger}})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}+(\bm{v}|\bm{f}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{A})\forall\bm{v}\in\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}.

(5.16)

We want to show that (
5.16
) holds if and only if

\bm{g}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{f}\in\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}
and

\bm{g}^{{\dagger}}=\bm{f}-(a+1)\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{f},\quad\bm{f}^{{\dagger}}=-\bm{L}_{a}\bm{g}-\bm{B}\bm{f}.

(5.17)

Suppose (
5.16
). Taking
\bm{u}=\bm{0}
, we see

-(\bm{L}_{a}\bm{v}|\bm{g})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(\bm{B}\bm{v}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{v}|\bm{f}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\forall\bm{v}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}).

Consider
\bm{h}=\bm{L}_{a}\bm{v}
, while
\forall\bm{v}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\Leftrightarrow\forall\bm{h}\in\mathfrak{H}
, we have

-(\bm{h}|\bm{g})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(\bm{B}\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{h}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{h}|\bm{f}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\forall\bm{h}\in\mathfrak{H}.

Therefore

-\bm{g}-\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{B}f=\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{f}^{{\dagger}}.

Here we know that
\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
is self-adjoint. Hence

\bm{f}^{{\dagger}}=-\bm{L}_{a}\bm{g}-\bm{B}\bm{f}.

So (
5.16
) reduces
to

(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{u}|\bm{g}^{{\dagger}})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}).

Recall
\bm{B}^{*}=-\bm{B}
. Consider
\bm{k}=\bm{L}_{a}\bm{u}
, while
\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\Leftrightarrow\bm{k}\in\mathfrak{H}
. Then

(\bm{L}\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{k}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{k}|\bm{g}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathfrak{H}.

Since
\bm{L}\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}=I-(a+1)\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}
, this reads

(\bm{k}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}-(a+1)(\bm{k}|\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}=(\bm{k}|\bm{g}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\forall\bm{k}\in\mathfrak{H},

so that

\bm{f}-(a+1)\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{f}=\bm{g}^{{\dagger}}.

Since
\bm{g}^{{\dagger}}\in\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}
, we see
\bm{f}=\bm{g}^{{\dagger}}+(a+1)\bm{L}_{a}^{-1}\bm{f}
should belong to
\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}
. Hence
\bm{g}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{f}\in\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}
and (
5.17
) should holds.

Inversely suppose that
\bm{g}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{f}\in\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}
and (
5.17
) holds. Then
we see, for
\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{v}\in\breve{\mathfrak{G}_{0}}
,

\displaystyle-(\bm{v}|\bm{g})_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}+(\bm{L}\bm{u}+\bm{B}\bm{v}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=-(\bm{v}|\bm{L}_{a}\bm{g})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(\bm{L}\bm{u}+\bm{B}\bm{v}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{v}|-\bm{L}_{a}\bm{f}-\bm{B}\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{f})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{v}|\bm{f}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(\bm{L}_{a}\bm{u}|\bm{g}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=(\bm{v}|\bm{f}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}+(\bm{u}|\bm{g}^{{\dagger}})_{\mathfrak{H}}.

That is, (
5.16
) holds.
\square

3)

Suppose
\Omega=0,\bm{B}=O
.

\phi

are two different eigenvectors of

(\Phi_{+}|\Phi_{-})_{\mathfrak{E}}=\|\phi\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}-\mu\|\phi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}=(a+1)\|\phi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\not=0,

that is, they are not orthogonal.

• Here we note the following fact:

Theorem 6

Suppose that the background is isentropic, that is,
\mathfrak{a}_{b}=0
everywhere.
Let
\bm{L}^{G}
be te Friedrichs extension of the operator
\mathcal{L}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b},;\mathbb{C}^{3})
in the functional space

\mathfrak{G}=\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}|\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}_{b},\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C})\}
. Then

\mbox{}(\bm{L}^{G})=\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\bm{L}^{G})
, and

\mbox{}(\bm{L}^{G})
consists of
\{0\}
and a sequence of eigenvalues of finite multiplicities which accumates
+\infty
.

The proof is the same as that of
[
15
, Sections III, IV ]
for the case of spherically symmetric background for
\Omega=\omega_{b}=0
, but we describe a sketch of proof for the sake of self-containedness. First of all we note that, since
\mathfrak{a}_{b}=0
, it is the case

\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=\mathrm{grad}\Big(-\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})+4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})]\Big).

Let us set the Hilbert spaces
\hat{\mathfrak{H}},\hat{\mathfrak{G}}
,

\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g}^{1},\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{1}
defined by

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=\rho\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}\Big(\mathfrak{R},\frac{1}{\rho}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|_{\hat{\mathfrak{H}}}^{2}=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|^{2}\frac{1}{\rho}d\bm{x},

(5.18)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathfrak{G}}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\hat{\bm{u}}\in\hat{\mathfrak{H}}\quad\Big|\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{u}}\in L^{2}\Big(\mathfrak{R},\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}\Big)\Big\},

\displaystyle\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|_{\hat{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}=\|\hat{\bm{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\frac{1}{\rho})}^{2}+\|\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{u}}\|_{L^{2}(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}})}^{2},

(5.19)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle=L^{2}\Big(\mathfrak{R},\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}\Big),

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathfrak{g}}^{2}=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}|g|^{2}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x},

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{1}

\displaystyle=\Big\{g\in L^{2}\Big(\mathfrak{R},\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}\Big)\quad\Big|

\displaystyle\mathrm{grad}\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}g\Big)\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{R},\rho d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}^{3}),\int_{\mathfrak{R}}gd\bm{x}=0\Big\},

\displaystyle\|g\|_{\mathfrak{g}^{1}}^{2}=\|g\|_{L^{2}(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}})}^{2}+\Big\|\mathrm{grad}\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}g\Big)\Big\|_{L^{2}(\rho)}^{2},

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{1}

\displaystyle=\mbox{the closure of}\quad C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{C})\quad\mbox{in}\quad\mathfrak{g}^{1}.

(5.22)

The operators
\hat{\bm{L}}
in
\hat{\mathfrak{G}}
,
\hat{\bm{M}}
from
\mathsf{D}(\hat{\bm{M}}(\subset\mathfrak{g})
into
\hat{\mathfrak{G}}
, and
\bm{N}
in
\mathfrak{g}
are defined by

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(\hat{\bm{L}}

\displaystyle=\{\hat{\bm{u}}\in\hat{\mathfrak{G}}_{0}\quad\Big|\hat{\mathcal{L}}\hat{\bm{u}}\in\hat{\mathfrak{G}}\}

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{L}}

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{L}}\hat{\bm{u}}=\rho\mathcal{L}\Big(\frac{\hat{\bm{u}}}{\rho}\Big),

(5.23)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(\hat{\bm{M}})

\displaystyle=\{g\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{1}\quad\Big|\hat{\mathcal{M}}g\in\hat{\mathfrak{G}}\}

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{M}}g

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathcal{M}}g=\rho\mathrm{grad}\Big(-\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}g+4\pi\mathsf{G}[g]\Big),

(5.24)

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(\bm{N})

\displaystyle=\{g\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{1}\quad\Big|\mathcal{N}g\in\mathfrak{g}\}

\displaystyle\bm{N}g

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}g=\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{M}}g

\displaystyle=\mathrm{div}\Big(\rho\mathrm{grad}\Big(-\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}g+4\pi\mathsf{G}[g]\Big),.

(5.25)

Then

\bm{N}g=\mathrm{div}(\hat{\bm{M}}g)\quad\mbox{for}\quad g\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{N})

and

\bm{L}\bm{u}=\frac{1}{\rho}\hat{\bm{M}}\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{u}}=\frac{1}{\rho}\hat{\bm{L}}(\rho\bm{u})\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{u}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{R}^{3}).

It follows that
\bm{N}
is a self-adjoint operator bounded from below in
\mathfrak{g}
. In fact,
\mathcal{N}
is symmetric and we have

(\mathcal{N}g|g)_{\mathfrak{g}}\geq(1-\tau)\|g\|_{\mathfrak{g}^{1}}^{2}-C_{\tau}\|g\|_{\mathfrak{g}}^{2},\quad\forall g\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R};\mathbb{C}),

where

0<\tau<1
,

C_{\tau}=\frac{1}{4\tau}(4\pi\mathsf{G}\kappa)^{2}\frac{\rho_{O}}{\nu}+\tau

with

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle=\sup\Big\{\|\mathcal{K}[g]\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R})}\quad\Big|\quad\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}=1\Big\}<+\infty,

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}>0.

On the other hand, the imbedding of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}^{1}
into
\mathfrak{g}
is compact. (See
[
15
, Proposition 4]
.) Consequently the resovent
(\lambda-\bm{N})^{-1},\lambda>C_{\tau},
is a compact operator in
\mathfrak{g}
. Hence

The spectrum
\mbox{}(\bm{N})
consists of a sequence of eigenvalues
\lambda_{n}
,
-C_{\tau}<\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{n}<\lambda_{n+1}<\cdots\rightarrow+\infty
,
of finite multiplicities.

We can claim

If
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\bm{N}),\lambda\not=0
, then

\lambda\in\mbox{}(\hat{\bm{L}})
.

Proof. In fact the resolvent
(\lambda-\hat{\bm{L}})^{-1}
is given by

(\lambda-\hat{\bm{L}})^{-1}\hat{\bm{f}}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\Big(\hat{\bm{f}}+\hat{\bm{M}}(\lambda-\bm{N})^{-1}\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{f}}\Big)

for
\hat{\bm{f}}\in\hat{\mathfrak{G}}
. Here
\hat{\bm{M}}(\lambda-\bm{N})^{-1}
turns out to be a bounded operator from
\mathfrak{g}
into
\hat{\mathfrak{G}}
.
\square

\hat{\bm{L}}
is a self-adjoint operator in
\hat{\mathfrak{G}}
.

Proof. Since it can be shown that
\hat{\bm{L}}
is symmetric and closable,
[
16
, Th. 3.16]
can be applied.
\square
.

If
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\bm{N}),\lambda\not=0
, then
\lambda
is an eigenvalue of

\hat{\bm{L}}
of finite multiplicity.

Proof. Let
\bm{N}g=\lambda g,\|g\|_{\mathfrak{g}}=1
. Then

\hat{\bm{u}}:=\frac{1}{\lambda}\hat{\bm{M}}g\in\hat{\mathfrak{G}}
, therefore

\lambda\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{u}}=\bm{N}g=\lambda g
. Since
\lambda\not=0
, we have
\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{u}}=g
, therefore,
\lambda\hat{\bm{u}}=\hat{\bm{M}}g=\hat{\bm{M}}\mathrm{div}\hat{\bm{u}}=\hat{\bm{L}}\hat{\bm{u}}
. Since

\hat{\bm{u}}\in\mathsf{N}(\lambda-\hat{\bm{L}})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\hat{\bm{u}}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\hat{\bm{M}}g\quad\mbox{for}\quad\exists g\in\mathsf{N}(\lambda-\bm{N}),

we have

\mathrm{dim}.\mathsf{N}(\lambda-\hat{\bm{L}})\leq\mathrm{dim}.\mathsf{N}(\lambda-\bm{N}).

\square
.

Summing up, we can claim

The spectrum of the self-adjoint operator
\hat{\bm{L}}
coincides with

\{0\}\cup\mbox{}(\bm{N})
and

\mathrm{dim}\mathsf{N}(\lambda-\hat{\bm{L}})<\infty
for
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\hat{\bm{L}})\setminus\{0\}
.

This completes the proof of Theorem
6
.

It is obvious that
\mbox{}_{p}(\bm{L})=\mbox{}_{p}(\bm{L}^{G})
, that is,
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{L}
if and only if

\lambda
is an eigenvalue of
\bm{L}^{G}
. However the relation between

\mbox{}(\bm{L})
and
\mbox{}(\bm{L}^{G})
is not clear. Namely is

\mbox{}(\bm{L})\subset\mbox{}(\bm{L}^{G})
the case? In other words, if
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\bm{L}^{G})
, then
\lambda\not\in\mbox{}_{p}(\bm{L})

\Big(=\mbox{}_{p}(\bm{L}^{G})\Big)
and so
(\lambda-\bm{L})^{-1}
exists as an operator in
\mathfrak{H}
; Although
(\lambda-\bm{L}^{G})^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{G})
, we do not know whether this
(\lambda-\bm{L})^{-1}\Big(\supset(\lambda-\bm{L}^{G})^{-1}\Big)
is bounded in
\mathfrak{H}
or not. This is the
Question

1
.

• When

,

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})=\Big\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ -\lambda^{2}\in\mbox{}(\bm{L})\Big\},

where
\mbox{}(\bm{L})

.

Generaly speaking, at least

Proposition 10

It holds

]-\infty,-\sqrt{\mu_{*}}[\quad\cup\quad]\sqrt{\mu_{*}},+\infty[\quad\subset\quad\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}(-\bm{A}).

(5.26)

For proof see the proof of Propositions

of
Appendix A
.

Proposition 11

For
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
, we have

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\geq\frac{1}{d(2|\lambda|+\beta^{\star}+d)}

(5.27)

where

d:=\mathrm{dist.}(\lambda,\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L}))
.

Proof. Let
\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
. Then, for
\Delta\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, the operator

\mathfrak{L}(\lambda+\Delta\lambda)=\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)\Big[I+\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\Delta\lambda(2\lambda+\bm{B}+\Delta\lambda)\Big]

admits the bounded inverse in
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
and
\lambda+\Delta\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
, if

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\Delta\lambda(2\lambda+\bm{B}+\Delta\lambda)\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}<1.

For this inequality, it is sufficient that

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\cdot|\Delta\lambda|\cdot(2|\lambda|+\beta^{\star}+|\Delta\lambda|)<1.

In other words, if
\lambda+\Delta\lambda\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
, then it should hold

|\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\cdot|\Delta\lambda|\cdot(2|\lambda|+\beta^{\star}+|\Delta\lambda|)\geq 1.

If
d<+\infty
, then there is a sequence
\lambda+(\Delta\lambda)_{n}\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})

such that
|(\Delta\lambda)_{n}|\rightarrow d
, and the assertion follows.
\square

Theorem 7

(
[
7
, Theorem 1]
)

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is a subset of

S:=\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\cup\Big\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\ \Big|\ |\lambda|\leq\sqrt{\mu_{*}},\quad|\mathfrak{Im}[\lambda]|\leq\frac{\beta^{\star}}{2}\Big\}.

(5.28)

Proof. First we consider
\lambda_{\infty}\in\partial\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
.

Let us take a sequance
(\lambda_{n})_{n}
such that
\lambda_{n}\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
and

\lambda_{n}\rightarrow\lambda_{\infty}
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. By Proposition
11

we have
|\|(\mathfrak{L}(\lambda_{n})^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})}\rightarrow+\infty
, therefore
there are
\bm{f}_{n}\in\mathfrak{H}
such that
\|\bm{f}_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
and
\|\mathfrak{L}(\lambda_{n})^{-1}\bm{f}_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\rightarrow+\infty
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Put
\bm{u}_{n}=\mathfrak{L}(\lambda_{n})^{-1}\bm{f}_{n}(\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}))
and
\bm{u}_{n}=\bm{u}_{n}/\|\bm{u}_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}
. Then
\|\bm{u}_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
and

\Big|(\mathfrak{L}(\lambda_{n})\bm{u}_{n}|\bm{u}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big|=\Big|\frac{1}{\|\bm{u}_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}(\bm{f}_{n}|\bm{u}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big|\leq\frac{1}{\|\bm{u}_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}}\rightarrow 0.

But we see

(\mathfrak{L}(\lambda_{n})\bm{u}_{n}|\bm{u}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}=\lambda_{n}^{2}+\mathrm{i}\lambda_{n}b_{n}+c_{n},

where

b_{n}:=-\mathrm{i}(\bm{B}\bm{u}_{n}|\bm{u}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}},\quad c_{n}=(\bm{L}\bm{u}_{n}|\bm{u}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}.

Here
b_{n},c_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and

|b_{n}|\leq\beta^{\star},c_{n}\geq-\mu_{*}
.
Hence, by taking a subsequence if necessary, we can suppose that
b_{n}
tends to a limit
b_{\infty}
such that
b_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R},|b_{\infty}|\leq\beta^{\star}
. Put

c_{\infty}:=-\lambda_{\infty}^{2}-\mathrm{i}\lambda_{\infty}b_{\infty}
. Then we see

c_{n}\rightarrow c_{\infty}
. Hence
c_{\infty}\in\mathbb{R}
and
c_{\infty}\geq-\mu_{*}
,
and
\lambda_{\infty}
turns out to enjoy the quadratic equation

\lambda_{\infty}^{2}+\mathrm{i}b_{\infty}\lambda_{\infty}+c_{\infty}=0.

Consequently,

\lambda_{\infty}=\mathrm{i}\Big[-\frac{b_{\infty}}{2}\pm\sqrt{\frac{b_{\infty}^{2}}{4}+c_{\infty}}\Big].

If
\displaystyle\frac{b_{\infty}^{2}}{4}+c_{\infty}\geq 0
, then
\lambda_{\infty}\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
. If
\displaystyle\frac{b_{\infty}^{2}}{4}+c_{\infty}\leq 0
, then

|\lambda_{\infty}|^{2}=-c_{\infty}\leq\mu_{*}
and

\displaystyle\Big|\mathfrak{Im}[\lambda_{\infty}]\Big|=\Big|-\frac{b_{\infty}}{2}\Big|\leq\frac{\beta^{\star}}{2}
. Hence
\lambda_{\infty}\in S
.

Let us consider
\lambda_{0}\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
. We claim
\lambda_{0}\in S
. Suppose

\lambda_{0}\not\in S
. By the symmetricity, we suppose
\mathfrak{Re}[\lambda_{0}]>0
.
Then there would exist a curve
\Gamma:t\in[0,1]\mapsto\lambda(t)\in\mathbb{C}\setminus S
such that
\lambda(0)=\lambda_{0}
and

\lambda(1)\in]\sqrt{\mu}_{*},+\infty[
. Recall
\lambda(1)\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
by Proposition

10
. The time

\bar{t}=\sup\Big\{t\in[0,1]\quad\Big|\quad\lambda(t)\in\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})\Big\}

would enjoy

\bar{t}\in[0,1[,\lambda(\bar{t})\in\partial\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L}),\lambda(\bar{t})\not\in S
,
a contradiction to
\partial\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})\subset S
. Therefore we can claim
\lambda_{0}\in S
.

\square

We want to clarify the structure of
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
more concretely. In
[
7
, p.405]
J. Dyson and B. F. Schutz say that the following assumption about
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})
is very reasonable and provable:

Dyson-Schutz’ Assumption
:
Let
S=S_{(i)}\cup S_{(ii)}\cup S_{(iii)}
, where

\displaystyle S_{(i)}=\{\lambda\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\ |\ |\mathfrak{Im}[\lambda]|>\frac{\beta^{\star}}{2}\}

\displaystyle S_{(ii)}=\{\lambda\in S\ |\ \lambda\not\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\},

\displaystyle S_{(iii)}=\{\lambda\in\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}\ |\ |\mathfrak{Im}[\lambda]|\leq\frac{\beta^{\star}}{2}\}.

Then

(i)

\displaystyle\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})\cap S_{(i)}\subset\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L}),

(ii)

\displaystyle\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})\cap S_{(ii)}\subset\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L}),\quad\mbox{and}\quad\partial(\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L})\cap S_{(ii)})\cap(\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R})=\emptyset,

(iii)

\displaystyle\Big(\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})\cap S_{(iii)}\Big)\setminus\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L})\not=\emptyset.

However it seems that this assumption has not yet been justified mathematically.
Note that this assumption imlies that, when
\Omega=0
, it holds that

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})=\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L})
and that, when
\Omega\not=0
, it holds that
\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})\setminus\mbox{}_{p}(\mathfrak{L})\not=\emptyset
.

6
Stability problem –New concepts of stability

Let us discuss on stability of solutions of
ELASO
, supposing

Assumption
:
The equation of state enjoys
Assumption

5
and the background
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
is a barotropic admissible stationary solution.

D. Lynden-Bell and J. P. Ostriker,
[
20
, p.301, line 18]
, say:

Equation (36) shows that the system is stable if
c
is positive for each eigen
\bm{\xi}
[ read
\bm{u}^{0}
] . This assured if
\mathbf{C}
[ read
L
] is positive definite. Thus:

A sufficient condition for stability is that
\mathbf{C}
[read
L
] is positive definite. This is
the
condition for secular stability.

We should be careful to understand the meaning of the saying ‘the system is stable’ and ‘
\bm{L}
is positve definite’. As for ‘stability’
C. Hunter
[
13
]
says:

A general system is said to be ordinarily or dynamically unstable if the amplitude of some mode grows exponentially in time, but ordinarily stable if every mode is oscillatory in time. An ordinarily stable system can be said to be secularly unstable if small additional dissipative forces can cause some perturbation to grow. Otherwise, the system is said to be secularly stable.

So ‘(ordinary) stability’ means the condition:

(ST.1)

For any eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})
of
ELASO
(
5.1
) and its associated solution
\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})=e^{\lambda t}\bm{u}^{0}(\bm{x}),\bm{u}^{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),\bm{u}^{0}\not=\bm{0},\mathfrak{L}(\lambda)\bm{u}^{0}=\bm{0}
, it holds

\|{U}(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{G}\times\mathfrak{H}}\leq C\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall t\in[0,+\infty[,

(6.1)

Here and hereafter we use the notation

{U}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}\\
\\
\dot{\bm{u}}\end{bmatrix},\quad\dot{\bm{u}}=\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}.

(6.2)

Since

\|\bm{u}(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=e^{\mathfrak{Re}[\lambda]t}\|\bm{u}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}},\quad\|\dot{\bm{u}}(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=|\lambda|e^{\mathfrak{Re}[\lambda]t}\|\bm{u}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}},

the following condition is necessary and sufficient for the stability in the sense of
(ST.1)
:

(*)

For any eigenvalue
\lambda\in\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})
it hlds that
\mathfrak{Re}[\lambda]=0
, that is,

\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})\subset\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
.

On the other hand, any
\lambda\in\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\mathfrak{L})
satisfies the quadratic equation

\lambda^{2}+\mathrm{i}b\lambda+c=0

(6.3)

where the coefficents
b,c
are given by

b=-\mathrm{i}(\bm{B}\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{u}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}},\quad c=(\bm{L}\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{u}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(6.4)

\bm{u}^{0}
being an associated eigenvector such that
\|\bm{u}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
,
and
b,c
being real numbers,
therefore

\lambda=\mathrm{i}\Big[\frac{b}{2}\pm\sqrt{\frac{b^{2}}{4}+c}\Big].

(6.5)

Hence

\mathfrak{Re}[\lambda]=0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\frac{b^{2}}{4}+c\geq 0\quad\Leftarrow c\geq 0.

(6.6)

Therefore, considering the condition

(PD.1)
:
It holds

(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\geq 0\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}),

(6.7)

we can claim

Proposition 12

If
\bm{L}
is ‘positive definite’ in the sense of
(PD.1)
, then the background
(\rho,S,\bm{v})=(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
is ‘stable’ in the sense of
(ST.1)
.

Thus the saying of
[
20
]
can be interpreted as this Proposition.

Note that
(PD.1)
implies

\inf_{\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})}\frac{(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}\geq 0,

so that

\mu_{*}=\Big(\inf_{\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})}\frac{(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}\Big)\vee 0=0,

and

\mbox{}(\mathfrak{L})\subset\mathrm{i}\mathbb{R}
by Theorem
7
.

The concept of ‘stability’ in the sense of
(ST.1)
is concerned with only ‘every modes’, say, waves associated with eigenvalues. We may consider all solutions

\bm{u}
of (
5.1
) and the wider stability concept:

(ST.2)
:
For all solution
\bm{u}\in C^{2}([0,+\infty[,\mathfrak{H})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{G}_{0})\cap C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}))
of

ELASO
(
5.1
) it holds

\|{U}(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{G}\times\mathfrak{H}}\leq C\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall t\in[0,+\infty[.

(6.8)

There is a gap between
(ST.1)
and
(ST.2)
. Of course
(ST.2)

\Rightarrow

(ST.1)
, but the inverse is not obvious, since we do not have an answer to the

Question 1

Let us note that, if
\Omega=\omega_{b}=0
and the background is spherically symmetric and isentropic, then it holds that
\mbox{}(\bm{L}^{G})=\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\bm{L}^{G})
, where
\bm{L}^{G}
is the Friedrichs extention of

\mathcal{L}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}
in the Hilbert space

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}=\Big\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}\ \Big|\ \mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\in L^{2}\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x}\Big)\Big\}
. See
Theorem
6
. But, even in this non-rotating case, we do not know whether
\mbox{}(\bm{L})=\mbox{}_{\mathrm{p}}(\bm{L})
or not,
\bm{L}
being the Friedrics extension in
\mathfrak{H}
. Moreover, when
\Omega\not=0
,

\bm{B}^{G}=\bm{B}\restriction\mathfrak{G}

is not a bounded linear operator on
\mathfrak{G}
so that the application of Hille-Yosida theory for the basic existence proof in Section 4 does not work if we take
\mathfrak{G}
as the basic space instead of
\mathfrak{H}
.

Also let us note that, if
\Omega=0,\bm{B}=\bf{0}
, when
ELASO
(
5.1
) reduces

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{u}}{\partial t^{2}}+\bm{L}\bm{u}=0,

(6.9)

(PD.1)
implies that

\bm{u}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\cos(\sqrt{\sigma}t)dE(\sigma)\bm{a}+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\sin(\sqrt{\sigma}t)dE(\sigma)\bm{b}

(6.10)

solves (
6.9
) with
\bm{u}^{0}=\bm{a},\bm{v}^{0}=\sqrt{\bm{L}}\bm{b}
,
provided that
\bm{a},\bm{b}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
. Here

(E(\sigma))_{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}}
is the spectral decomposition of the self-adjoint operator
\bm{L}
,
which enjoys
E(-0)=O
thanks to
(PD.1)
. In this case we have

\|{U}\|_{\mathfrak{G}\times\mathfrak{H}}\leq C\Big(\|\bm{a}\|_{\mathsf{D}(\sqrt{\bm{L}})}+\|\bm{b}\|_{\mathsf{D}(\sqrt{\bm{L}})}\Big).

Here

\sqrt{\bm{L}}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\sqrt{\sigma}dE(\sigma)

so that
(\sqrt{\bm{L}})^{2}=\bm{L}
and

\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathsf{D}(\sqrt{\bm{L}})}=\Big[\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\bm{L}}\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

• Proposal of new concepts of stability

Now let us introduce more general concepts of stability. Namely, let

\mathfrak{N}
be a semi-norm on
\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\times\mathfrak{G}_{0}
, and consider the condition:

(ST.3)
:
For any solution
\bm{u}
of
ELASO
(
5.1
) it holds

\mathfrak{N}({U}(t,\cdot))\leq C\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall t\in[0,+\infty[.

(6.11)

Here
C
is a bounce deending on
{U}^{0}={U}(0,\cdot)=(\bm{u}^{0},\bm{v}^{0})^{\top}
.

Recall

\mathfrak{N}_{0}({U}(t,\cdot))\leq e^{\Lambda t}\mathfrak{N}_{0}({U}^{0})

for

\mathfrak{N}_{0}(U):=\|U\|_{\mathfrak{E}}=\Big[\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}}^{2}+\|\dot{\bm{u}}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad U=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{u}\\
\\
\dot{\bm{u}}\end{bmatrix}

Here
\Lambda=1+\mu_{*}\geq 1
so this does not give the stability
(ST.3)
. We are keeping in mind the following situation.

Suppose a seminorm
\mathfrak{n}
on
\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})
satisfy

(PD.2)
:
There is a positive number
{\delta}
such that

(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})\geq{\delta}\mathfrak{n}(\bm{u})^{2}\quad\forall\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}).

(6.12)

Put

\mathfrak{N}(U):=\Big[{\delta}\mathfrak{n}(\bm{u})^{2}+\|\dot{\bm{u}}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.13)

Then
(ST.3)
holds with

C=\Big[\|\bm{v}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+(\bm{L}\bm{u}^{0}|\bm{u}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

In fact, multipling (
5.1
) by
\dot{\bm{u}}
by the
\mathfrak{H}
-inner product, taking the real part, using
\mathfrak{Re}[(B\dot{\bm{u}}|\dot{\bm{u}})_{\mathfrak{H}}]=0
, we have

\frac{d}{dt}\Big[\|\dot{\bm{u}}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big]=0.

Of course
(PD.2)
implies
(PD.1)
but is much stronger concept of ‘positive definiteness’.

Now we are going to find a seminorm
\mathfrak{n}
which enjoys
(PD.2)
, under the following situation:

Situation 1

1) The equation of state is (
2.2
):
P=\rho^{\gamma}\exp(S/\mathsf{C}_{V})
;

2)
\Omega=\omega_{b}=0
and the background
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{0})
is spherically symmetric, and is isentropic, that is,
S_{b}=S_{0}
,
S_{0}
being a constant;

3)
\displaystyle\frac{4}{3}<\gamma<2
.

Then

\displaystyle\varUpsilon=\varUpsilon_{O}\theta\big(\frac{r}{\mathsf{a}};\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\Big)
and

\mathfrak{R}=\{\bm{x}|r=\|\bm{x}\|<R\}
with
R:=\mathsf{a}\xi_{1}\Big(\frac{1}{\gamma-1}\Big)
, where
\theta(\cdot,\frac{1}{\gamma-1})
is the Lane-Emden function and
\xi_{1}(\frac{1}{\gamma-1})
is its zero.
It holds

P=\mathsf{A}\rho^{\gamma},\quad\varUpsilon=\frac{\mathsf{A}\gamma}{\gamma-1}\rho^{\gamma-1},\quad\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}=\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}=\mathsf{A}\rho^{-(2-\gamma)},

and

(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\Big(\frac{d\Upsilon}{d\rho}|g|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}\Big)d\bm{x},

(6.14)

with
g=\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})
. ( Recall Remark
4
. )

Denote

\mathfrak{g}_{L}:=\Big\{g\in L^{2}\Big(\frac{d\Upsilon}{d\rho}d\bm{x}\Big)\Big|\exists\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}):g=\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\Big\},

(6.15)

and put

\Psi=-\mathcal{K}[g]

(6.16)

for
g\in\mathfrak{g}_{L}
. Then, for
g\in\mathfrak{g}_{L}
,
g\in C(\mathfrak{R}),\Psi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and the expansion with respect to
spherical harmonics

\displaystyle g(\bm{x})=\sum_{0\leq\ell,|m|\leq\ell}g_{\ell m}(r)Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi),

(6.17a)

\displaystyle\Psi(\bm{x})=\sum_{0\leq\ell,|m|\leq\ell}\Psi_{\ell m}(r)Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi)

(6.17b)

can be used.
Here

\bm{x}=(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})=(r\sin\vartheta\cos\phi,r\sin\vartheta\sin\phi,r\cos\vartheta)

and

\displaystyle Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi)=\sqrt{\frac{2\ell+1}{4\pi}\frac{(\ell-m)!}{(\ell+m)!}}P_{\ell}^{m}(\cos\vartheta)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi},

\displaystyle Y_{\ell,-m}=(-1)^{m}Y_{\ell m}^{*}\qquad(0\leq m\leq\ell),

(6.18)

\displaystyle g_{\ell m}(r)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}g(\bm{x})Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi)^{*}\sin\vartheta d\vartheta d\phi,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\Psi_{\ell m}(r)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\Psi(\bm{x})Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi)^{*}\sin\vartheta d\vartheta d\phi.

(6.20)

See
[
15
, Lemma 5]
. Note that

\triangle^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}=g_{\ell m},\quad\Psi_{\ell m}=-\mathcal{H}_{\ell}[g_{\ell m}],

(6.21)

where

\displaystyle\triangle^{(\ell)}w=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}r^{2}\frac{dw}{dr}-\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}}w,

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\ell}[\check{g}](r)=\frac{1}{2\ell+1}\Big[\int_{0}^{r}\check{g}(s)\Big(\frac{r}{s}\Big)^{-\ell-1}sds+\int_{r}^{+\infty}\check{g}(s)\Big(\frac{r}{s}\Big)^{\ell}sds\Big],

(6.23)

and

\|{\nabla}\Psi\|^{2}=\sum_{\ell m}\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2},

(6.24)

where

\|\nabla^{(\ell)}w\|^{2}=\Big|\frac{dw}{dr}\Big|^{2}+\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{r^{2}}|w|^{2}.

(6.25)

We are observing

(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}=4\pi\sum_{\ell,m}Q_{\ell m},

(6.26)

where

Q_{\ell m}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}|\triangle^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr.

(6.27)

First we consider
\ell=0
. Then we have

Q_{00}=\int_{0}^{R}(\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ss}}y)y^{*}\rho r^{4}dr,

(6.28)

where

\displaystyle y=-\frac{1}{r\rho}\frac{d}{dr}\Psi_{00},\quad g_{00}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}(r^{3}\rho y),

(6.29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ss}}y=-\frac{1}{\rho r^{4}}\frac{d}{dr}\Big(\gamma r^{4}\rho\frac{dy}{dr}\Big)-(3\gamma-4)\frac{1}{r}\frac{d\varUpsilon}{dr}y,

(6.30)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}(\mathcal{L}^{\mathsf{ss}}y)y^{*}\rho r^{4}dr=\int_{0}^{R}\Big(\gamma\Big|\frac{dy}{dr}\Big|^{2}-(3\gamma-4)\frac{1}{r}\frac{d\varUpsilon}{dr}|y|^{2}\Big)\rho r^{4}dr.

(6.31)

Since we are supposing
\frac{4}{3}<\gamma
, we have

{\delta}_{*}:=\inf_{0<r<R}-(3\gamma-4)\frac{1}{r}\frac{d\varUpsilon}{dr}>0,

(6.32)

and

Q_{00}\geq{\delta}_{*}\int_{0}^{R}|y|^{2}\rho r^{4}dr={\delta}_{*}\int_{0}^{R}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr.

(6.33)

But

\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{0}^{r}g_{00}(s)s^{2}ds=0\quad\mbox{for}\quad r>R,

since

g_{00}=0
on
]R,+\infty[
and

\int_{0}^{R}g_{00}(r)r^{2}dr=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi}}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}g(\bm{x})d\bm{x}=0

for
g\in\mathfrak{g}_{L}
.
On the other hand,

\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}\in L^{\infty}(0,R),

since

\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}=-\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{r}^{R}g_{00}(s)s^{2}ds\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0<r<R

enjoys

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{r^{2}}\sqrt{\int_{r}^{R}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}s^{2}ds}\sqrt{\int_{0}^{R}\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}|g_{00}(s)|^{2}s^{2}ds}

\displaystyle\leq C(R-r)^{\frac{1}{2(\gamma-1)}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\frac{R}{2}\leq r<R,

for
\displaystyle\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}=O((R-r)^{\frac{2-\gamma}{\gamma-1}})
.

Therefore, under the convention that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}
means
0
for
r>R
, we can write

Q_{00}\geq{\delta}_{*}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr.

(6.34)

In other words, if we adopt the decomposition

\displaystyle u^{r}(\bm{x})=\sum_{\ell m}u^{r}_{\ell m}(r)Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi),

(6.35)

\displaystyle u^{r}_{\ell m}(r)=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}u^{r}(\bm{x})Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi)^{*}\sin\vartheta d\vartheta d\phi,

(6.36)

while

\displaystyle\bm{u}(\bm{x})=u^{r}(\bm{x})\bm{e}_{r}+u^{\vartheta}(\bm{x})\bm{e}_{\vartheta}+u^{\phi}(\bm{x})\bm{e}_{\phi},

(6.37)

where

\displaystyle\bm{e}_{r}=\frac{\partial}{\partial r},\quad\bm{e}_{\vartheta}=\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta},\quad\bm{e}_{\phi}=\frac{1}{r\sin\vartheta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}.

(6.38)

We see

\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}(r^{2}\rho u^{r}_{00})=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}(r^{2}g_{00}),

(6.39)

for

g=\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^{2}\rho u^{r})+\frac{1}{r\sin\vartheta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta}(\sin\vartheta u^{\vartheta})+\frac{1}{r\sin\vartheta}\frac{\partial u^{\phi}}{\partial\phi}.

Hence we have

\rho(r)u^{r}_{00}(r)=\int_{0}^{r}g_{00}(s)s^{2}ds=-\frac{d}{dr}\Psi_{00}(r)\quad\mbox{for}\quad 0\leq r<R,

therefore we can write

\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr=\int_{0}^{+\infty}|u^{r}_{00}|^{2}\rho(r)r^{2}dr.

Thus

Q_{00}\geq{\delta}_{*}\int_{0}^{+\infty}|u^{r}_{00}|^{2}\rho(r)r^{2}dr.

(6.40)

Moreover there is a positive number
\mu_{0}
such that

\int_{0}^{R}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr\geq\mu_{0}\int_{0}^{R}|\Psi_{00}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr.

(6.41)

Proof. Looking at

\mu:=\inf_{\psi\in\mathcal{F}}\frac{\int_{0}^{R}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr}{\int_{0}^{R}|\Psi_{00}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr},

(6.42)

where

\mathcal{F}=\Big\{\psi\in C^{1}[0,R]\ \Big|\ \psi(R)=0,|\psi(r)|\leq\|\Psi_{00}\|_{L^{\infty}}\Big\},

we prove
\mu>0
.

Suppose
\mu=0
for reductio ad absurdum. Then there is a sequence
(\psi_{n})_{n=1,2,\cdots,}
in
\mathcal{F}

such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}|\psi_{n}|^{2}r^{2}dr=1
and

M_{n}:=\int_{0}^{R}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\psi_{n}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr\searrow 0\quad\mbox{as}\quad n\to\infty.

For
0<r\leq r^{\prime}\leq R
it hold

\displaystyle|\psi_{n}(r)|

\displaystyle=\Big|-\int_{r}^{R}D\psi_{n}(s)ds\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\int_{r}^{R}\frac{\rho(s)}{s^{2}}ds}\sqrt{\int_{r}^{R}\frac{1}{\rho(s)}|D\psi_{n}(s)|^{2}s^{2}ds}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\rho_{O}}\sqrt{\frac{R-r}{Rr}}M_{n}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\rho_{O}}}{r}M_{1},

and

\displaystyle|\psi_{n}(r^{\prime})-\psi_{n}(r)|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\rho_{O}}\sqrt{\frac{r^{\prime}-r}{r^{\prime}r}}M_{n}\leq\sqrt{\rho_{O}}\sqrt{\frac{r^{\prime}-r}{r^{\prime}r}}M_{1}.

Therefore by the Ascoli-Arzelà theorem we can suppose
\psi_{n}
tends to a limit

\psi_{\infty}
uniformly on any compact subinterval of
]0,R]
, by taking a subsequence. Since
M_{n}\to 0
, we have
\psi_{\infty}=0
.
For any
0<r_{*}\ll 1
we have

\int_{0}^{r_{*}}|\psi_{n}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr\leq\|\Psi_{00}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\frac{r_{*}^{3}}{3}.

Therefore

\displaystyle 1=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}|\psi_{n}(r)^{2}r^{2}dr

\displaystyle\leq\|\Psi_{00}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\frac{r_{*}^{3}}{3}+\int_{r_{*}}^{R}|\psi_{n}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr

\displaystyle\to\|\Psi_{00}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\frac{r_{*}^{3}}{3}+\int_{r_{*}}^{R}|\psi_{\infty}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr

\displaystyle=\|\Psi_{00}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\frac{r_{*}^{3}}{3}.

Taking
r_{*}
so small that
\displaystyle\|\Psi_{00}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\frac{r_{*}^{3}}{3}<1
, we see a contradiction.

\square

Next we consider
\ell\geq 1
. We claim

Q_{\ell m}\geq 4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}--4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr.

(6.43)

For
0<\epsilon\ll 1
, we put

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}=\gamma\mathsf{A}(\rho+\epsilon)^{\gamma-2}
. Then, as
\epsilon\searrow+0
,

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}\nearrow\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}
on
0<r<R
, and

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}|g_{\ell m}|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr

\displaystyle\nearrow

\displaystyle\quad Q_{\ell m}=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)|g_{\ell m}|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr.

But we see

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}|g_{\ell m}|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big|\sqrt{\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}}\triangle^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\sqrt{\Big(\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}\Big)^{-1}}\Psi_{\ell m}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr

\displaystyle-4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{0}^{+\infty}2\mathfrak{Re}[(\triangle^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m})\Psi_{\ell m}^{*}]+\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\Big(\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}\Big)^{-1}|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr

\displaystyle\geq 4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\Big(\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}\Big)^{-1}|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr,

for

\int_{0}^{+\infty}(\triangle^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m})\Psi_{\ell m}^{*}r^{2}dr=-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}r^{2}dr,

since

\displaystyle r^{2}\frac{d\Psi_{\ell m}}{dr}\Psi_{\ell m}^{*}=O\Big(\frac{1}{r}\Big)
as
r\rightarrow+\infty
.
Since
\displaystyle\Big(\Big(\frac{d\varUpsilon}{d\rho}\Big)_{\epsilon}\Big)^{-1}=\frac{(\rho+\epsilon)^{2-\gamma}}{\gamma\mathsf{A}}\searrow\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}=\frac{\rho^{2-\gamma}}{\gamma\mathsf{A}}
as
\epsilon\searrow 0
, we have (
6.43
).

Let us show that

\mathcal{Q}_{1}[w]:=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\|\nabla^{(1)}w\|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}|w|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr\geq 0

(6.44)

for
w=\Psi_{\ell m}
.

Fixing
{N}\geq R
, put

\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{N}[w]:=\int_{0}^{N}\Big(\|\nabla^{(1)}w\|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}|w|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr.

(6.45)

This is the quadratic form associated with the operator

\mathcal{P}w=\Big[-\triangle^{(1)}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}\Big]w.

(6.46)

We consider
\mathcal{P}
in
\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{N}=L^{2}([0,{N}];r^{2}dr)
. We have the Friedrichs extension
\bm{P}^{N}
of
\mathcal{P}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}(]0,{N}[)
,
keeping in mind that

4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}\leq\frac{4\pi\mathsf{G}}{\gamma\mathsf{A}}\rho_{O}^{2-\gamma}.

We see that
\bm{P}^{N}
is of the Strum-Liuville type. the boundary condition at
r={N}
is the Dirichlet boundary condition
w|_{r=S}=0
.

Let
\mu_{1}
be the least eigenvalue of
\bm{P}^{N}
. We are going to show
\mu_{1}\geq 0
. Let
\phi_{1}
be an eigenfunction. We can suppose
\phi_{1}(r)>0
for
0<r<{N}
.
Consider
\displaystyle\varUpsilon^{\prime}:=\frac{d\varUpsilon}{dr}
, which satisfies

\mathcal{P}\varUpsilon^{\prime}=0
on
[0,+\infty[
. Here we consider

\varUpsilon(r)=-K\Big(\frac{1}{R}-\frac{1}{r}\Big),\quad\varUpsilon^{\prime}(r)=-\frac{K}{r^{2}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad R<r

with
\displaystyle K=-\Big(r^{2}\frac{d\varUpsilon}{dr}\Big)_{r=R-0}(>0)
.
we look at

\displaystyle 0=(\mathcal{P}\varUpsilon^{\prime}|\phi_{1})_{\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{N}}

\displaystyle=(\varUpsilon^{\prime}|\mathcal{P}\phi_{1})_{\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{N}}+r^{2}\varUpsilon^{\prime}\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dr}\Big|_{r={N}-0}

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}(\varUpsilon^{\prime}|\phi_{1})+r^{2}\varUpsilon^{\prime}\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dr}\Big|_{r={N}-0}

Since
\varUpsilon^{\prime}<0
on
]0,{N}]
, we have
(\varUpsilon^{\prime}|\phi_{1})_{\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{N}}<0
. But
\displaystyle r^{2}\varUpsilon^{\prime}\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dr}\Big|_{r={N}-0}\geq 0
, since

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dr}\Big|_{r={N}-0}\leq 0
. Consequently
\mu_{1}\geq 0
and

\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{N}[w]\geq 0\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall w\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{P}^{N}).

Since we cannot say
w=\Psi_{\ell m}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{P}^{N})
, for maybe
\Psi_{\ell m}({N})\not=0
,
we decompose it as

w=\Psi_{\ell m}=W^{N}+\Pi^{N},

(6.47)

where

\Pi^{N}=-C_{\ell m}{N}^{-\ell-1}\Big(\frac{r}{{N}}\Big)^{\ell},\quad C_{\ell m}=\frac{1}{2\ell+1}\int_{0}^{R}g_{\ell m}(s)s^{\ell+2}ds.

(6.48)

Then

\triangle^{(\ell)}W^{N}=\triangle^{(\ell)}w,\quad W^{N}({N})=0.

(6.49)

Note

\mathcal{P}W^{N}=-g_{\ell m}-\Big(\frac{\ell(\ell+1)-2}{r^{2}}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}\Big)(w-\Pi^{N})\in\mathfrak{X}_{0}^{N},

since
g_{\ell m}\in C([0,R[,w=\Psi_{\ell m}=O(r^{2})
for
\ell\geq 2
,
\Pi^{N}=O(r^{\ell})
. Therefore
W^{N}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{P}^{N})
and

\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{N}[W^{N}]\geq 0.

Since

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{(1)}\Pi^{N}\|^{2}\leq 3C_{\ell m}{N}^{-2\ell-4},

\displaystyle 4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}|\Pi^{N}|^{2}\leq 4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{\rho_{O}^{2-\gamma}}{\gamma\mathsf{A}}C_{\ell m}^{2}{N}^{-2\ell-2}

on
[0,{N}]
, we see

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{N}[w]

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{Q}_{1}^{S}[W^{N}]+O({N}^{-2\ell+1})

\displaystyle\geq O({N}^{-2\ell+1}),

Taking the limit as
{N}\rightarrow+\infty
, we get (
6.44
) for
w=\Psi_{\ell m}
.

Now

Q_{\ell m}\geq 4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{Q}_{1}[\Psi_{\ell m}]\geq 0,

(6.50)

and, for
\ell\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\ell m}

\displaystyle\geq 4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{0}^{+\infty}\Big(\|\nabla^{(\ell)}\Psi_{\ell m}\|^{2}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{d\rho}{d\varUpsilon}|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}\Big)r^{2}dr

\displaystyle=4\pi\mathsf{G}\Big(\mathcal{Q}_{1}[\Psi_{\ell m}]+\int_{0}^{+\infty}(\ell(\ell+1)-2)|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}dr\Big)

\displaystyle\geq 4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{0}^{+\infty}(\ell(\ell+1)-2)|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}dr.

(6.51)

Note that

(\ell(\ell+1)-2)\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\Psi_{\ell m}(r)|^{2}dr\geq\frac{4}{R^{2}}\int_{0}^{R}|\Psi_{\ell m}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr

(6.52)

for
\ell\geq 2
.

Consequently

\displaystyle(\bm{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=4\pi\sum_{0\leq\ell,|m|\leq\ell}Q_{\ell m}

\displaystyle\geq\mathfrak{d}\Big(\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr+\sum_{2\leq\ell,|m|\leq\ell}(\ell(\ell+1)-2)\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}dr\Big),

(6.53)

with

\displaystyle{\delta}:=4\pi({\delta}_{*}\wedge\mathsf{G})
.

Therefore

Theorem 8

Under
Situation
1

,
(PD.2)
holds
for
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{10},\mathfrak{n}_{1}\geq\mathfrak{n}_{10},
defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{1}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\Big[\int_{0}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{\rho}\Big|\frac{d\Psi_{00}}{dr}\Big|^{2}r^{2}dr+\sum_{2\leq\ell,|m|\leq\ell}(\ell(\ell+1)-2)\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}dr\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\Big[\int_{0}^{+\infty}|u^{r}_{00}|^{2}\rho(r)r^{2}dr+\sum_{2\leq\ell,|m|\leq\ell}(\ell(\ell+1)-2)\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\Psi_{\ell m}|^{2}dr\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}},

(6.54a)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{10}(\bm{u})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mu_{1}}\Big[\int_{0}^{R}|\Psi_{00}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr+\sum_{2\leq\ell,|m|\leq\ell}\int_{0}^{R}|\Psi_{\ell m}(r)|^{2}r^{2}dr\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(6.54b)

\displaystyle\mbox{with}\quad\mu_{1}=\mu_{0}\wedge\frac{4}{R^{2}}.

Here

\displaystyle\Psi_{\ell m}(r)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathcal{K}[g](\bm{x})Y_{\ell m}(\vartheta,\phi)^{*}\sin\vartheta d\vartheta d\phi,

(6.55)

\displaystyle u^{r}_{00}(r)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{4\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\Big(\bm{u}(\bm{x})\Big|\frac{\bm{x}}{\|\bm{x}\|}\Big)\sin\vartheta d\vartheta d\phi,

(6.56)

in which

\bm{x}=(x^{1},x^{2},x^{3})=(r\sin\vartheta\cos\phi,r\sin\vartheta\sin\phi,r\cos\vartheta)

and
g=\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})
.

Note that the seminorm
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{10}
and the associated
\mathfrak{N}
are not norm, that is,
\mathfrak{N}(U)=0
does not imply
U=\bm{0}
, say, does not imply
\bm{u}=0
.
But
\mathfrak{N}({U})
can control the amplitude of

\Psi=\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})]
, except for the
\ell=1
components, and
\dot{\bm{u}}
. Here
-4\pi\mathsf{G}\Psi=\mathfrak{d}\Phi=\Phi_{\rho_{b}+\mathfrak{d}\rho}-\Phi_{b},\mathfrak{d}\rho=-\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})
and
\bm{v}=\dot{\bm{u}}
are the quantities essential for the perturbation, and
the control of the magnitude of
\bm{u}=\bm{\varphi}(t,\bm{x})-\bm{x}+\bm{u}^{0}
itelf is not essential in the discussion of the stability of the background.
( Recall
\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bar{\bm{x}}
for
\omega_{b}=0
.)
In fact

\mathfrak{n}_{1}(\bm{u})=0
implies that
\Psi_{\ell m}=0,g_{\ell m}=0
on
[0,+\infty[
for
\ell\not=1
, and

\mathfrak{n}_{10}(\bm{u})=0
implies that
\Psi_{\ell m}=0,g_{\ell m}=0
on
[0,+R]
for
\ell\not=1
,
and

g(\bm{x})=\sqrt{\frac{3}{4\pi}}g_{1,0}(r)\cos\vartheta-\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}g_{1,1}(r)\sin\vartheta e^{\mathrm{i}\phi}+\sqrt{\frac{3}{8\pi}}g_{1,-1}(r)\sin\vartheta e^{-\mathrm{i}\phi}.

Remark 8

It seems impossible to control the magnitude of
\Psi_{1m},m=0,\pm 1
by the following reason. The differential operator
\mathcal{P}\restriction C_{0}^{\infty}(]0,+\infty[)
admits the Friedrichs extention
\bm{P}
in
\mathfrak{X}=L^{2}([0,+\infty[,r^{2}dr)
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is the quadratic form associated with
\bm{P}
. But
\bm{P}
is not of the Sturm-Liouville type. In fact, let
\phi\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{P})
and
\mathcal{P}\phi=\lambda\phi
with
\lambda>0
. Since
-\triangle^{(1)}\phi=0
on
]R,+\infty[
,
there are constants
C_{\pm}
such that

\phi=C_{+}\phi_{+}+C_{-}\phi_{-}\quad\mbox{on}\quad]R,+\infty[,

where

\phi_{\pm}(r)=\sqrt{r}J_{\pm\frac{3}{2}}(\sqrt{\lambda}r),\quad J_{\pm\frac{3}{2}}\quad\mbox{being the Bessel function}.

Since

J_{\pm\frac{3}{2}}(r)\sim\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi r}}\cos\Big(r\pm\frac{3\pi}{4}-\frac{\pi}{4}\Big)\quad\mbox{as}\quad r\rightarrow+\infty,

we see
\phi_{\pm}\not\in L^{2}([R,+\infty[,r^{2}dr)
, and

\phi\in L^{2}([R,+\infty[,r^{2}dr)
requires
C_{\pm}=0
,
\phi=0
on
]R,+\infty[
.
By the uniqueness of solutions of ODE, it follows that
\phi=0
on
[0,+\infty[
.
In other words, any positive real number cannot be an eigenvalue of
\bm{P}
, so
\bm{P}
is not of the Sturm-Liouville type.

However, if we restrict ourselves to axially and equatorially symmetric perturbations, functions of
(r,|\zeta|)
, we have
\Psi_{\ell m}=0
for odd
\ell
a priori, since

\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}d\phi=0
for
m\not=0
and
P_{\ell}^{0}(-\zeta)=-P_{\ell}^{0}(\zeta)
for odd
\ell
, we need not control the magnitudes of
\Psi_{1m},m=0,\pm 1
. In this situation
\mathfrak{n}(\bm{u})=0
implies
\Psi=0,g=0
, or,

\mathfrak{d}\Phi=0,\mathfrak{d}\rho=0
, or, more precisely, we have

\mathfrak{n}_{10}(\bm{u})\geq\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{1}}{4\pi}}\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu_{1}}{4\pi}}\|\mathcal{K}[\mathfrak{d}\rho]\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{R})},

(6.57)

since

\|\Psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{R})}^{2}=4\pi\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell:\mbox{even}\\
|m|\leq\ell\end{subarray}}\|\Psi_{\ell m}\|_{L^{2}([0,R],r^{2}dr)}^{2}

in this case.

• Now we are going to try to derive
(PD.2)
with another seminorm

\mathfrak{n}(\bm{u}):=\|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\|_{L^{2}\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x}\Big)}=\Big[\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}

(6.58)

for

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+2\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\bm{n})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}\Big]d\bm{x}

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}|(\bm{u}|\bm{n})|^{2}d\bm{x}+

\displaystyle-4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})]\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}d\bm{x}.

(6.59)

First we claim:

Proposition 13

Let
\varepsilon
be a positive number. If

-\varepsilon\frac{\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}}{\rho}\leq\mathscr{A}\leq 0\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{R},

(6.60)

then

(\bm{L}_{0}\bm{u}|\bm{u})\geq(1-\varepsilon)\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}.

(6.61)

Here
g=\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})
.

Proof. We see

\displaystyle\Big|2\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\bm{n})g^{*}d\bm{x}\Big]\Big|\leq\varepsilon\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\Gamma P\mathscr{A}^{2}|(\bm{u}|\bm{n}))|^{2}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}+\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\Gamma P|\mathscr{A}|}{\rho}\|{\nabla}\rho\|^{2}|(\bm{u}|\bm{n}))|^{2}d\bm{x}

by (
6.60
), where
g=\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})
, since

-|\mathscr{A}|-\mathscr{A}=0
for
\mathscr{A}\leq 0
, we have (
6.61
).

\square

Therefore, if we adopt the Cowling approximation, which neglect the perturbed self-gravitation term

-4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}d\bm{x}
,
we have
(PD.2)
with
0<\varepsilon<1
.
Namely we consider the following

Situation 2

The equation of state is (
2.2
):
P=\rho^{\gamma}\exp(S/\mathsf{C}_{V})
.
Instead of
\bm{L}
, we consider
\bm{L}_{0}
:

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{0}\bm{u}

\displaystyle={\nabla}\Big(-\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})+\frac{\gamma P}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a})\Big)+

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma P}{\rho}\Big(-\mathfrak{a}\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})+(\bm{u}|\mathfrak{a}){\nabla}\rho\Big).

(6.62)

We can claim

Theorem 9

Under the
Situation
2

suppose (
6.60
) with
0<\varepsilon<1
. Put
\delta:=1-\varepsilon
. Then
(PD.2)
holds for
\bm{L}
replaced by
\bm{L}_{0}
with

\delta
and
\mathfrak{n}=\mathfrak{n}_{2}
defined by

\mathfrak{n}_{2}(\bm{u})=\Big[\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(6.63)

So let us look at the perturbed self-gravitation term in order to justify the Cowling approximation in this context.

Put

\displaystyle K^{\spadesuit}:=\sup_{g\in\mathfrak{g}_{L}}\frac{4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}d\bm{x}}{\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}},

(6.64)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{L}:=\Big\{g\in L^{2}\Big(\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x},\mathfrak{R}\Big)\Big|\ g=\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\quad\mbox{for}\quad\exists\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L})\Big\}.

(6.65)

We can claim
K^{\spadesuit}<\infty
by the following

Proposition 14

There exists a constant
C
such that

0\leq 4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}d\bm{x}\leq C\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}.

(6.66)

Proof. We have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{K}[g](\bm{x})|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\pi}\sqrt{\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{d\bm{x}^{\prime}}{\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|}}\sqrt{\int_{\mathfrak{R}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{R}{2\pi}}\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{R})},

where

R:=\sup\{\|\bm{x}\|\ |\ \bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}\}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{R}{2\pi}}\|g\|_{L^{2}}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}|g|d\bm{x}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}R^{2}\|g\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

On the other hand,

\nu_{*}:=\inf_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}>0,

(6.67)

since

\frac{1}{C}\mathsf{d}^{-\frac{2-\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\leq\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\leq C\mathsf{d}^{-\frac{2-\gamma}{\gamma-1}}\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{R},

where
\mathsf{d}:=\mathrm{dist}(\bm{x},\partial\mathfrak{R})
and
1<\gamma<2
.
Then

\int_{\mathfrak{R}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}\leq\frac{1}{\nu_{*}}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x},

therefore (
6.66
) holds with

\displaystyle C=\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\frac{R^{2}}{\nu_{*}}
.
\square

Consequently we can claim

Suppose
K^{\spadesuit}<1
and let
0<\varepsilon<1-K^{\spadesuit}
so that

\delta:=1-\varepsilon-K^{\spadesuit}>0
. If (
6.60
) holds with
\varepsilon
,
then
(PD.2)
holds with
{\delta}
and

\mathfrak{n}(\bm{u}):=\|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})\|_{L^{2}\Big(\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}d\bm{x}\Big)}=\Big[\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Thus we have the following question, which is still open:

Question 2

When the background realizes the condition
K^{\spadesuit}<1
?

Let us note the nondimensionalization of the estimate of
K^{\spadesuit}
. We have

\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}=4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathsf{a}^{2}(\gamma-1)\Theta^{-\frac{2-\gamma}{\gamma-1}}(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2}),

where

\displaystyle\Theta=\Theta\Big(\frac{\|\bm{x}\|}{\mathsf{a}},\frac{x^{3}}{\|\bm{x}\|};\frac{1}{\gamma-1},\mathfrak{b}\Big),

\displaystyle\omega_{1}=O(\varUpsilon_{O}),\quad\omega_{2}=O\Big(\frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma\mathsf{A}}\varUpsilon_{O}\Big),

and

\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\frac{\gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|g|^{2}d\bm{x}=4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathsf{a}^{5}\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}(\gamma-1)\Theta^{-\frac{2-\gamma}{\gamma-1}}(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})|\hat{g}(\underline{\bm{x}})|^{2}d\underline{\bm{x}},

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{1}=\Big\{\underline{\bm{x}}\quad\Big|\quad\|\underline{\bm{x}}\|<{U}_{1}\Big(\frac{\underline{x}^{3}}{\|\underline{\bm{x}}\|}\Big)\quad\Big\},

\displaystyle\Theta=\Theta\Big(\|\underline{\bm{x}}\|,\frac{\underline{x}^{3}}{\|\underline{\bm{x}}\|};\frac{1}{\gamma-1},\mathfrak{b}\Big),\quad\hat{g}(\underline{\bm{x}})=g(\mathsf{a}\underline{\bm{x}}).

On the other hand, we see

4\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}}\mathcal{K}[g]g^{*}d\bm{x}=4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathsf{a}^{5}\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}\mathcal{K}[\hat{g}](\underline{\bm{x}})\hat{g}^{*}(\underline{\bm{x}})d\underline{\bm{x}}.

Therefore we have the reduction

K^{\spadesuit}=\sup_{\hat{g}}\frac{\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}\mathcal{K}[\hat{g}]\hat{g}^{*}d\underline{\bm{x}}}{\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}(\gamma-1)\Theta^{-\frac{2-\gamma}{\gamma-1}}(1+\omega_{1})(1+\omega_{2})|\hat{g}|^{2}d\underline{\bm{x}}}.

(6.68)

If the background is isentropic, we have
\omega_{1}=\omega_{2}=0
.
If
\Omega=0
and the background is isentropic and spherically symmetric, then

K^{\spadesuit}=\sup_{\hat{g}}\frac{\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}\mathcal{K}[\hat{g}]\hat{g}^{*}d\underline{\bm{x}}}{\int_{\mathfrak{R}_{1}}(\gamma-1)\theta^{-\frac{2-\gamma}{\gamma-1}}|\hat{g}|^{2}d\underline{\bm{x}}}.

where
\theta=\theta(\|\underline{\bm{x}}\|,;\frac{1}{\gamma-1})
is the Lane-Emden function.

7
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
of astrophysicists

The equation of linearized adiabatic stellar oscillations traditionally used by astrophysicists is little bit different from
ELASO

(
1.11
) with (
1.12
), (
1.13a
), (
1.13b
), (
1.13c
), (
1.13d
)
derived in Section 3. In this section we derive the equation
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
used
by astrophysicists after V. R. Lebovitz 1961
[
17
]
, D. Lynden-Bell and J. P. Ostriker 1967
[
20
]
and J. L. Friedman and B. F. Schutz 1978
[
8
]
.

We consider a pair of solutions
(\rho,S,\bm{v})
and
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
of the Euler-Poisson equations described in a rotating frame (
1.1a
),(
1.1b
),(
1.1c
),(
1.6
) with the equation of state (
1.8
).
Here
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
is a compactly supported axially symmetric stationary solution with

\bm{v}_{b}(t,\bm{x})=\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix}\times\bm{x}=\omega_{b}(\varpi,z)\begin{bmatrix}-x^{2}\\
x^{1}\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

(7.1)

We define the Lagrange displacement
\bm{\xi}
in the Lebovitz sense (
[
17
]
) by

\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b})||\bm{v})-\bm{x}.

(7.2)

Here, for
\bm{V}=\bm{v},\bm{v}_{b}
,
\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{V})
stands for the solution
\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{V})
of the problem

\frac{d\bm{x}}{dt}=\bm{V}(t,\bm{x}),\quad\bm{x}|_{t=s}=\bm{y}.

(7.3)

Let us suppose Assumption
3
:

The initial configurations of
\rho,S
are the same to those of the background, that is,

\displaystyle\rho^{0}=\rho(0,\cdot)=\rho_{b},

(7.4a)

\displaystyle S^{0}=S(0,\cdot)=S_{b}.

(7.4b)

Then the linearized equation for
\bm{\xi}
turns out to be

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}}{\partial t^{2}}+\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}=0\quad\mbox{in}\quad[0,+\infty[\times\mathfrak{R}_{b},

(7.5)

where

\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\dot{\bm{\xi}}=2\bm{\Omega}\times\dot{\bm{\xi}}+\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\dot{\bm{\xi}}

(7.6)

with

\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\dot{\bm{\xi}}=2(D\dot{\bm{\xi}})\bm{v}_{b}=2\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\dot{\bm{\xi}}}{\partial\phi},

(7.7)

and

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}+\mathcal{L},

(7.8)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=D((D\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b}=\omega_{b}^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}}{\partial\phi^{2}},

(7.9a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=2\bm{\Omega}\times((D\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b})=2\Omega\omega_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\begin{bmatrix}-\xi^{2}\\
\\
\xi^{1}\\
\\
0\end{bmatrix},

(7.9b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=-\Big(D((D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b})+D(2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b})\Big)\bm{\xi}=W_{b}\bm{\xi},

(7.9c)

with

\displaystyle W_{b}

\displaystyle:=-D((D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b})-D(2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b})

\displaystyle=(2\Omega+\omega_{b})\omega_{b}\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix}+2(\Omega+\omega_{b})\begin{bmatrix}\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial\varpi}(x^{1})^{2}&\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial\varpi}x^{1}x^{2}&\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial z}x^{1}\\
\\
\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial\varpi}x^{1}x^{2}&\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial\varpi}(x^{2})^{2}&\frac{\partial\omega_{b}}{\partial z}x^{2}\\
\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix},

(7.9d)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\lceil\delta P\rfloor-\frac{\nabla P_{b}}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor-4\pi\mathsf{G}\nabla\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor],

(7.9e)

with

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\xi}),\quad\lceil\delta S\rfloor=-(\bm{\xi}|\nabla S_{b}),

\displaystyle\lceil\delta P\rfloor

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta S\rfloor=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+(\Gamma P)_{b}(\bm{\xi}|\mathfrak{a}_{b}).

(7.9f)

Here and hereafter
\nabla,D
stand for
\nabla_{\bm{x}},D_{\bm{x}}
,
namely,

\displaystyle\nabla f

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{1}}\\
\\
\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{2}}\\
\\
\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{3}}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle D\bm{Q}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial Q^{1}}{\partial x^{1}}&\frac{\partial Q^{1}}{\partial x^{2}}&\frac{\partial Q^{1}}{\partial x^{3}}\\
\\
\frac{\partial Q^{2}}{\partial x^{1}}&\frac{\partial Q^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}&\frac{\partial Q^{2}}{\partial x^{3}}\\
\\
\frac{\partial Q^{3}}{\partial x^{1}}&\frac{\partial Q^{3}}{\partial x^{2}}&\frac{\partial Q^{3}}{\partial x^{3}}\end{bmatrix},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}
means

\displaystyle-x^{2}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial x^{1}}+x^{1}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial x^{2}}
.

The equation (
7.5
) with (
7.6
) (
7.7
) (
7.8
) with (
7.9a
) - (
7.9f
) is the equation used by astrophysicists after
[
17
]

[
8
]

[
20
]
.
We call this equation the ‘
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
‘.

Let us explain how to derive the equation
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
(
7.5
) following

[
17
]
,
[
8
]
and
[
20
]
.

We shall use the identities

\displaystyle D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{V})=\Big(D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(s|t,\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{V})||\bm{V})\Big)^{-1},

(7.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{s}\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{V})=-D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{V})\bm{V}(s,\bm{y}).

(7.11)

In fact, (
7.10
) can be deduced by differentiating the identity

\bm{y}=\bm{\varphi}(s|t,\bm{\varphi}(t|s,y||\bm{V})||\bm{V})

(7.12)

by
\bm{y}
,
and (
7.11
) can be deduced by differentiating (
7.12
) by
s

and using (
7.10
).

Note that, for
\bm{x}=\bm{x}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{V})
, it holds that

\displaystyle D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\exp\Big[\int_{0}^{t}D_{\bm{x}}\bm{V}(t^{\prime},\bm{\varphi}(t^{\prime}|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{V}))dt^{\prime}\Big],

(7.13)

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\exp\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{V}(t^{\prime},\bm{\varphi}(t^{\prime}|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{V}))dt^{\prime}\Big],

(7.14)

Definition 8

For a pair
(Q,Q_{b})
of scalar fields, we define the scaler field
\Delta Q
, the Lagrangian perturbation, and
\delta Q
, the Eulerian perturbation, by

\displaystyle\Delta Q(t,\bm{x})=Q(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x}))-Q_{b}(t,\bm{x}),

(7.15)

\displaystyle\delta Q(t,\bm{x})=Q(t,\bm{x})-Q_{b}(t,\bm{x}).

(7.16)

For a pair
(\bm{Q},\bm{Q}_{b})
of vector fields, we define

\displaystyle\Delta\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x}))-\bm{Q}_{b}(t,\bm{x}),

(7.17)

\displaystyle\delta\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x})-\bm{Q}_{b}(t,\bm{x}).

(7.18)

We suppose that
\delta\rho,\delta S,\delta\bm{v}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.

Then we have
\bm{\xi}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.

In fact, writing

\bm{\xi}=\underline{\bm{x}}-\bm{x},\quad\underline{\bm{x}}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}),\quad\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}_{b}),

we see that

\bm{\psi}(t)=\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})
satisfies

\frac{d\bm{\psi}}{dt}=\bm{v}(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\psi})-\bm{v}(t,\bm{x})+\bm{v}(t,\bm{x})-\bm{v}_{b}(t,\bm{x}).

Hence

\Big\|\frac{d\bm{\psi}}{dt}\Big\|\leq C_{0}+C_{1}\|\bm{\psi}\|,\quad\bm{\psi}(0)=\bm{0},

where

C_{0}=\|\delta\bm{v}\|_{L^{\infty}},\quad C_{1}=\|D_{\bm{x}}\bm{v}\|_{L^{\infty}}.

The Gronwall argument imlies

\|\bm{\psi}(t)\|\leq C_{0}(e^{C_{1}t}-1).

Thus
\bm{\xi}={O}(\varepsilon)
.
As for the derivatives
