\displaystyle\psi_{j}(t)=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})
, we see

\psi_{j}=O(\varepsilon)
by the same argument, so we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\bm{\xi}=O(\varepsilon)
.

Consequently, when
\delta Q=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
for scalar fileds paired, we can claim

\Delta Q=\delta Q+(\bm{\xi}|\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q_{b})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(7.19)

and when
\delta\bm{Q}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
for vector fields, we have

\Delta\bm{Q}=\delta\bm{Q}+(D\bm{Q}_{b})\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(7.20)

• Note that it hold that

\mathrm{div}\bm{v}_{b}=0,\quad(\bm{v}_{b}|\nabla\rho_{b})=0,\quad(\bm{v}_{b}|\nabla S_{b})=0.

(7.21)

• Integration of the equation (
1.1c
) gives

S(t.\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}))=S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}}):=S(0,\bar{\bm{x}}).

(7.22)

Then

\Delta S(t,\bm{x})=S^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-S_{b}(\bm{x}).

Here
\bar{\bm{x}}=\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b})
.

We suppose

S^{0}=S_{b}.

(7.23)

Since
S_{b}
is supposed to be axially symmetric, and
the values of coordinates
\varpi,z
of
\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{v}_{b})
are invariant in
t
, we see
S_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})=S_{b}(\bm{x})
. Therefore

\Delta S=0,

(7.24)

provided (
7.23
).
Thanks to (
7.19
), this implies

\delta S=-(\bm{\xi}|\nabla S_{b})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(7.25)

• Integration of the equation of continuity (
1.1a
) gives

\rho(t,\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}))=\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})\exp\Big[-\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{v})(t^{\prime},\bm{\varphi}(t^{\prime}|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v})dt^{\prime}\Big],

(7.26)

where
\rho^{0}=\rho(0,\cdot)
.

We denote

\mathfrak{R}(t)=\{\bm{x}\quad|\quad\rho(t,\bm{x})>0\},\quad\mathfrak{R}_{b}=\{\bm{x}\quad|\quad\rho_{b}(\bm{x})>0\}.

(7.27)

Of course

\mathfrak{R}(t)=\{\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v})\quad|\quad\bar{\bm{x}}\in\mathfrak{R}(0)\},

and the equation (
7.26
) is valid for

\forall(t,\bar{\bm{x}})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

We suppose

\rho^{0}=\rho_{b}.

(7.28)

Then
\mathfrak{R}(0)=\mathfrak{R}_{b}
and

\mathfrak{R}(t)=\{\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v})\quad|\quad\bar{\bm{x}}\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}\},

that is,

\underline{\bm{x}}=\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})\in\mathfrak{R}(t)\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}.

Here we note that
\rho_{b}(\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b}))=\rho_{b}(\bm{x})
by the axial symmetricity.

We claim

\Delta\rho+\rho_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(7.29)

Thanks to (
7.19
), this implies

\delta\rho=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\xi})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(7.30)

Proof of (
7.29
). Proof is devided into several steps. 1) Let
(Q,Q_{b})
be a pair of scalar fields such that
\delta Q=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
. Let
D
be an arbitrary domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Put

\displaystyle\underline{D}(t)=\{\underline{\bm{x}}=\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})\quad|\quad\bm{x}\in D\},

\displaystyle I=\int_{\underline{D}(t)}Q(t,\underline{\bm{x}})d\underline{\bm{x}},\quad I_{b}=\int_{D}Q_{b}(t,\bm{x})d\bm{x},

\displaystyle\delta I=I-I_{b}.

(7.31)

Then we have

\delta I=\int_{D}q(t,\bm{x})d\bm{x},

(7.32)

where

q(t,\bm{x})=\Delta Q(t,\bm{x})+Q(t,\underline{\bm{x}})(\mathrm{det}(I+D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x}))-I).

(7.33)

Note that

q=\Delta Q+Q_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

2) Let
\bm{V}=\bm{v}
or
\bm{v}_{b}
. Let
Q
be a scalar field, and
E
a domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Put

\displaystyle E(t)=\{\bm{z}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bm{y})\quad|\quad\bm{y}\in E\},

\displaystyle I(t)=\int_{E(t)}Q(t,\bm{z})d\bm{z}.

(7.34)

Here we denote
\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y})=\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{V})
.
Then

I(t)=\int_{E}Q(t,\bm{z})\mathrm{det}D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bm{y})d\bm{y}.

(7.35)

Note that

\mathrm{det}D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bm{y})=\exp\Big[\int_{0}^{t}(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{V})(t^{\prime},\bm{\varphi}(t^{\prime}|0,\bm{y}))dt\Big]>0

and

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathrm{det}D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bm{y})=(\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{V})(t,\bm{z})\cdot\mathrm{det}D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bm{y}).

Differentiating (
7.35
) by
t
, we get

\frac{dI}{dt}=\int_{E}\Big(\frac{\partial Q}{\partial t}+(\bm{V}|\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q)+Q\mathrm{div}_{\bm{x}}\bm{V}\Big)(t,\bm{z})\mathrm{det}D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}d\bm{y}

(7.36)

Therefore, if

\frac{\partial Q}{\partial t}+(\bm{V}|\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q)+Q\mathrm{div}\bm{V}=0

(7.37)

holds everywhere, then
dI/dt=0
and

I(t)=\int_{E}Q(0,\bm{y})d\bm{y}.

(7.38)

Note that (
7.37
) holds for
Q=\rho
or
\rho_{b}
respectively with

\bm{V}=\bm{v}
or
\bm{v}_{b}
, that is, (
1.1a
).

3) Let
D
be an arbitrary domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
(Q,Q_{b})
be a pair of scalar fields such that
(
7.37
) holds everywhere for
Q
with
\bm{V}=\bm{v}
and for
Q_{b}
with
\bm{v}_{b}
.

Then, keeping in mind

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{x}}=\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}),

\displaystyle\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}_{b})\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bar{\bm{x}}=\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b}),

we see

\bm{x}\in D\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bar{\bm{x}}\in\bar{D}\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\underline{\bm{x}}\in D(t),

where

\displaystyle\bar{D}=\{\bar{\bm{x}}=\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b})\quad|\quad\bm{x}\in D\},

\displaystyle\underline{D}(t)=\{\underline{\bm{x}}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v})\quad|\quad\bar{\bm{x}}\in\bar{D}\}.

We consider

I=\int_{\underline{D}}Q(t,\underline{\bm{x}})d\underline{\bm{x}},\quad I_{b}=\int_{D}Q(t,\bm{x})d\bm{x},

\delta I=I-I_{b}.

Thanks to 2) we have

I=\int_{\bar{D}}Q(0,\bar{\bm{x}})d\bar{\bm{x}},\quad I_{b}=\int_{\bar{D}}Q_{b}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})d\bar{\bm{x}}.

Therefore

\delta I=\int_{\bar{D}}(Q(0,\bar{\bm{x}})-Q_{b}(0,\bar{\bm{x}}))d\bar{\bm{x}}.

Onthe other hand, thanks to 1), we have

\delta I=\int_{D}q(t,\bm{x})d\bm{x}.

Hence

\int_{\bar{D}}(Q(0,\bar{\bm{x}})-Q_{b}(0,\bar{\bm{x}}))d\bar{\bm{x}}=\int_{D}q(t,\bm{x})d\bm{x}.

The change of variable

\bar{D}\rightarrow D:\bar{\bm{x}}\mapsto\bm{x}=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}_{b})
,
for which we have
\mathrm{det}D_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{x}=1
since
\mathrm{div}\bm{v}_{b}=0
, we get

\int_{\bar{D}}\Big(Q(0,\bar{\bm{x}})-Q_{b}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})-q(t,\bm{x})\Big)d\bar{\bm{x}}=0.

Since
D
, therefore
\bar{D}
is arbitrary, we can claim

Q(0,\bar{\bm{x}})-Q_{b}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})=q(t,\bm{x})\quad\forall\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

4) Applying the above discussion to
Q=\rho,Q_{b}=\rho_{b}
,
we can claim

\rho^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})-\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})=\Delta\rho(t,\bm{x})+\rho(t,\underline{\bm{x}})\Big(\mathrm{der}(I+D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x}))-1\Big).

(7.39)

Supposing
\rho^{0}=\rho_{b}
, we can claim

\Delta\rho+\rho_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Proof is done.
\square

• As for the equation of motion (
1.1b
), we devide it by
\rho
and
\rho_{b}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial t}+\sum_{k}v^{k}\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial x^{k}}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})+\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P+\nabla\Phi=0,

(7.40)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{v}_{b}}{\partial t}+\sum_{k}v_{b}^{k}\frac{\partial\bm{v}_{b}}{\partial x^{k}}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla P_{b}+\nabla\Phi_{b}=0.

(7.41)

Here (
7.40
) is valid on
\mathfrak{R}(t)
and (
7.41
) is valid on
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
. So we can apply the operator

\Delta
on
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
, for which
\underline{\bm{x}}=\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}\in\mathfrak{R}(t)
while
\bm{x}\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}
, to consider

\Delta\Big[\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial t}+\sum_{k}v^{k}\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial x^{k}}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})+\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P+\nabla\Phi\Big]=0,

(7.42)

In order to analyze

\displaystyle\Delta\Big[\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial t}+\sum_{k}v^{k}\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial x^{k}}\Big]
,
we introduce the operator

\displaystyle\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{V}},\bm{V}=\bm{v},\bm{v}_{b}
. Namely,

Definition 9

For a scalar field
Q
, we define

\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{V}}Q(t,\bm{x})=\partial_{t}Q(t,\bm{x})+(\bm{V}(t,\bm{x})|\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x})).

(7.43)

Here
\partial_{t}=\partial/\partial t
. For a vector field
\bm{Q}
, we define

\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{V}}\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x})=\partial_{t}\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x})+(D_{\bm{x}}\bm{Q}(t,\bm{x}))\bm{V}(t,\bm{x}).

(7.44)

Let us note that

\Big(\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{V}}\bm{Q}\Big)^{j}=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{V}}Q^{j},

since
((D_{\bm{x}}\bm{Q})\bm{V})^{j}=(\bm{V}|\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q^{j})
.

We claim the formula:

Formula
:
For scalar field
Q
:

\Delta\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}}Q=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\Delta Q,

(7.45)

and for vector field
\bm{Q}
:

\Delta\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}}\bm{Q}=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\Delta\bm{Q}.

(7.46)

Here we mean

\Big(\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}}Q\Big)_{b}=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}Q_{b},

so we mean

\Delta\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}}Q(t,\bm{x})=\Big(\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}}Q\Big)(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})-\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}Q_{b}(t,\bm{x}).

Proof. It is sufficient to verify (
7.45
). By definition we see

\displaystyle\clubsuit

\displaystyle:=\Delta\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}}Q(t,\bm{x})-\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\Delta Q_{b}(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=(\bm{v}(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})|\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})-\Big(\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})\Big|\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}\Big)

\displaystyle-(\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})|(D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi})^{\top}\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}))-(\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})|\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}))

Using (
7.11
), we have

\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}=\bm{v}(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})-D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}})D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0|t,\bm{x})\bm{v}_{b}(x).

(7.47)

Here we denote

\displaystyle\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y})=\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{v}),\quad\bm{\varphi}_{b}(t|s,\bm{y})=\bm{\varphi}(t|s,\bm{y}||\bm{v}_{b}),

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{x}}=\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0|t,\bm{x}).

On the other hand, we have

D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})=D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}})D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0|t,\bm{x})-I.

(7.48)

Hence

\displaystyle\clubsuit

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})|D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}})D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0|t,x)\bm{v}_{b}(t,\bm{x}))

\displaystyle-\Big(\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})\Big|\Big(D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}})D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0|t,\bm{x})\Big)^{\top}\nabla_{\bm{x}}Q(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})\Big)

\displaystyle=0.

\square

We have

\Delta\bm{v}=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\bm{\xi}.

(7.49)

Proof. Consider

\clubsuit:=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})=\partial_{t}\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})+D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x}).

Recalling (
7.47
), (
7.48
), we see

\displaystyle\clubsuit

\displaystyle=\bm{v}(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})-D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}})D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0|t,\bm{x})\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle+D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}})D_{\bm{y}}\bm{\varphi}_{b}(0|t,\bm{x})\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})-\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\bm{v}(t,\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})-\bm{v}_{b}(\bm{x})=\Delta\bm{v}(t,\bm{x}).

\square

Consequently we have

\displaystyle\Delta\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}}\bm{v}

\displaystyle=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\Delta\bm{v}=\Big(\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\Big)^{2}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}}{\partial t^{2}}+2\Big(D_{\bm{x}}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}\Big)\bm{v}_{b}+D_{\bm{x}}((D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b}

(7.50)

and

\displaystyle\Delta[2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}]

\displaystyle=2\bm{\Omega}\times\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=2\bm{\Omega}\times\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+2\bm{\Omega}\times((D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b}).

(7.51)

Since
\Delta\bm{x}=\bm{\xi}
clearly, we have

\Delta[\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x}]=\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{\xi}).

(7.52)

.

Summing up, we can write

\displaystyle\Delta\Big[\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial t}+\sum_{k}v^{k}\frac{\partial\bm{v}}{\partial x^{k}}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})\Big]=

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}}{\partial t^{2}}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{\xi}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{\xi}+L_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{\xi},

(7.53)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\dot{\bm{\xi}}

\displaystyle=2(D_{\bm{x}}\dot{\bm{\xi}})\bm{v}_{b},

(7.54a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=D_{\bm{x}}((D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b},

(7.54b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=2\bm{\Omega}\times((D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b}),

(7.54c)

\displaystyle L_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{\xi}).

(7.54d)

• Let us analyze

\displaystyle\Delta\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P\Big)
. It is easy to see

\Delta\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P\Big)=-\frac{\Delta\rho}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\nabla P_{b}+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\delta P+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}(D_{\bm{x}}\nabla P_{b})\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

We can write this as

\Delta\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P\Big)=-\frac{\delta\rho}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\nabla P_{b}-\frac{(\bm{\xi}|\nabla\rho_{b})}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\nabla P_{b}+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\delta P+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}(D_{\bm{x}}\nabla P_{b})\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

since

\Delta\rho=\delta\rho+(\bm{\xi}|\nabla\rho_{b})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Moreover we can write

\Delta\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P\Big)=-\frac{\delta\rho}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\nabla P_{b}+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\delta P+D_{\bm{x}}\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla P_{b}\Big)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(7.55)

since

D_{\bm{x}}\Big(\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla P_{b}\Big)\bm{\xi}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}(D_{\bm{x}}\nabla P_{b})\bm{\xi}-\frac{(\bm{\xi}|\nabla\rho_{b})}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\nabla P_{b}.

Now let us consider
\delta P
. Since
\Delta S=0
, we have

\Delta P=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}\Delta\rho+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(7.56)

By (
7.19
), this means

\displaystyle\delta P

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}\delta\rho+\Big(\bm{\xi}\Big|\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}\nabla\rho_{b}-\nabla P_{b}\Big)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}\delta\rho-\Big(\bm{\xi}\Big|\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\nabla S_{b}\Big)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}\Big)_{b}\delta\rho+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\delta S+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Here we have used
(
7.25
).

Summing up, we have

\Delta\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P\Big)=\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{\xi}+\Big(D_{\bm{x}}\Big(\frac{\nabla P_{b}}{\rho_{b}}\Big)\Big)\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(7.57)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\nabla P_{b}}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\lceil\delta P\rfloor,

(7.58a)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\xi}),

(7.58b)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta S\rfloor

\displaystyle=-(\bm{\xi}|\nabla S_{b}),

(7.58c)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta P\rfloor

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta S\rfloor.

(7.58d)

Here we recall
\displaystyle\frac{\partial P}{\partial\rho}=\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}
by the definition of
\Gamma
, Definition
3
.

Moreover we can avoid to mention
\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}

in the approximation of
\delta P
. In fact, since

\displaystyle\delta P=\Delta P-(\bm{\xi}|\nabla P_{b})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

\displaystyle\Delta\rho=-\rho_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),

(
7.56
) implies

\delta P=-(\Gamma P)_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi}-(\bm{\xi}|\nabla P_{b})+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(7.59)

We note that
[
8
]
gives another expression of
\displaystyle\Delta\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P\Big))
, namely,

\Delta\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\nabla P\Big))=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\Big[-\nabla(\Gamma_{b}P_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi})-((D_{\bm{x}}\bm{\xi})\nabla P_{b})^{\top}+(\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi})\nabla P_{b}\Big]+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

(7.60)

This expression (
7.60
) can be derived by applying the formula

\displaystyle\Delta\nabla Q

\displaystyle=\nabla\Delta Q+(D\nabla Q)\bm{\xi}-\nabla(\bm{\xi}|\nabla Q)+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

\displaystyle=\nabla\Delta Q-((D\bm{\xi})\nabla Q)^{\top}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(7.61)

to
Q=P
and noting

\Delta P=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\Delta\rho+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})=\Gamma_{b}P_{b}\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

and

\frac{\Delta\rho}{\rho_{b}}=-\mathrm{div}\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

• Let us look at

\Delta\nabla\Phi(\bm{x})=\nabla\Phi(\bm{x}+\bm{\xi})-\nabla\Phi_{b}(\bm{x}),

where

\displaystyle\Phi=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\rho],\qquad\Phi_{b}=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}[\rho_{b}],

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}[g](\bm{x})=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{g(\bm{x}^{\prime})}{\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|}d\bm{x}^{\prime}.

We introduce the integral operator
\mathcal{K}^{\nabla}
by

\mathcal{K}^{\nabla}[g](\bm{x})=-\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\frac{\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}}{\|\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\|^{3}}g(\bm{x}^{\prime})d\bm{x}^{\prime}.

(7.62)

When
g\in C^{\alpha}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{3}),0<\alpha<1
, then

\mathcal{K}^{\nabla}[g]\in C^{1+\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and

\nabla\mathcal{K}[g]=\mathcal{K}^{\nabla}[g]
. So

\displaystyle\Delta\nabla\Phi(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\Big(\mathcal{K}^{\nabla}[\delta\rho](\underline{\bm{x}})+\nabla\Phi_{b}(\underline{\bm{x}})-\nabla\Phi_{b}(\bm{x})\Big)

\displaystyle=-4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{K}^{\nabla}[\delta\rho]+(D_{\bm{x}}\nabla\Phi_{b}(\bm{x}))\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}).

Here
\underline{\bm{x}}=\bm{x}+\bm{\xi}
, and we note

\mathcal{K}^{\nabla}[\delta\rho]\in C^{1+\alpha}
and
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.

Consequently we can claim

\Delta\nabla\Phi=4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{L}_{1}\bm{\xi}+(D_{\bm{x}}\nabla_{\bm{x}}\Phi_{b})\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})

(7.63)

with

\mathcal{L}_{1}\bm{\xi}=\nabla\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\xi})].

(7.64)

• Summing up, we have derived the equation used by astrophysicists:

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}}{\partial t^{2}}+\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}=0,

(7.65)

where

\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\dot{\bm{\xi}}=2\bm{\Omega}\times\dot{\bm{\xi}}+\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\dot{\bm{\xi}},

(7.66)

with

\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\dot{\bm{\xi}}=2(D\dot{\bm{\xi}})\bm{v}_{b},

(7.67)

and

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}+L_{\blacklozenge 0}+\mathcal{L}_{0}+L_{0\blacklozenge}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{L}_{1}+L_{1\blacklozenge}

(7.68)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=D((D\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b},

(7.69a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=2\bm{\Omega}\times((D\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b}),

(7.69b)

\displaystyle L_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{\xi}),

(7.69c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor}{\rho_{b}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla\lceil\delta P\rfloor,

(7.69d)

with

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\xi}),\quad\lceil\delta S\rfloor=-(\bm{\xi}|\nabla S_{b}),

\displaystyle\lceil\delta P\rfloor

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta S\rfloor=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+\Gamma_{b}P_{b}(\bm{\xi}|\mathfrak{a}_{b})

(7.69e)

\displaystyle L_{0\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=\Big(D\Big(\frac{\nabla P_{b}}{\rho_{b}}\Big)\Big)\bm{\xi},

(7.69f)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=-\nabla\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor]=\nabla\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\xi})],

(7.69g)

\displaystyle L_{1\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}

\displaystyle=(D\nabla\Phi_{b})\bm{\xi}.

(7.69h)

We denote

\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{0}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{L}_{1}.

However we would like to rewrite
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}
further. Since
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
satisfies the equation of motion

(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})+\frac{\nabla P_{b}}{\rho_{b}}+\nabla\Phi_{b}=0

on
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
, we see

L_{0\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}+L_{1\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}=-D\Big((D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b}+2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b}+\bm{\Omega}\times(\Omega\times\bm{x}\Big)\bm{\xi}.

But we have

D\Big(\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{x})\Big)\bm{\xi}=\bm{\Omega}\times(\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{\xi}).

Thus

L_{0\blacklozenge}+L_{1\blacklozenge}+L_{\blacklozenge 0}=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 00}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 01},

(7.70)

where

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 00}\bm{\xi}=-\Big(D((D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b})\Big)\bm{\xi},\quad\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 01}\bm{\xi}=-\Big(D(2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b})\Big)\bm{\xi}.

(7.71)

We define

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}:=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 00}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 01}.

(7.72)

Consequently we have
the equation (
7.5
) with (
7.6
) (
7.7
) (
7.8
) with (
7.9a
) - (
7.9f
), as the equation of linear adiabatic stellar oscillations used by astrophysicists after
[
17
]

[
8
]

[
20
]
.

We note that the derivation above does not require that
\bm{v}_{b}
is small, say,
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
during the linear approximation procedure, while we supposed

\bm{v}_{b}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
to derive
ELASO
(
1.11
) in Section 3.
But we recall that (
7.28
):
\rho^{0}=\rho_{b}
and (
7.23
):
S^{0}=S_{b}
are supposed, while only (
3.1
):
\{\rho^{0}>0\}=\{\rho_{b}>0\}
was supposed in Section 3. Moreover we note that
\bm{\xi}(0,\bm{x})=\bm{0}
by the definition
(
7.2
), while the Lagrange displacement

\bm{u}
in our equation (
1.11
) which was defined by
(
1.14
) or (
3.27
):

\bm{u}(t,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v})-\bm{\varphi}(t|0,\bar{\bm{x}}||\bm{v}_{b})+\bm{u}^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})

enjoys
\bm{u}(0,\bar{\bm{x}})=\bm{u}^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})
, which
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
was arbitrary as long as

\rho^{0}-\rho_{b}=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u}^{0}),\quad S^{0}-S_{b}=-(\nabla S_{b}|\bm{u}^{0})

and can be
\not=\bm{0}
.

8
Analysis of
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)

8.1
Trivial displacements

By the definition (
7.2
) it holds that
\bm{\xi}(0,\bm{x})=0
. But it is possible to consider solutions
\tilde{\bm{\xi}}=\bm{\xi}+\bm{\eta}
of ELASO
(
\blacklozenge
) (
7.5
). After
[
8
]
let us call
\bm{\eta}(t,\bm{x})
a trivial displacement if it satisfies

\displaystyle 0=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\eta}),

(8.1)

\displaystyle 0=-(\nabla S_{b}|\bm{\eta}),

(8.2)

\displaystyle\bm{0}=\partial_{t}\bm{\eta}+(D\bm{\eta})\bm{v}_{b}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\eta}.

(8.3)

Here
\partial_{t}=\partial/\partial t
. Then

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\rho||\bm{\eta}\rfloor:=-\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{\eta})=0,

(8.4)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta S||\bm{\eta}\rfloor:=-(\nabla S_{b}|\bm{\eta})=0,

(8.5)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta P||\bm{\eta}\rfloor:=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta\rho||\bm{\eta}\rfloor-\Big(\frac{\partial P}{\partial S}\Big)_{b}(\nabla S_{b}|\bm{\eta})=0,

(8.6)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\bm{v}||\bm{\eta}\rfloor:=\partial_{t}\bm{\eta}+(D\bm{\eta})\bm{v}_{b}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\eta}=\bm{0}.

(8.7)

Here we note that

\displaystyle\delta\bm{v}

\displaystyle=\Delta\bm{v}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\quad(\mbox{see}\quad\eqref{z19}),

\displaystyle=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\bm{\xi}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})\quad(\mbox{see}\quad\eqref{Z20.1})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\bm{\xi}+(D\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\xi}+\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2}),\quad(\mbox{see}\quad\eqref{z40}),

so

\lceil\delta\bm{v}||\bm{\xi}\rfloor=\partial_{t}\bm{\xi}+(D\bm{\xi})\bm{v}_{b}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\xi}

approximates
\delta\bm{v}
.

Let
\bm{\eta}(t,\bm{x})
be a trivial displacement. Then (
8.1
) and (
8.3
) imply
\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{\eta}=\bm{0}
and
\mathcal{L}_{1}\bm{\eta}=\bm{0}
. Moreover we have

\partial_{t}^{2}\bm{\eta}+\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\partial_{t}\bm{\eta}+(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 00})\bm{\eta}=\bm{0},

(8.8)

and

2\bm{\Omega}\times\partial_{t}\bm{\eta}+(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 01})\bm{\eta}=\bm{0}.

(8.9)

Therefore we can claim that
\bm{\eta}
satisfies the equation (
7.5
), so
\tilde{\bm{\xi}}=\bm{\xi}+\bm{\eta}
should be a solution of (
7.5
).

Proof of (
8.8
). Eliminating
\eta_{t}\bm{\eta},\partial_{t}^{2}\bm{\eta}
by (
8.3
), we see that the left-hand side of (
8.8
) reduces to

\displaystyle(D((D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\eta})-(D\bm{v}_{b})(D\bm{\eta}))\bm{v}_{b}-((D\bm{v}_{b})(D\bm{v}_{b})-D((D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{v}_{b}))\bm{\eta}

\displaystyle=\langle D^{2}\bm{v}_{b}|\bm{\eta},\bm{v}_{b}\rangle-\langle D^{2}\bm{v}_{b}|\bm{\eta},\bm{v}_{b}\rangle=\bm{0}.

Here we denote

\langle D^{2}\bm{f}|\bm{X},\bm{Y}\rangle^{i}=\sum_{k.\ell}\frac{\partial^{2}f^{i}}{\partial x^{k}\partial x^{\ell}}X^{k}Y^{\ell}.

(8.10)

\square

Proof of (
8.9
). Eliminating
\partial_{t}\bm{\eta}
by (
8.3
), we see that
the left-hand side of (
8.9
) reduces to

2\bm{\Omega}\times(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\eta}-(D(2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}_{b}))\bm{\eta}=\bm{0}.

\square

Of course
\bm{\eta}=\bm{0}
is a trivial displacement, which could be called the trivial trivial displacement, and we are interested in non-trivial trivial displacements in this sense.

Let us consider

\bm{\eta}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla S_{b}\times\nabla f,

(8.11)

where
f
is a scalar fields wich satisfies

\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}f=\partial_{t}f+(\bm{v}_{b}|f)=0.

(8.12)

Then
\eta
is a trivial displacement.

Proof. Clearly (
8.1
), (
8.2
) are satisfied, for any
f
. Usng the properties of the background

\sum_{k}\bm{v}_{b}\partial_{k}\rho_{b}=0,\qquad\sum_{k}\bm{v}_{b}\partial_{k}S_{b}=0,

(8.13)

where
\partial_{k}=\partial/\partial x^{k}
, we can verify

((D\bm{\eta})\bm{v}_{b}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\eta})^{j}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\Big(-\sum_{\alpha,\beta}E^{j\alpha\beta}(\partial_{\alpha}S_{b})(\partial_{\beta}f)+\nabla S_{b}\times\nabla(\bm{v}_{b}|f)\Big),

(8.14)

where

E^{j\alpha\beta}=\sum_{k}\Big(\epsilon^{jk\beta}\partial_{k}v_{b}^{\alpha}+\epsilon^{j\alpha k}\partial_{k}v_{b}^{\beta}+\epsilon^{k\alpha\beta}\partial_{k}v_{b}^{j}\Big),\quad\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu}=\mathrm{sign}\left(\begin{array}[]{rrr}1&2&3\\
\lambda&\mu&\nu\end{array}\right).

(8.15)

But we can verify

E^{j\alpha\beta}=\epsilon^{j\alpha\beta}\sum_{k}\partial_{k}\bm{v}_{b}^{k},

(8.16)

therefore
E^{j\alpha\beta}=0
for
\forall j,\alpha,\beta
,
since
\mathrm{div}\bm{v}_{b}=0
. Thus

(D\bm{\eta})\bm{v}_{b}-(D\bm{v}_{b})\bm{\eta}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla S_{b}\times\nabla(\bm{v}_{b}|f)

so that (
8.3
) is satisfied, since

\partial_{t}\bm{\eta}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\nabla S_{b}\times\nabla\partial_{t}f.

\square

General solution of (
8.12
) is

f(t,\bm{x})=f^{0}(\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b})),

(8.17)

where
\bar{\bm{x}}\mapsto f^{0}(\bar{\bm{x}})
is an arbitrary function on
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
.Then

\bm{\eta}(0,\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}(\bm{x})}\nabla S_{b}\times\nabla_{\bar{\bm{x}}}f^{0}(\bm{x}).

(8.18)

When
\nabla S_{b}\not=\bm{0}
,
that is,
S_{b}
is not a constant, then this construction gives many non-trivial trivial solutions.
However when
\nabla S_{b}=\bm{0}
,
\bm{\eta}
given by (
8.11
) is trivial,
=\bm{0}
.
If
\bm{v}_{b}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
the displacement given by

\bm{\eta}(t,\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}(\bar{\bm{x}})}\mathrm{rot}_{\bar{\bm{x}}}\bm{f}(\bar{\bm{x}}),\quad\bar{\bm{x}}=\bm{\varphi}(0|t,\bm{x}||\bm{v}_{b})

(8.19)

satisfies (
8.1
), (
8.3
) approximately with
errors
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, provided that the function
\bm{f}
is small

\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
.
We compare these observations with the discussion of Theorem
4
in Section 4.

8.2
Functional analysis of ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)

Let us consider the equation (
7.5
) in the functional space

\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}_{b},\rho_{b}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}^{3})
. We suppose

Assumption
:
The equation of state enjoys
Assumtion

5
and the background
(\rho_{b},S_{b},\bm{v}_{b})
is barotropic.

The operator
\mathcal{B}:\dot{\bm{\xi}}\mapsto 2\bm{\Omega}\times\dot{\bm{\xi}}
can be considered as a bounded linear operator in
\mathcal{B}(\mathfrak{H})
, and
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{0}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{L}_{1}
can be extended to a self-adjoint operator bounded from below in
\mathfrak{H}
, by following the discussion of Section 4. We look at the resting terms

\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}
.

First, we see that
\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}
is skew-symmetric considered on
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})
. In fact,
for
\bm{v}_{1},\bm{v}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{v}_{1}|\bm{v}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}=2\int\omega_{b}\Big(\frac{\partial\bm{v}_{1}}{\partial\phi}\Big|\bm{v}_{2}\Big)\rho_{b}d\bm{x}=

\displaystyle=-2\int\omega_{b}\Big(\bm{v}_{1}\Big|\frac{\partial\bm{v}_{2}}{\partial\phi}\Big)\rho_{b}d\bm{x}=-(\bm{v}_{1}|\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{v}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}.

However this operator is not bounded in
\mathfrak{H}
-norm, if
\bm{v}_{b}\not=\bm{0}
, say,
\omega_{b}\not=0
.

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}
are symmetric on
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})
. In fact
we have,
for
\bm{u}_{1},\bm{u}_{2}\in C_{0}^{\infty}
,

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=-\int\omega_{b}^{2}\Big(\frac{\partial\bm{u}_{1}}{\partial\phi}\Big|\frac{\partial\bm{u}_{2}}{\partial\phi}\Big)\rho_{b}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}},

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}_{1}|\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=2\Omega\int\omega_{b}\Big(\frac{\partial u_{1}^{1}}{\partial\phi}(u_{2}^{2})^{*}+u_{1}^{2}\Big(\frac{\partial u_{2}^{1}}{\partial\phi}\Big)^{*}\Big)\rho_{b}d\bm{x}

\displaystyle=(\bm{u}_{1}|\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}_{2})_{\mathfrak{H}}.

However
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}
cannot be bounded from below, if
\bm{v}_{b}\not=0
. In fact,
we see

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=-\int\omega_{b}^{2}\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|^{2}\rho_{b}d\bm{x},

(8.20)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle=4\Omega\int\omega_{b}\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\frac{\partial u^{1}}{\partial{\phi}}(u^{2})^{*}\Big]\rho_{b}d\bm{x}.

(8.21)

Therefore

((\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1})\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}=-m^{2}\int\omega_{b}^{2}\|\bm{u}\|^{2}\rho_{b}d\bm{x},

when

\bm{u}(\bm{x})=\bm{U}(r,\vartheta)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi},\quad\bm{U}=\begin{bmatrix}U\\
U\\
0\end{bmatrix},\quad\mbox{while}\quad U\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{R}).

Suppose
\omega_{b}\not=0
, and
we suppose
\omega_{b}^{2}\geq\kappa^{2}>0
,
\kappa
being a constant, on a domain
\mathcal{V}(\not=\emptyset),\mathrm{supp}[U]\subset\mathcal{V}
. Then we have

((\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1})\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq-m^{2}\kappa^{2}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

Therefore

\inf_{\bm{u}\in C_{0}^{\infty}}\frac{((\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1})\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}\leq-m^{2}\kappa^{2}

for
\forall m
so that

\inf_{\bm{u}\in C_{0}^{\infty}}\frac{((\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1})\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}}{\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}=-\infty.

Thus

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}
is not bounded from below, if
\omega_{b}\not=0
.

Finally, as for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 00}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 01}
, it is a simple multiplication by the real-valued matrix function
W_{b}\in C(\mathbb{R}^{3};\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3})
,
therefore
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}

can be considered as a symmetric bounded operator on
\mathfrak{H}
.

Summing up, when
\bm{v}_{b}\not=0
, or at least, when

\bm{v}_{b}\not=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, by which we cannot neglect terms
\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\partial_{t}\bm{\xi},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{\xi},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{\xi},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{\xi}
as
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
, the application of the Hille-Yosida theory as discussed in Section 4 does not work
for the unboundedness of
\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}
, which implies the unboundedness of the total
\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}
, and the unboundedness from below of

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}
, which implies the unboundedness from below of the total
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}
. However,
if we can suppose that
\bm{v}_{b}=\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon)
, then the terms
\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge 1}\partial_{t}\bm{\xi},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{\xi},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{\xi},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{\xi}
are of
\mathscr{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
and negligible. In this situation,
\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge},\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}
reduce
to
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{0}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{L}_{1}
, that is, the astrophysicists’
ELASO (
\blacklozenge
)
(
7.5
) reduces to our
ELASO
(
1.11
).

• However we want to say something for the initial value problem to
ELASO (
\blacklozenge
)
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}}{\partial t^{2}}+\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}=\bm{f}(t,\bm{x}),

(8.22)

\displaystyle\bm{\xi}|_{t=0}=\bm{\xi}^{0},\quad\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}=\bm{v}^{0},

(8.23)

where
\bm{f},\bm{\xi}^{0},\bm{v}^{0}
are given data. Suppose that the background is a barotropic admissible stationary solution.

In order to fix the idea to consider the integro-differential operator
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}
in the Hilbert space

\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}_{b},\rho_{b}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}^{3}\}
,
we define
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}

as

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge})=\{\bm{u}\in(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge})_{0}\quad|\quad\mbox{Both }\quad\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{L}\bm{u}

\displaystyle\mbox{ belong to}\quad\mathfrak{H}\quad\mbox{in distribution sense}\},

(8.24)

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}),

(8.25)

where
\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
stands for the functional space

\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}=\Big\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}\quad\Big|\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})\in L^{2}\Big(\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}d\bm{x}\Big),\quad\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\in\mathfrak{H}\Big\}

(8.26)

endowed with the norm

\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}}=\Big[\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\Big\|\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u});L^{2}\Big(\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}d\bm{x}\Big)\Big\|^{2}+\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\Big]^{\frac{1}{2}},

(8.27)

and

(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge})_{0}
stands for the closure of
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})

in
\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
.

Note that, if
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
, then we have, a priori,
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
and

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
. Thus, if

\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge})
, then
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
in distribusion sense. But, if
\bm{v}_{b}\not=0
, we may not say that
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
guarantees

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u},\mathcal{L}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
separately.

This formulation comes from the following observation.

Put, for
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
,

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge 2}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle:=-\int\omega_{b}^{2}\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|^{2}\rho_{b}d\bm{x}=-\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

(8.28)

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge 1}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle:=4\Omega\int\omega_{b}\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\frac{\partial u^{1}}{\partial\phi}(u^{2})^{*}\Big]\rho_{b}d\bm{x},

(8.29)

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge 0}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle:=-\int(W_{b}\bm{u}|\bm{u})\rho_{b}d\bm{x},

(8.30)

\displaystyle Q_{0}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle:=\int\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+2\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(\bm{u}|\bm{n})\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}d\bm{x}\Big]+

\displaystyle-\int\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}|(\bm{u}|\bm{n})|^{2}\|{\nabla}\rho\|d\bm{x}4\pi\mathsf{G}\int\mathcal{K}[\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})]\mathrm{div}(\rho\bm{u})^{*}d\bm{x}.

(8.31)

Then we have the estimates

\displaystyle|Q_{\blacklozenge 1}[\bm{u}]|

\displaystyle\leq 2|\Omega|\Big(\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}\Big),

(8.32)

\displaystyle|Q_{\blacklozenge 0}[\bm{u}]|

\displaystyle\leq\|W_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

(8.33)

Put, for
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
,

Q_{0}^{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]:=\sum_{\nu=0,1,2}Q_{\blacklozenge\nu}[\bm{u}]+Q_{0}[\bm{u}],

(8.34)

Then, if
\bm{u}\in C_{0}^{\infty}
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge 2}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(8.35)

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge 1}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(8.36)

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge 0}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(8.37)

\displaystyle Q_{0}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}},

(8.38)

and

Q_{0}^{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]=(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}.

(8.39)

Let us introduce an equivalent norm
\|\cdot\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}\blacklozenge}
on
\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
as follows.

Putting

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle:=2+4|\Omega|,

(8.40)

\displaystyle K_{0}

\displaystyle:=2+4|\Omega|+2\|W_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}},

(8.41)

we have

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{K_{0}}{2}+1\Big)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\frac{K_{1}}{2}\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu=0,1,2}Q_{\blacklozenge\nu}[\bm{u}]+K_{0}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+K_{1}\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\frac{3K_{0}}{2}-1\Big)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\frac{3K_{1}}{2}\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

(8.42)

As for
\mathcal{L}
and
Q_{0}
we know

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}|\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}\leq Q_{0}[\bm{u}]+\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\int\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}|\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}+2a\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

(8.43)

with the positive number
a
defined by (
4.23
).
Therefore, putting

Q_{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]:=Q_{0}^{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]+(a+K_{0})\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+K_{1}\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

(8.44)

when

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle=(K_{1}-1)\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+(a+K_{0})\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+Q_{\blacklozenge 1}[\bm{u}]+Q_{\blacklozenge 0}[\bm{u}]+Q_{0}[\bm{u}]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{K_{1}}{2}\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\frac{K_{0}-2}{2}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}|\mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})|^{2}d\bm{x}\geq 0,

(8.45)

we have

\frac{1}{C}\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}}^{2}\leq Q_{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]+\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\leq C\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}}^{2},

(8.46)

that is,

\|\cdot\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}\blacklozenge}:=\sqrt{Q_{\blacklozenge}+\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}}

(8.47)

is equivalent to
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}}
.

Although we do not know whether this operator
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}
is a closed operator in

\mathfrak{H}
or not, we can claim, at least, the following

Proposition 15

If a sequence
(\bm{u}_{n})_{n=0,1,2,\cdots}
in
\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge})
converges to a limit
\bm{u}
in
\mathfrak{H}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{n}
converge in
\mathfrak{H}
, then the limit
\bm{u}
belongs to
\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge})
and
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}_{n}
converge to
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}
.

Proof. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}_{n}\rightarrow\bm{f}_{\blacklozenge 2}
,

\mathcal{L}\bm{u}\rightarrow\bm{f}
in
\mathfrak{H}
. Of course
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{u}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{u}
in
\mathfrak{H}
. Put
\bm{f}_{\blacklozenge 0}:=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0}\bm{u}
. Since

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}_{n}
are supposed to converge, we have

\displaystyle-\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}(\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n})\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}=Q_{\blacklozenge 2}[\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n}]

\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}_{n+p}-\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}_{n}|\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\rightarrow 0

as
n+p\geq n\rightarrow\infty
. For any
\bm{\psi}\in C_{0}^{\infty}
, we have

\displaystyle|(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}_{n+p}-\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}_{n}|\bm{\psi})_{\mathfrak{H}}|=|Q_{\blacklozenge 1}(\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n},\bm{\psi})|

\displaystyle\leq 2|\Omega|\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}(\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n})\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\bm{\psi}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}.

Therefore

\|\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}_{n+p}-\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq 2|\Omega|\Big\|\omega_{b}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}(\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n})\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\rightarrow 0,

that is,
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}_{n}
converge in
\mathfrak{H}
. Put

\bm{f}_{\blacklozenge 1}:=\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}
.
Since
\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{n}
converge in
{\mathfrak{H}}
,

Q_{0}[\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n}]=(\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{n+p}-\mathcal{L}\bm{u}_{n}|\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n})_{\mathfrak{H}}\rightarrow 0.

Summing up, we have that
Q_{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n}]\rightarrow 0
and
\|\bm{u}_{n+p}-\bm{u}_{n}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}\blacklozenge}\rightarrow 0
. This implies that
\bm{u}_{n}
converge in
\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
to the limit

\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}
. Since

\bm{u}_{n}\in(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge})_{0}
,
\bm{u}\in(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge})_{0}
. We see
\bm{u}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge})

and
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}_{n}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}\bm{u}=\bm{f}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\bm{f}_{\blacklozenge 1}+\bm{f}_{\blacklozenge 0}+\bm{f}
.
\square

If the condition could be weakend to that the total
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}_{n}

converge, then it would be told that
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}
is a closed operator,
which is the case when
\omega_{b}=0
.

8.3
Splitting
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
with respect to tha azimuthal numbers

Since we do not know whether
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}
is a self-adjoint operator in
\mathfrak{H}
or not, we try to consider its action on vector fields
\bm{u}
of the form
\bm{u}(\bm{x})=\check{\bm{u}}(r,\vartheta)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}
, where
m
is a fixed
integer.

Precisely speaking, we put

\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}:=\{(r,\vartheta)|(r\sin\vartheta,0,r\cos\vartheta)\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}\}\subset[0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}.

(8.48)

Since
\mathfrak{R}_{b}
is axially symmetric, it holds that

\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}=\{(r,\vartheta)|(r\sin\vartheta\cos\phi,r\sin\vartheta\sin\phi,r\cos\vartheta)\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}\quad\forall\phi\}.

While
\rho_{b}(\bm{x})=\rho_{b}^{\flat}(r,\vartheta)
,
\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}=\{(r,\vartheta)|\rho_{b}^{\flat}(r,\vartheta)>0\}
.

Let
m
be an integer.

Definition 10

We denote by
\mathfrak{H}_{|m}=\mathfrak{H}_{|m}(\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{C}^{d})
,
d=1,3
, the set of all functions
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}_{b},\rho_{b}d\bm{x};\mathbb{C}^{d})
such that there exists a function
\check{\bm{u}}
defined on
\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}
for which it holds that

\bm{u}(\bm{x})=\check{\bm{u}}(r,\vartheta)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}
.

We denote

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{|m}:=C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})\cap\mathfrak{H}_{|m},

(8.49)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m}:=\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge}\cap\mathfrak{H}_{|m}

(8.50)

Clearly,
u(\bm{x})=\check{u}(r,\vartheta)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi}
belongs to
\mathfrak{D}_{|m}
if and only if there exists
\check{u}^{\sharp}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}^{\sharp})
such that

\check{u}(r,\vartheta)=\check{u}^{\sharp}(r\sin\vartheta,0,r\cos\vartheta)
, where
\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}^{\sharp}:=\{(r\sin\vartheta,0,r\cos\vartheta)|(r,\vartheta\in\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}\}
.

The following calculus can be easily verified:

1) If
q\in\mathfrak{H}
is axially symmetric, that is,
\in\mathfrak{H}_{|0}
, like
\rho_{b},S_{b},P_{b}
, and if

\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m},q\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}
, then
q\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
and

{(q\bm{u})}\check{\ }=q^{\flat}\check{\bm{u}}
.

2) If
\bm{Q}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
and
\mathrm{div}\bm{Q}\in\mathfrak{H}
, then
\mathrm{div}\bm{Q}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
. In fact

(\mathrm{div}\bm{Q})\check{\ }(r,\vartheta)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r^{2}\check{Q}^{r})+\frac{1}{r\sin\vartheta}\frac{\partial}{\partial\vartheta}(\sin\vartheta\check{Q}^{\vartheta})+\frac{\mathrm{i}m}{r\sin\vartheta}\check{Q}^{\phi}

for
\check{\bm{Q}}=\check{Q}^{r}\bm{e}_{r}+\check{Q}^{\vartheta}\bm{e}_{\vartheta}+\check{Q}^{\phi}\bm{e}_{\phi}
.

3) If
Q\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
and
\nabla Q\in\mathfrak{H}
, then
\nabla Q\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
. In fact

(\nabla Q)\check{\ }=\frac{\partial\check{Q}}{\partial r}\bm{e}_{r}+\frac{1}{r}\frac{\partial\check{Q}}{\partial\vartheta}\bm{e}_{\vartheta}+\frac{\mathrm{i}m}{r\sin\vartheta}\check{Q}\bm{e}_{\phi}.

4) If
\bm{Q}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
, then
(\bm{Q}|\nabla S_{b})\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
. In fact

(\bm{Q}|\nabla S_{b})\check{\ }=\check{Q}^{r}\frac{\partial S_{b}}{\partial r}+\check{Q}^{\vartheta}\frac{\partial S_{b}}{\partial\vartheta}.

5) If
q\in C^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{R}_{b}\cap\partial\mathfrak{R}_{b})\cap\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
, then

\mathcal{K}[q]\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
. In fact

\mathcal{K}[q]\check{\ }(r,\vartheta)=\int\int_{\check{\mathfrak{R}}_{b}}\check{q}(r^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime})K_{|m}(r,\vartheta,r^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime})(r^{\prime})^{2}\sin\vartheta^{\prime}dr^{\prime}d\vartheta^{\prime},

where

K_{|m}(r,\vartheta,r^{\prime},\vartheta^{\prime}):=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{\pi}\frac{\cos(m\phi)d\phi}{\sqrt{r^{2}+(r^{\prime})^{2}-2rr^{\prime}(\sin\vartheta\sin\vartheta^{\prime}\cos\phi+\cos\vartheta\cos\vartheta^{\prime})}}.

and so on.

Thanks to these calculus, we can claim that the operators
\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge|m},\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge|m}

can be considered as operators from dense subspace of
\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
into
\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
, where

Definition 11

We put

\displaystyle\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge|m}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\restriction\mathfrak{H}_{|m},

(8.51)

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge|m}

\displaystyle:=\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\restriction\mathfrak{H}_{|m}

(8.52)

Let us consider
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m}
. Then, since

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial\phi}=\mathrm{i}m\bm{u}
, we see

0\leq Q_{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]\leq Q^{\blacklozenge}_{0}[\bm{u}]+a(|m|)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

(8.53)

where

a(|m|):=a+K_{0}+K_{1}\|\omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}|m|^{2}.

(8.54)

Thus
Q_{\blacklozenge}\restriction\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m}
is equivalent to the norm of

\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m}=\Big\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}\Big|\ \mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})\in L^{2}\Big(\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}d\bm{x}\Big)\Big\}.

Of course, for
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{D}_{|m}
, we have

Q^{\blacklozenge}_{0}[u]=(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}|\bm{u})_{\mathfrak{H}}\geq-a(|m|)\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2},

(8.55)

that is,
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\restriction\mathfrak{D}_{|m}
is bounded from below, and symmetric. Therefore

\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge|m}:=\bm{L}\restriction\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
is self-adjoint, while

\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge|m})=\{\bm{u}\in(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m})_{0}\ |\ \mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}\}.

Here
(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m})_{0}
stands for the closure of
\mathfrak{D}_{|m}=C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})\cap\mathfrak{H}_{|m}

in
\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m}
. Note that it holds

(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m})_{0}=(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge})_{0}\cap\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
, and, for
\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m}
, we have

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}\bm{u}=-m^{2}\omega_{b}^{2}\bm{u},\quad\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}\bm{u}=-2m\mathrm{i}\Omega\omega_{b}\begin{bmatrix}-u^{2}\\
\\
u^{1}\\
\\
0\end{bmatrix},

which belong to
\mathfrak{H}
automatically.

On the other hand,

\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge|m}=\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\restriction\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
is an operator in
\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
. Actually, for
\bm{v}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
, it turns out to be that

\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge|m}\bm{v}=2\bm{\Omega}\times\bm{v}+2\mathrm{i}m\omega_{b}\bm{v}.

therefore
\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge|m}
is a bounded skew-symmetric linear operator
in
\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
with

\mathfrak{Re}[(\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge|m}\bm{v}|\bm{v})_{\mathfrak{H}}]=0\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{v}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}.

(8.56)

Consequently we can apply the Hille-Yosida theory to the initial value problem (
8.22
)(
8.23
) to claim:

Proposition 16

If
\bm{\xi}^{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge|m}),\bm{v}^{0}\in(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m})_{0},\bm{f}\in C([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H}_{|m})
, then the problem
(
8.22
)(
8.23
)
admits a unique solution

\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})

in

C^{2}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H}_{|m})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[;(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m})_{0})\cap C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge|m})).

Moreover the energy

E(t,\bm{u})=\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}\blacklozenge}^{2}+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}=\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+Q_{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

of the solution enjoys the inequality

\sqrt{E(t,\bm{\xi})}\leq e^{\kappa(|m|)t}\sqrt{E(0,\bm{\xi})}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\kappa(|m|)(t-s)}\|\bm{f}(s)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}ds,

(8.57)

where

\kappa(|m|):=1+a(|m|).

(8.58)

Note that

E(0,\bm{\xi})=(1+a(|m|))\|\bm{\xi}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}^{0}|\bm{\xi}^{0})_{\mathfrak{H}}+\|\bm{v}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}.

Now, given a positive integer
m^{\vee}=1,2,\cdots
, we consider the Hilbert space

\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}}
defined as

Definition 12

The Hilbert space

\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}}
is the set of all linear combinations

\bm{u}=\sum_{|m|\leq m^{\vee}}\bm{u}_{m}=\sum_{|m|\leq m^{\vee}}\check{\bm{u}}_{m}(r,\vartheta)e^{\mathrm{i}m\phi},

where
\bm{u}_{m}\in\mathfrak{H}_{|m}
.

Put

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}}

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}}\ \Big|\ \mathrm{div}(\rho_{b}\bm{u})\in L^{2}\Big(\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\Big)_{b}d\bm{x}\Big)\Big\},

(8.59)

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})_{0}

\displaystyle:=\mbox{the closure of }\quad C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b})\cap\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}}\quad\mbox{in}\quad\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}}.

(8.60)

Denote

\displaystyle\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\restriction\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}},

(8.61)

\displaystyle\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}}

\displaystyle:=\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}\restriction\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}}

(8.62)

Then
\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}}
turns out to be a self-adjoint operator in
\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}}
with

\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})=\{\bm{u}\in(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})_{0}\ |\ \mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{u}\in\mathfrak{H}\}

and
\bm{B}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}}
turns out to be a bounded linear operator in
\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}}
.

We can claim the following

Theorem 10

Let
m^{\vee}
be a positive integer.
If
\bm{\xi}^{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}}),\bm{v}^{0}\in(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})_{0},\bm{f}\in C([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}})
, then the problem
(
8.22
)(
8.23
)
admits a unique solution

\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})

in

C^{2}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H}_{||m^{\vee}})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[;(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})_{0})\cap C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})).

Moreover the energy

E(t,\bm{u})=\|\bm{u}\|_{\breve{\mathfrak{G}}\blacklozenge}^{2}+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}=\|\bm{u}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}+Q_{\blacklozenge}[\bm{u}]+\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{u}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}

of the solution enjoys the inequality

\sqrt{E(t,\bm{\xi})}\leq e^{\kappa(m^{\vee})t}\sqrt{E(0,\bm{\xi})}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{\kappa(m^{\vee})(t-s)}\|\bm{f}(s)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}ds.

(8.63)

Proof. First we note that, if
m\not=m^{\prime}
,
\bm{u}\in C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m})
,
\bm{u}^{\prime}\in C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge|m^{\prime}})
, then
(\bm{u}|\bm{u}^{\prime})_{\mathfrak{H}}=0,Q_{\blacklozenge}(\bm{u},\bm{u}^{\prime})=0
,

(\partial_{t}\bm{u}|\partial_{t}\bm{u}^{\prime})_{\mathfrak{H}}=0
, therefore

E(t,\bm{u}+\bm{u}^{\prime})=E(t,\bm{u})+E(t,\bm{u}^{\prime})
. Therefore the assertion is a direct consequence of Proposition
16
, since

\kappa(|m|)\leq\kappa(m^{\vee})
for
|m|\leq m^{\vee}
.

\square

When
\|\omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}\not=0
, then we see

\kappa(m^{\vee})t\geq 2\|\omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}(m^{\vee})^{2}t\rightarrow\infty
as
m^{\vee}\rightarrow\infty
for
\forall t>0
Therefore we can not deduce from this theorem the existence of solutions to the problem (
8.22
)(
8.23
)
for non-trivial data
(\bm{\xi}^{0},\bm{v}^{0})\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge})\times(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge})_{0}
by considering the sequence
of the solutions
\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}_{m^{\vee}}(t,\bm{x})
to the data

(\bm{\xi}_{m^{\vee}}^{0},\bm{v}_{m^{\vee}}^{0})\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})\times(\mathfrak{G}_{\blacklozenge||m^{\vee}})_{0}

which approximate the data

(\bm{\xi}^{0},\bm{v}^{0})
and passing to the limit of
m^{\vee}\rightarrow\infty
.

8.4
Axially symmetric perturbations and Rayleigh’s instability

Let us consider
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)

in the cylindrical co-ordinates
(\varpi,z,\phi)
:

x^{1}=\varpi\cos\phi.\quad x^{2}=\varpi\sin\phi,\quad x^{3}=z.

by following
[
18
]
.

For a vector field
\displaystyle\bm{Q}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}Q^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
we denote

\bm{Q}^{<}=\begin{bmatrix}Q^{\varpi}\\
\\
Q^{z}\\
\\
Q^{\phi}\end{bmatrix},

where

\displaystyle\bm{Q}=\sum Q^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}

\displaystyle=Q^{\varpi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}+Q^{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+\frac{Q^{\phi}}{\varpi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=Q^{\varpi}\bm{e}_{\varpi}+Q^{z}\bm{e}_{z}+Q^{\phi}\bm{e}_{\phi},

that is,

\displaystyle Q^{\varpi}

\displaystyle=Q^{1}\cos\phi+Q^{2}\sin\phi

\displaystyle Q^{z}

\displaystyle=Q^{3}

\displaystyle Q^{\phi}

\displaystyle=-Q^{1}\cos\phi+Q^{2}\cos\phi.

Then
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
reads

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}^{<}}{\partial t^{2}}+\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}\Big)^{<}+(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi})^{<}=0,

(8.64)

where

\displaystyle(\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\dot{\bm{\xi}})^{<}=2\omega_{b}\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{\phi}\dot{\xi}^{\phi}\\
\\
\partial_{\phi}\dot{\xi}^{z}\\
\\
\partial_{\phi}\dot{\xi}^{\varpi}\end{bmatrix}+2\Omega_{b}\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{\xi}^{\phi}\\
\\
0\\
\\
\dot{\xi}^{\varpi}\end{bmatrix},

(8.65a)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge})^{<}=((\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0})\bm{\xi})^{<}+(\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi})^{<},

(8.65b)

\displaystyle((\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 2}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 1}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge 0})\bm{\xi})^{<}=\omega_{b}^{2}\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{\phi}^{2}\xi^{\varpi}\\
\\
\partial_{\phi}^{2}\xi^{z}\\
\\
\partial_{\phi}^{2}\xi^{\phi}\end{bmatrix}-2\Omega\omega_{b}\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{\phi}\xi^{\phi}\\
\\
0\\
\\
\partial_{\phi}\xi^{\varpi}\end{bmatrix}+

\displaystyle+2\Omega_{b}\begin{bmatrix}\varpi\Big(\frac{\partial\Omega_{b}}{\partial\varpi}\xi^{\varpi}+\frac{\partial\Omega_{b}}{\partial z}\xi^{z}\Big)\\
\\
0\\
\\
0\end{bmatrix},

(8.65c)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi})^{<}=\frac{1}{\rho_{b}}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}\lceil\delta P\rfloor\\
\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\lceil\delta P\rfloor\\
\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\lceil\delta P\rfloor\end{bmatrix}-\frac{\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor}{\rho_{b}^{2}}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial P_{b}}{\partial\varpi}\\
\\
\frac{\partial P_{b}}{\partial z}\\
\\
0\end{bmatrix}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor]\\
\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor]\\
\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor]\end{bmatrix},

(8.65d)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor=-\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}(\varpi\rho_{b}\xi^{\varpi})-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}(\rho_{b}\xi^{z})-\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}(\rho_{b}\xi^{\phi}),

(8.65e)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta P\rfloor=\Big(\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\Big)_{b}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+(\Gamma P)_{b}(\xi^{\varpi}\mathfrak{a}_{b}^{\varpi}+\xi^{z}\mathfrak{a}_{b}^{z}),

(8.65f)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{b}^{\varpi}=\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial\varpi}-\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial\varpi}\Big)_{b},\quad\mathfrak{a}_{b}^{z}=\Big(\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial z}-\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial z}\Big)_{b}.

(8.65g)

Here we denote

\Omega_{b}:=\Omega+\omega_{b}.

(8.66)

Now let us consider a solution
\bm{\xi}(t,\bm{x})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\xi^{\varpi}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\xi^{z}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\xi^{\phi}=0
, namely, an axisymmetric perturbation
\bm{\xi}=\bm{\xi}(t,\varpi,z)
.

Then it holds that

\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\delta\rho=0,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\delta P=0,\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\mathcal{K}[\delta\rho]=0,

therefore
(\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi})^{\phi}=0
. Moreover

(\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi})^{<}
involve only
\xi^{\varpi},\xi^{z}
, and involve neither

\xi^{\phi}
nor its derivatives, while

\delta\rho=-\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}(\varpi\rho_{b}\xi^{\varpi})-\frac{\partial}{\partial z}(\rho_{b}\xi^{z}).

Therefore the
\phi
-component of (
8.64
) reads

\frac{\partial^{2}\xi^{\phi}}{\partial t^{2}}+2\Omega_{b}\frac{\partial\xi^{\varpi}}{\partial t}=0.

(8.67)

Integrating this equation, we have

\frac{\partial\xi^{\phi}}{\partial t}+2\Omega_{b}\xi^{\varpi}=C(\varpi,z)\quad\forall t\geq 0.

Assuming that the initial data

\displaystyle{\bm{\xi}}^{0}=\bm{\xi}|_{t=0},{\bm{v}}^{0}=\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}

satisfy

{v}^{\phi 0}+2\Omega_{b}{\xi}^{\varpi 0}=0,

(8.68)

we have the identity

\frac{\partial\xi^{\phi}}{\partial t}+2\Omega_{b}\xi^{\varpi}=0\quad\forall t\geq 0.

(8.69)

This identity means that the angular momentum per unit mass with respect to
z
-axis,

J=\varpi(\varpi\Omega+v^{\phi})

of the perturbed motion coincides with the angular momentum per unit mass

J_{b}=\varpi(\varpi\Omega+v_{b}^{\phi})=\varpi^{2}\Omega_{b}

of the background, that is,
\Delta J=0
. In fact, noting that

\bm{v}_{b}^{<}=\omega_{b}\begin{bmatrix}0\\
0\\
1\end{bmatrix},\quad\Delta\varpi=\xi^{\varpi},\quad\Delta\bm{v}=\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\bm{\xi},

we see

\displaystyle\Delta J

\displaystyle=(\Delta\varpi)(\varpi\Omega+\bm{v}_{b}^{\phi})+\varpi\Delta(\varpi+v^{\phi})

\displaystyle=2\varpi\Omega\xi^{\varpi}+\varpi\omega_{b}\xi^{\varpi}+\varpi\frac{D}{Dt}\Big|_{\bm{v}_{b}}\xi^{\phi}

\displaystyle=2\varpi\Omega\xi^{\varpi}+\varpi\omega_{b}\xi^{\varpi}+\varpi\Big(\frac{\partial\xi^{\phi}}{\partial t}+\omega_{b}\xi^{\varpi}\Big)

\displaystyle=\varpi\Big(\frac{\partial\xi^{\phi}}{\partial t}+2(\Omega+\omega_{b})\xi^{\varpi}\Big)

\displaystyle=0.

The
\varpi
-component of (
8.64
) turns out to be

\frac{\partial^{2}\xi^{\varpi}}{\partial t^{2}}+c_{\varpi}\xi^{\varpi}+c_{z}\xi^{z}+(\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi})^{\varpi}=0,

(8.70)

where

c_{\varpi}:=\frac{1}{\varpi^{3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}(\varpi^{4}\Omega_{b}^{2}),\quad c_{z}:=\frac{1}{\varpi^{3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}(\varpi^{4}\Omega_{b}^{2}).

(8.71)

If
\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\omega_{b}=0
, as in barotropic background, then

c_{\varpi}=\mathscr{C},c_{z}=0
, where
\mathscr{C}
defined by

\mathscr{C}:=\frac{1}{\varpi^{3}}\frac{d}{d\varpi}(\varpi^{4}\Omega_{b}^{2})

(8.72)

is so called ‘
Rayleigh stability discriminant
’.

The
z
-component of (
8.69
) reads

\frac{\partial^{2}\xi^{z}}{\partial t^{2}}+(\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi})^{z}=0,

(8.73)

Since
(\mathcal{L}\bm{\xi})^{<}
does not involve
\xi^{\phi}
, the equations
(
8.70
)(
8.73
) form a system for unknowns
\xi^{\varpi},\xi^{z}
.
when a solution
(\xi^{\varpi},\xi^{z})
of this system is given, then, integrating (
8.69
), we get
\xi^{\phi}
as

\xi^{\phi}(t)={\xi}^{\phi 0}-2\Omega_{b}\int_{0}^{t}\xi^{\varpi}(t^{\prime})dt^{\prime}.

Let us consider the existence theorem for the system of equations (
8.70
)(
8.73
) for the unkown
(\xi^{\varpi}(t,\varpi,z),\xi^{z}(t,\varpi,z))
. We assume that the background is barotropic.

We use the following notations:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{<}

\displaystyle=\{(\varpi,z)\in[0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}\ |\ (\varpi,0,z)\in\mathfrak{R}_{b}\},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{d}

\displaystyle=L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}^{<},2\pi\varpi d\varpi dz;\mathbb{C}^{d}),\quad d=1,2,3,

\displaystyle(\bm{q}^{\eqslantless})^{>}(\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}q^{\varpi}(\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}},x^{3})\\
\\
0\\
\\
q^{z}(\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+(x^{2})^{2}},x^{3})\end{bmatrix}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\bm{q}^{\eqslantless}=\begin{bmatrix}q^{\varpi}(\varpi,z)\\
\\
0\\
\\
q^{z}(\varpi,z)\end{bmatrix}\in\mathfrak{h}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\eqslantless}

\displaystyle=\{\bm{q}^{\eqslantless}\in\mathfrak{h}^{2}\ |\ (\bm{q}^{\eqslantless})^{>}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathfrak{R}_{b};\mathbb{C}^{3})\},

The system (
8.70
)(
8.73
) reads

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}}{\partial t^{2}}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}=0,

(8.74)

where

\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}=\Big((\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}).(\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})^{>}\Big)^{\eqslantless}=\begin{bmatrix}\Big((\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}).(\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})^{>}\Big)^{\varpi}\\
\\
\Big((\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}).(\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})^{>}\Big)^{z}\end{bmatrix},

(8.75)

\displaystyle\Big((\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}).(\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})^{>}\Big)^{\varpi}=

(8.76a)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{C}\xi^{\varpi}+\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}\lceil\delta P\rfloor-\frac{\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor}{\rho^{2}}\frac{\partial P}{\partial\varpi}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor],

(8.76b)

\displaystyle\Big((\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}).(\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})^{>}\Big)^{z}=

(8.76c)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\lceil\delta P\rfloor-\frac{\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor}{\rho^{2}}\frac{\partial P}{\partial z}-4\pi\mathsf{G}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor].

(8.76d)

Here

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{div}(\rho(\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})^{>})=

\displaystyle=-\Big[\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}(\varpi\rho{\xi}^{\varpi}+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}(\rho\xi^{z})\Big],

(8.77a)

\displaystyle\lceil\delta P\rfloor

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho}\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor+\Gamma P(\xi^{\varpi}\mathfrak{a}^{\varpi}+\xi^{z}\mathfrak{a}^{z}),

(8.77b)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}^{\varpi}=\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial\varpi}-\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial\varpi},\quad\mathfrak{a}^{z}=\frac{1}{\rho}\frac{\partial\rho}{\partial z}-\frac{1}{\Gamma P}\frac{\partial P}{\partial z}.

(8.77c)

and

\rho,P,\Gamma
stand for

\rho_{b},P_{b},\Gamma_{b}
.

Then, for
\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}=(\xi^{\varpi},\xi^{z})^{\top}\in\mathfrak{D}^{\eqslantless}
, it holds that

(\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}|\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}=Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}],

where

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}]

\displaystyle:=2\pi\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\mathscr{C}|\xi^{\varpi}|^{2}\rho\varpi d\varpi dz+2\pi\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor|^{2}\varpi d\varpi dz+

\displaystyle+4\pi\mathfrak{Re}\Big[\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}(\bm{\xi}|\bm{n})\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor^{*}\varpi d\varpi dz\Big]-2\pi\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\frac{\Gamma P\mathscr{A}}{\rho}\|\nabla\rho\|^{2}|(\bm{\xi}|\bm{n})|^{2}\varpi d\varpi dz

\displaystyle-8\pi\mathsf{G}\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\mathcal{K}[\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor]\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor^{*}\varpi d\varpi dz,

with

\displaystyle\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor

\displaystyle=-\Big[\frac{1}{\varpi}\frac{\partial}{\partial\varpi}(\varpi\rho{\xi}^{\varpi})+\frac{\partial}{\partial z}(\rho\xi^{z})\Big],

\displaystyle(\bm{\xi}|\bm{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi^{\varpi}\partial_{\varpi}\rho+\xi^{z}\partial_{z}\rho}{\sqrt{(\partial_{\varpi}\rho)^{2}+(\partial_{z}\rho)^{2}}}.

Recall that
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{A}_{b}
is such that

\mathfrak{a}_{b}=-\mathscr{A}\bm{n}=-\mathscr{A}\frac{\nabla\rho_{b}}{\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|}.

Put

\mathfrak{g}:=\Big\{\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}=\begin{bmatrix}\xi^{\varpi}\\
\xi^{z}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathfrak{h}^{2}\ \Big|\ \lceil\delta\rho\rfloor\in L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}^{<},\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}2\pi\varpi d\varpi dz;\mathbb{C})\Big\}

(8.78)

and denote the closure of
\mathfrak{D}^{\eqslantless}
in
\mathfrak{g}
by
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
.

We can consider the quadratic form
Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}
on
\mathfrak{g}
, and it is bounded from below, since

\inf\mathscr{C}(\varpi)>-\infty
,
and by Proposition
1
, Proposition
2
. Thus we see that
\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}\restriction\mathfrak{D}^{\eqslantless}
has the Friedrichs extension

\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}
which is a self-adjoint operator in
\mathfrak{h}^{2}
with domain

\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless})=\{\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}\ |\ \mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}\in\mathfrak{h}^{2}\}.

It holds that

(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}|\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless})_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}=Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}]

for
\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless})
.

Consequently we can claim

Proposition 17

If
\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless 0}\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}),\bm{v}^{\eqslantless 0}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, then the initial value problem

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}}{\partial t^{2}}+\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}=0,\quad\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}|_{t=0}=\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless 0},\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}=\bm{v}^{\eqslantless 0}

(8.79)

admits a unique solution in

C^{2}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{h}^{2})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[,\mathfrak{g}_{0})\cap C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless})).

Let
\bm{\xi}^{<0}=(\xi^{\varpi 0},\xi^{z0},\xi^{\phi 0})\in\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless})\times\mathfrak{h}^{1},\bm{v}^{<0}=(v^{\varpi 0},v^{z0},v^{\phi 0})\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}\times\mathfrak{h}^{1}
satisfy

v^{\phi 0}+2\Omega_{b}(\varpi)\xi^{\varpi 0}=0.

Let
\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}=(\xi^{\varpi}(t,\varpi,z),\xi^{z}(t,\varpi,z))
be the solution of Proposition
17
. Put

\xi^{\phi}(t,\varpi,z)=\xi^{\phi 0}(\varpi,z)-2\Omega_{b}(\varpi)\int_{0}^{t}\xi^{\varpi}(t^{\prime},\varpi,z)dt^{\prime}.

Then
\bm{\xi}^{<}=(\xi^{\varpi},\xi^{z},\xi^{\phi})
turns out to be the unique solution
of the initial value problem

\frac{\partial^{2}\bm{\xi}^{<}}{\partial t^{2}}+\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\blacklozenge}\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}+\mathcal{L}_{\blacklozenge}\bm{\xi}\Big)^{<}=0,\quad\bm{\xi}^{<}|_{t=0}=\bm{\xi}^{<0},\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}^{<}}{\partial t}\Big|_{t=0}=\bm{v}^{<0}

(8.80)

in

C^{2}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{h}^{3})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{g}_{0}\times\mathfrak{h}^{1})\cap C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless})\times\mathfrak{h}^{1}).

Thus
\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\xi}^{<})^{>}
, namely,

\bm{\xi}=\begin{bmatrix}\xi^{1}(t,\bm{x})\\
\\
\xi^{2}(t,\bm{x})\\
\\
\xi^{3}(t,\bm{x})\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\xi^{\varpi}(t,\varpi,z)\cos\phi-\xi^{\phi}(t,\varpi,z)\sin\phi\\
\\
\xi^{\varpi}(t,\varpi,z)\sin\phi+\xi^{\phi}(t,\varpi,z)\cos\phi\\
\\
\xi^{z}(t,\varpi,z)\end{bmatrix},

is the solution of the equation LASO(
\blacklozenge
)
in

C^{2}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{H})\cap C^{1}([0,+\infty[;\mathfrak{G}_{0})\cap C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{D}(\bm{L}_{\blacklozenge}).

Taking the inner product of the equation of (
8.79
) with
\partial\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}/\partial t
, and integrating it, we can show

Proposition 18

The solution
\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}=(\xi^{\varpi},\xi^{z})
of (
8.79
) given by Proposition
17
enjoys the energy equality

\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}^{2}+Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}(t)]=\|\bm{v}^{\eqslantless 0}\|_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}^{2}+Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless 0}].

(8.81)

On the other hand, if (i) we suppose

-\varepsilon\frac{\|\nabla\rho_{b}\|^{2}}{\rho_{b}}\leq\mathscr{A}_{b}\leq 0\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{R}_{b},\quad 0<\exists\varepsilon<1,

and if (ii) we adopt the Cowling approximation, that is, we relpace
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{0}+4\pi\mathsf{G}\mathcal{L}_{1}

by
\mathcal{L}_{0}
, then we have

\displaystyle Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}]\geq

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\mathscr{C}|\xi^{\varpi}|^{2}\varpi d\varpi dz

\displaystyle+2\pi(1-\varepsilon)\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor|^{2}\varpi d\varpi dz\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}\in\mathfrak{g}.

( Proposition
13
.)
Moreover if (iii) we suppose ‘Rayleigh stability condition’

\mathscr{C}\geq 0\quad\mbox{on}\quad\mathfrak{R}_{b},

( As for the meaning of the Rayleigh criterion, see
Appendix D
.),
then we have

Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}]\geq 2\pi(1-\varepsilon)\int_{\mathfrak{R}^{<}}\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}|\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor|^{2}\varpi d\varpi dz\geq 0\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}\in\mathfrak{g}.

In this case, Proposition
18
deduces the stability in the sense that

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}\leq C_{1},\quad\|\lceil\delta\rho\rfloor\|_{L^{2}(\mathfrak{R}^{<},\frac{\Gamma P}{\rho^{2}}\cdot 2\pi\varpi d\varpi dz)}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon}},

(8.82a)

\displaystyle\|\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless}(t)\|_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}\leq C_{0}+C_{1}t.

(8.82b)

Here
C_{0}=\|\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless 0}\|_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}
and

\displaystyle C_{1}=\Big(\|\bm{v}^{\eqslantless 0}\|_{\mathfrak{h}^{2}}^{2}+Q_{\blacklozenge}^{\eqslantless}[\bm{\xi}^{\eqslantless 0}]\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Consequently we can claim:

The associated solution
\bm{\xi}=(\bm{\xi}^{<})^{>}
of
ELASO(
\blacklozenge
)
enjoys

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{\partial\bm{\xi}}{\partial t}\Big\|_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}+2\|\Omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}C_{0}+2\|\Omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}C_{1}t,

(8.83a)

\displaystyle\|\bm{\xi}(t)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\bm{\xi}^{0}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}+(C_{1}+2\|\Omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}C_{0})t+\|\Omega_{b}\|_{L^{\infty}}C_{1}t^{2}.

(8.83b)

Appendix A

We consider the initial value problem:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}{u}}{dt^{2}}+{B}\frac{d{u}}{dt}+{L}{u}=0,

\displaystyle{u}(0)=\overset{\circ}{{u}},\quad\frac{d{u}}{dt}(0)=\overset{\circ}{{v}}.

Here the unkown is a function
{u}:t\mapsto{u}(t):[0,+\infty[\rightarrow\mathsf{X}
,

\mathsf{X}
being a Hilbert space endowed with an inner product
(\cdot|\cdot)_{\mathsf{X}}
.

We assume:

\mathsf{Y}
is a Hilbert space endowed with an inner product

(\cdot|\cdot)_{\mathsf{Y}}
, which is continuously and densely inbedded in
\mathsf{X}
.

{L}
is a self-adjoint operator in
\mathsf{X}
whose domain
\mathsf{D}({L})

is included in
\mathsf{Y}
and dense in
\mathsf{Y}
( in the
\mathsf{Y}
-norm).
It holds that

\|u\|_{\mathsf{Y}}\geq\|u\|_{\mathsf{X}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall u\in\mathsf{Y},

(({L}+a+1){u}_{1}|{u}_{2})_{\mathsf{X}}=({u}_{1}|{u}_{2})_{\mathsf{Y}}\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall{u}_{1}\in\mathsf{D}({L}),\forall{u}_{2}\in\mathsf{Y},

where
a
is a non-negative number.

{B}
is a bounded linear operator in
\mathsf{X}

with

\beta:=\sup\frac{|\mathfrak{Re}[({B}{v}|{v})_{\mathsf{X}}]|}{\|{v}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}}.

Remark
:
If
B
is skew-symmetric, then we have
\beta=0
. This is the case when
B=\bm{B}=2\bm{\Omega}\times
on
\mathsf{X}=\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathfrak{R},\rho d\bm{x})
.

Proposition A1

For any
c\in\mathbb{R}
and
\lambda>a+|c|\beta
the operator

{L}+c{B}+\lambda
has the bounded linear inverse operator
({L}+c{B}+\lambda)^{-1}
defined on the whole space
\mathsf{X}

such that

|\|({L}+c{B}+\lambda)^{-1}\||_{\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{X})}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda-a-|c|\beta}.

Proof. First we see
{L}+c{B}+\lambda
is invertible. In fact, if

({L}+c{B}+\lambda){u}={f},\quad{u}\in\mathsf{D}({L}),\quad{f}\in\mathsf{X},

then we see

\displaystyle(\lambda-a-|c|\beta)\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\|u\|_{\mathsf{Y}}^{2}+(\lambda-a-1)\|u\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}+c\mathfrak{Re}[({B}{u}|{u})_{\mathsf{X}}]=

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Re}[({u}|{f})_{\mathsf{X}}]\leq\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{X}}\|{f}\|_{\mathsf{X}},

therefore we have

\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{X}}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda-a-|c|\beta}\|{f}\|_{\mathsf{X}}.

Hence the range
\mathsf{R}({L}+c{B}+\lambda)
is closed, since
{L}+c{B}+\lambda
is a closed operator.
We claim that
\mathsf{R}({L}+c{B}+\lambda)=\mathsf{X}
. In fact, suppose

(({L}+c{B}+\lambda){u}|{h})_{\mathsf{X}}=0\quad\forall{u}\in\mathsf{D}({L}).

Then

\displaystyle({L}{u}|{h})

\displaystyle=-((c{B}+\lambda){u}|{h})_{\mathsf{X}}

\displaystyle=({u}|(-c{B}^{*}-\lambda){h})_{\mathsf{X}}

for
\forall{u}\in\mathsf{D}({L})
.
Hence
{h}\in\mathsf{D}({L}^{*})
and

{L}^{*}{h}=-c{B}^{*}-\lambda{h}.

Since
{L}={L}^{*}
, this means that
{h}\in\mathsf{D}({L})
and

({L}+c{B}^{*}+\lambda){h}=0.

Since
{L}+c{B}^{*}+\lambda
is invertible, for

({B}^{*}v|v)_{\mathsf{X}}=(Bv|v)_{\mathsf{X}}^{*}
, it follows that
{h}=0
.
Therefore

\mathsf{R}({L}+c{B}+\lambda)=\mathsf{X}
.
Summing up, we have
(L+cB+\lambda)^{-1}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{X})
.

\square

We are going to apply the Hille-Yosida theory to the initial value problem

(IVP)
:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}{u}}{dt^{2}}+{B}\frac{d{u}}{dt}+{L}{u}={f}(t),

\displaystyle{u}(t)\in\mathsf{D}({L})\quad\mbox{for}\quad\forall t\geq 0,

\displaystyle{u}=\overset{\circ}{{u}},\quad\frac{d{u}}{dt}=\overset{\circ}{{v}}\quad\mbox{at}\quad t=0,

We put

\displaystyle U=\begin{bmatrix}{u}\\
\\
\dot{{u}}\end{bmatrix},\quad\dot{{u}}=\frac{d{u}}{dt},

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}U=\begin{bmatrix}O&-I\\
\\
{L}&{B}\end{bmatrix}U=\begin{bmatrix}-\dot{{u}}\\
\\
{B}\dot{{u}}+{L}{u}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\mathsf{Z}=\mathsf{Y}\times\mathsf{X}

with

\displaystyle(U_{1}|U_{2})_{\mathsf{Z}}=({u}_{1}|{u}_{2})_{\mathsf{Y}}+(\dot{{u}}_{1}|\dot{{u}}_{2})_{\mathsf{X}},

\displaystyle\mathsf{D}(\mathbf{A})=\mathsf{D}({L})\times\mathsf{Y}.

Then the initial value problem
(IVP)
can be written as the problem
(IVP‡)
:

\frac{dU}{dt}+\mathbf{A}U=F(t),\quad U|_{t=0}=U_{0},

where

U_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}\overset{\circ}{{u}}\\
\\
\overset{\circ}{{v}}\end{bmatrix}.\quad F(t)=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
\\
{f}(t)\end{bmatrix}.

Applying
[
4
, Theorem 7.4]
, we can claim

Proposition A2

If
U_{0}\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbf{A})

and
F\in C([0,+\infty[;\mathsf{Z})
, then there exists a unique solution

U\in C^{1}([0,+\infty[,\mathsf{Z})\cap C([0,+\infty[,\mathsf{D}(\mathbf{A}))

to the problem
(IVP‡)
. Moreover
E(t)=\|U(t)\|_{\mathsf{Z}}^{2}
enjoys

\sqrt{E(t)}\leq e^{\Lambda t}\Big(\sqrt{E(0)}+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\Lambda s}\|F(s)\|_{\mathsf{Z}}ds\Big),

where
\Lambda=1+a+\beta
.

Here we consider that
\mathsf{D}(\mathbf{A})
is equipped with the operator norm

(\|U\|_{\mathsf{Z}}^{2}+\|\mathbf{A}U\|_{\mathsf{Z}}^{2})^{1/2}
.

Proof of Proposition A2. Firstly
\mathbf{A}+1+a+\beta
is monotone, that is, for
\forall U\in\mathsf{D}(\mathbf{A})
we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Re}[(\mathbf{A}U|U)_{\mathsf{Z}}]+(1+a+\beta)\|U\|_{\mathsf{Z}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{Re}\Big[-(\dot{{u}}|{u})_{\mathsf{Y}}+({L}{u}|\dot{{u}})_{\mathsf{X}}+(\mbox{\boldmath$B$}\dot{{u}}|\dot{{u}})_{\mathsf{X}}\Big]+

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1+a+\beta)(\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{Y}}^{2}+\|\dot{{u}}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2})

\displaystyle\geq-(1+a)\mathfrak{Re}[(\dot{{u}}|{u})_{\mathsf{X}}]-\beta\|\dot{{u}}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}+

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1+a+\beta)\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}+(1+a+\beta)\|\dot{{u}}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(1+a)\Big[\|\dot{{u}}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}-\mathfrak{Re}[(\dot{{u}}|{u})_{\mathsf{X}}]+\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\geq 0,

since
(({L}+a+1){u}|\dot{{u}})_{\mathsf{X}}=({u}|\dot{{u}})_{\mathsf{Y}}=(\dot{{u}}|{u})_{\mathsf{Y}}^{*}
and

|\mathfrak{Re}[({B}\dot{{u}}|\dot{{u}})_{\mathsf{X}}]|\leq\beta\|\dot{{u}}\|_{\mathsf{X}}^{2}
.

If
\Lambda>\frac{\beta}{2}+\sqrt{\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}+a}
, then the operator
\mathbf{A}+\Lambda
has the bounded inverse defined on
\mathsf{Z}
. Actually the equation

\mathbf{A}U+\Lambda U=F=\begin{bmatrix}{f}\\
\\
\mbox{\boldmath$g$}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathsf{Z}

means

\begin{cases}-\dot{{u}}+\Lambda{u}={f}\in\mathsf{Y}\\
\\
{B}\dot{{u}}+{L}{u}+\Lambda\dot{{u}}=\mbox{\boldmath$g$}\in\mathsf{X},\end{cases}

which can be solved as

\begin{cases}{u}=({L}+\Lambda{B}+\Lambda^{2})^{-1}({B}{f}+\Lambda{f}+\mbox{\boldmath$g$})\in\mathsf{D}({L}),\\
\\
\dot{{u}}=({L}+\Lambda{B}+\Lambda^{2})^{-1}({B}{f}+\Lambda{f}+\mbox{\boldmath$g$})-{f}\in\mathsf{Y},\end{cases}
